# Validation Record — NetworkHealthCheck

## v1.2.12 — the one retransmission figure a run can attribute · 2026-09-12 — backlog #52 — the third change of the batch; not yet released

**What and why.** The retransmission rows count every TCP segment this computer sent during the window, to every destination, over every interface — the tool's own checks, a browser, a backup, a remote session — and the row has said so in both languages since 1.1: *a system-wide statistic for the entire computer*. On a large sample the figure is still worth having; on the small sample an idle machine produces in the shipped eight seconds, a reader cannot tell whether two retransmissions belonged to the network being diagnosed or to a background process talking to a server on another continent, and this record holds the sharpest instance of it — the 125-second IT windows of the 1.2.3 validation measured the traffic of the session that was driving the tool. More of the same counter does not help: a longer window over a system-wide counter is more unattributable traffic, not less. The owner raised #52 on 2026-09-09 for a *different* measurement — a connection the tool opens itself to a known target, reported as what it is — and took the five decisions of the previous session's recommendation on 2026-09-12: measure the tool's own handshakes, read them against the operating system's own initial retransmission timeout, put the figure in the existing `tcp` row, decide nothing with it, and state its cost in the time budget.

**Version.** 1.2.12 unchanged, the batch's release still ahead; `FUNCTION_COUNT` 117 → **120** (`Get-TcpInitialRto`, `New-TcpConnectSample`, `Get-TcpConnectSampleText`); `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated over the same 28 files, 17 of which changed.

**#52 — the attributable figure.** *Measured before it was written* (reference machine, Windows 11 26200, unelevated, 2026-09-12): four `TcpClient` handshakes to `1.1.1.1:443` in 23 / 7 / 7 / 7 ms and to `www.microsoft.com:443` in 24 / 9 / 6 / 7 ms — the first of a fresh process slower than the rest by some milliseconds on both, a difference a 1000 ms timeout does not notice; `IPAddress.TryParse` accepts `1.1.1.1` and `2606:4700::1111` and refuses `www.microsoft.com`, which is the rule `TcpClient.BeginConnect(string)` itself applies before deciding whether to resolve, so it is the rule the tool uses to decide whether a first connection carried a lookup; `Get-NetTCPSetting` returns the six templates with **no `InitialRtoMs` property on the object at all** on this build, while `netsh int tcp show global` reports *Initial RTO : 1000* and *Max SYN Retransmissions : 4*, the registry's `Tcpip\Parameters` key holds no `InitialRto` value, and `Get-NetTransportFilter` — which would say which template a connection gets — is refused with *Access is denied*; the cmdlet costs 468 ms in a fresh shell, where it loads the NetTCPIP module, and 41 ms then 27 ms once `Get-NetIPConfiguration` has loaded it, which the adapter discovery does before any TCP target is tried. *The criterion*: Microsoft documents `InitialRtoMs` as *the period, in milliseconds, before connect, or SYN, retransmit* (300–3000 ms in steps of 10; the `Set-NetTCPSetting` reference, read 2026-09-12), so a handshake that lasts at least that long is read as one in which that timer expired and a SYN or SYN-ACK was sent again, and one below it as a handshake in which this computer sent its SYN once — a basis with a source, not a magnitude somebody picked, recorded on the repository's threshold page with the citations. *The measurement*: a TCP target that answers is connected to `PingCount` times — the connection that decides the row, then `PingCount` − 1 more to the address the first one reached, so that a name is resolved once and every time is a handshake and nothing else, one after another, stopping at the first that fails, so that a target which stops answering costs at most one more `TcpTimeoutMs` and not `PingCount` of them (the recommendation's worst case was three timeouts; this is one). The timeout is read once per run and only after a connection has succeeded, so a run whose targets all fail pays nothing for it; the Internet template's value is used where the cmdlet reports one and 1000 ms is assumed where it does not, and the row says which. *The row*: the message gains the times and the count — *4 connection(s) timed: 28 / 9 / 7 / 8 ms; 0 of 4 reached the 1000 ms (assumed) retransmission timeout* — the message names an assumed timeout as assumed, so that the headline does not claim what the details qualify — and the details three lines: the connections, with the local address the handshakes left from and, for a name target, the address it resolved to and the sentence that its first connection is listed and not read; the timeout, its value and its provenance, with `netsh int tcp show global` as the check; and the reading rule, ending with what the figure is — this tool's own connections, to this one target, at this moment, which the system-wide rows cannot attribute — and what it is not: a verdict, since the row's status is the first connection's, as before. A failed repeat is named in the message by its position and carried in the details with its cause; a target that never answered is one connection, no timeout read, no sample. *The other side*: the system-wide rows gain one line — *The TCP Connection rows carry what this row cannot* — only where the run took a sample, so a run without an answering TCP target is not sent to a row it does not have; `Invoke-TcpConnectionTest` returns the remote and local addresses the socket used, read before it is closed; no JSON field, no configuration key, no threshold, no panel control. *On the reference machine, the first run of the new tree*: a 23-second console run with `www.microsoft.com:443` added, 34 rows, both TCP rows *Connected* with their samples — `1.1.1.1:443` 28 / 9 / 7 / 8 ms, the name target 9 / 6 / 6 / 8 ms resolved to `23.39.61.232`, the seven read against the timeout all far below it and all eight completed in 28 ms or less — while the system-wide TCPv4 row in the same run read **577 sent, 58 retransmitted, 10.052 %, *Problem Detected*** over a 12.6-second window. That is the item, measured on the day it closed: the counter convicted the computer, and the tool's own eight handshakes, to two named targets, took 28 ms at the longest.

**Tests.** Unit 762 × 2 → **785 × 2**, twenty-three assertions per language on synthetic results: four connections to an address are four times all read and none at the timeout; a connection at the timeout is counted with its time and the boundary is inclusive (1000 counted, 999 not); a name target lists four times and reads three, and a slow first connection of one is not read as a retransmission; a failed repeat is named by its position with its error and the times are the successes; the timeout and its source travel with the sample and the addresses come from the first connection; a timeout the setting reports is the one read against; the message lists the times and the count in whichever language the script speaks, with the SYN sentence only where a connection reached the timeout; the first details line names the target and the local address, the RTO line the value, its source and the `netsh` check, and a value read from the setting names the property and does not call itself assumed; a name target names the address it resolved to and a name target with one connection says nothing was read without printing a count of nothing; one reading line naming both halves of the rule; `Get-TcpInitialRto` with the cmdlet mocked — a reported value used and named as the setting, an empty value, an absent property and a cmdlet that throws all the 1000 ms default — then read for real on the machine running the tests (this one: 1000 ms, assumed); `Invoke-TcpConnectionTest` run for real against a listener on the loopback, which every machine has, reporting both addresses beside its time; and the run's loop read off the AST — repeats only after a success, to the address the first reached, stopping at the first failure, the count `PingCount` never below one, the timeout read once a connection has succeeded; and the system-wide row naming the TCP Connection rows only where a sample was taken. Report-stage 211 × 2 → **221 × 2**, a scenario driven through `Test-ConnectivityTargets` with the socket and the timeout read mocked: a target that answers is connected to four times, all to one address, one passed and weighted row with the sample in its message and the reading in its details, the sample count and the timeout read both moving once; a name asked once and its address three times with the timeout read once for two targets, the name row naming the resolved address and reading three of four; a target that does not answer costing one connection, no timeout read and no sample, and failing as before; a repeat that fails ending the repeats at three, the row still passing on its first connection, naming the failure by position and carrying its cause, the run still *Overall Healthy*; `PingCount` 1 as one connection still read. The chain's oracle holds every `tcp` row that passed to its sample — the details must carry the token `RTO` beside a value in ms, in both languages — and its self-test drops the live `tcp` row and rebuilds a passed one, so that the case is the same on a machine without the connection: 100 → **102** (the sample stripped is reported; a row that did not connect carries none and is not held to one). Eight mutants, all caught: the name target's first connection judged (3 unit + 1 report), the repeats never stopping at a failure (1 + 1), the boundary made exclusive (1), the repeats sent through the resolver again (1 + 1), the attribution line unconditional (1), the default timeout 3000 (1), the RTO line dropped from the details (2 + 2), the sample count not moved (1 report).

**The documents.** Technical guides, six copies: a 4.6 paragraph on the connection times and the timeout they are read against, 4.8's closing sentence pointing at them, limitation 3 amended. User manuals, md + html, both languages: *a few TCP connections* in section 1, a section 9 question on the times and the timeout. IT deployment manuals, md + html, both languages: section 1's *what it contacts* (`PingCount` connections to port 443, four as shipped), the `PingCount` row's second meaning, the `TcpTimeoutMs` row's cost — a target that answers costs milliseconds, one that stops answering at most one more timeout. The field manual's `tcp-retransmissions` and `tcp` rows and the quality-rows bullet, md + html. `docs/thresholds.md` gains the timeout's provenance with its three sources and check dates. Backlog #52 closed with the batch's parenthesis, #65's reference to it in the closed form, 23 open / 44 closed; the README's tool row and backlog row; tests/README's totals and the unit and result-set descriptions.

**Chain on the tree before the review (same machine), `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Package -RequireHealthy -WorkDir $env:TEMP\nhc-pr7-f`, a fresh directory: 34 / 34 in 878 s.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 113 / 113; guards OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43 (212 s); unit **785 × 2**; report-stage **221 × 2**; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 132 / 132 in 112 s; headless GUI 4 / 4, the IT panel's 24 fixed-size controls in their boxes and its three spinners at 4 / 21 / 8 — no control was added, which is decision three; the four real-window `gui` runs all *Overall Healthy*, **33 results** each, the two IT ones over the configured 30 probes and 125-second sample; five acceptance runs, all *Overall Healthy*, at 33 / 35 / 33 / 38 / 38 results; result-set **102 / 102** on the live report; the package **705 295 bytes**, SHA256 `2f709d659a801023a420f43b0ca4a2013ec072ee21f609400b92e87696949e51`, the validator 113 / 113 from inside the extracted package and its four entries opened as real windows — the same caveat as always: those figures are the step's own, and a release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit. Two earlier attempts, from `nhc-pr7-b` and `nhc-pr7-d`, were stopped by hand during their document steps so that wording corrections could go into the tree they would have tested, and are not results. **What the thirteen reports of this chain say about the change:** every TCP target that answered carries its sample, sized by the run's `PingCount` — 4 in the user runs, 6 with the IT switches, 30 in the two IT windows, 2 in the unreachable-targets runs — the handshakes 6 to 15 ms with the first of a run at 11 to 57 ms, and **not one of the 108 connections read against the timeout reached it**; the thirty of an IT window cost about a fifth of a second; the target that never answers (`192.0.2.1:9`) is the *Unable to connect* row it was, with no sample, and the dropped `8.8.8.8` row is untouched; every TCPv4 row carries the line pointing at the TCP Connection rows. In the two unreachable-targets runs the system-wide row read 2 retransmissions in 76 and in 82 sent segments — *too few retransmissions to rate* — which is consistent with the tool's own SYN to `192.0.2.1:9` being sent again at the 1-second and 3-second marks of its 4-second wait: the one retransmitting connection of the run is the one that gets no sample, by decision, so the counter's two stay unattributed there, and the row's own words say so.

## v1.2.12 — the air, by the adapter's own count · 2026-09-12 — backlog #61, the retry-counter half — the second change of the batch; not yet released

**What and why.** On a machine that has only Wi-Fi, nothing in a report separated the air from what is behind the access point: the three-way comparison #60 made possible needs a wired host, and the tool collected three signs of the air and compared none of them. The one discriminator that settles it outright is the 802.11 retry — a frame the adapter sent again because no acknowledgement came back, absorbed as delay, so that IP and TCP see no loss and the retransmission rows see nothing — and Windows exposes it through the Native Wifi API only. #61 had left the decision open until the implementation on purpose; the owner took it on 2026-09-12 in five parts, on this record's recommendation, and the backlog item's Status line carries them: read the counters through P/Invoke, because nothing else can; say what application control does to that branch; never add the PHY entries; divide retries by frames transmitted plus abandoned; and let the row decide nothing, since no threshold for a wireless retry rate has a stated basis. The other half of #61 — the BSSID sampled twice, the access-point-is-the-gateway hint, a latency variability figure with its minimum sample — stays open, and so does the item.

**Version.** 1.2.12 unchanged, the batch's release still ahead; `FUNCTION_COUNT` 113 → **117** (`Get-WifiRetrySnapshot`, `Get-Win32ErrorText`, `Get-WifiInterfaceStateText`, `Compare-WifiRetryCounters`); `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated over the same 28 files, 19 of which changed.

**#61 — the retry half.** *Measured before it was written* (reference machine, Intel Wi-Fi 6E AX211, Windows 11 26200, unelevated, 2026-09-12): `Add-Type` of the five `DllImport` lines takes 649 ms and leaves nothing in `%TEMP%`; the same definition added twice in one process is accepted, a different one under the same name is refused, which is why the reader checks for the type before compiling; and the driver writes the interface's totals into **every** one of its six PHY entries — six identical rows of counters, moving in step (+116 transmitted, +18 retried in five seconds of pings) — so the recommendation's "sum the entries" would have counted every frame six times, and the rule became *the entry with the largest transmitted-frame delta, the entries never added, the row saying how many moved and whether they agreed*. *The reader*: `WlanOpenHandle`, `WlanEnumInterfaces`, `WlanQueryInterface` with `wlan_intf_opcode_statistics`, the 532-byte interface entries and the 224 + 144·n layout the 1088-byte buffer confirmed, guarded against a buffer shorter than its own PHY count, every handle and buffer freed in `finally`, and an envelope that names why there is no reading — `addtype`, `open`, `enumerate`, `none`, `error` — the way the TCP snapshot does. *The analysis*: deltas per entry as doubles, a counter that went backwards as the TCP rows' reset row, the rate as `ullRetryCount` over `ullTransmittedFrameCount` + `ullFailedCount` with `ullMultipleRetryCount` listed as the subset it is, a window that transmitted nothing as an Information row without a rate, and every row weightless — measured, none, unavailable, reset — under the tag `wifi-retry`, one per wireless interface. *The run*: the counters are read at progress 9, before the TCP baseline, so that the compile is paid outside the retransmission window, and at 91, after the TCP window has closed and been extended where it was, so that the two windows cover the same run; the analysis at 93. *The switch*: `Checks.WifiRetryCounters`, in `Checks` rather than the `Tests` the recommendation named, because that is where the code keeps its on/off flags — the boolean rule of the configuration check, the `ChecksEnabled` projection and the run profile's *disabled* list come with the block — on as shipped, off from the file only, no panel box, no parameter. *On the reference machine*: a 27-second console run, 33 rows, the new one reading *66 of 211 frames needed retransmission (31.3 %), 22 of them more than once, 0 abandoned, over 24 seconds*, the far-end and gateway rows unchanged — the first wireless retry figure this project has produced, and 31 % on a link whose every other row is healthy is exactly the reading the manuals now say not to convict on.

**Tests.** Unit 705 × 2 → **745 × 2**, forty assertions per language: the mirrored six entries counted once; one entry moving giving the same figure with a different entries line; two entries differing, the larger used, both listed, never summed; abandoned frames in the denominator (25 %, not 30 %); the multiple-retry subset not added; nothing transmitted as an Information row without a rate; the backwards counter as *Unable to Check* with both readings; the four unavailable reasons and which side failed; an interface absent from the baseline; a failed query naming its error; every row weightless and tagged; the state words; the reader's `Add-Type` inside a try and behind the type check, a refused compile becoming the `addtype` reason, and one real reading on whatever machine runs the tests (this one: one interface, six PHY entries); the flag validated, projected and read at the three gated steps; the weightless collectors now seven, at 9, 10, 13, 82, 89, 90 and 91. Report-stage 201 × 2 → **211 × 2**: the flag projected on as shipped and off from the file with the run profile naming it, a reader that could not be compiled leaving a healthy run healthy, a measured rate deciding nothing. The chain's oracle expects one `wifi-retry` row per wireless interface the machine lists — a new machine fact, the interface GUID lines of `netsh wlan show interfaces`, added to the facts key — or one row saying there is none, and none at all when the flag is off, with the report's `ChecksEnabled` required to agree with the file; the result-set self-test 79 → **84**. Five mutants: the entries summed fails 7 assertions, the abandoned frames dropped from the denominator 1, the multiple-retry count added 2, the measured row weighted 3, and the backwards-counter branch removed stops the harness at the unsigned cast — detection of a cruder kind, with the reset row's status as the assertion behind it.

**The documents.** User manuals: the section 5 inventory and a section 9 question, md + html, both languages. IT deployment manuals: section 1's sentence on the one thing the tool compiles, the timeouts row, section 3.4's intro and the `WifiRetryCounters` row, the switch table, section 7's tag list and section 8's AppLocker row. Technical guides, six copies: a new 4.11, the tag list, limitation 6 amended and 18 added — and the list's second item numbered 12 renumbered, which moves what PR 5's record called limitation 16 to 17 in the source, as the rendered page had shown it all along. The field manual's counters row, the `wifi` trap extended and a Wi-Fi-only bullet, md + html; the SOP's station 2 and segment probe; the backlog's Status line on #61; the README's row and its #61 sentence; tests/README's totals and result-set paragraph.

**Chain on the tree before the review (same machine), `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Package -RequireHealthy -WorkDir $env:TEMP\nhc-1212-chain-e` on `6f25dc5`, a fresh directory: 34 / 34 in 864 s — at the second attempt.** The first, from `$env:TEMP\nhc-1212-chain-d`, also fresh, was 33 / 34 in 1,242 s: every step passed except the zh-TW IT real-window run, whose report read *Attention Required* under `-RequireHealthy` on one row — TCPv4, 584 segments sent, 12 retransmitted, 2.055 %, one twentieth of a point over the 2 % warning threshold across the 125-second IT window — while the new row in the same window read 187 of 552 wireless frames retried (33.9 %). That is a measurement, not a defect: the same air seen at two layers, on a machine whose every other row was healthy, and the kind of reading closed item #51's record says an idle machine's own traffic produces in a long window; the new row is weightless and decides nothing, which the shape of the failure confirms — a WARN from the TCP row alone. The second attempt, twenty minutes later: parser 0 × 2; validator 113 / 113; guards OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43 (457 s at the first attempt, 203 s at the second — the mutations run against every document, and the documents grew); unit **745 × 2**; report-stage **211 × 2**; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 132 / 132 in 104 s; headless GUI 4 / 4, the IT panel's 24 fixed-size controls in their boxes and its three spinners at 4 / 21 / 8 — no control was added, which is the decision above; the four real-window `gui` runs all *Overall Healthy*, **33 results** each where the last release had 32, the two IT ones over the configured 30 probes and 125-second sample; five acceptance runs, all *Overall Healthy*, at 33 / 35 / 33 / 38 / 38 results; result-set 84 / 84 on the live report; the package **682 990 bytes**, SHA256 `119a3a632573de6c3d4306923c005e66e7c08ab1e7056f02231557632423b120`, the validator 113 / 113 from inside the extracted package and its four entries opened as real windows — the same caveat as always: those figures are the step's own, and a release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit.

**Review round 1 — one finding, accepted, in the chain's oracle rather than in the tool.** The oracle expected one `wifi-retry` row per wireless interface the machine lists, and the tool writes one *aggregate* row when the reader fails before it can list them — the type refused, the WLAN service silent — so on a machine with two wireless adapters and a refused compile a valid report would have failed the chain. The oracle now takes a single *Unable to Check* row for that shape where more than one interface is listed, the way it already takes the single aggregate adapter-errors row when one statistics sample failed; a single Information row is not that shape and still counts as a missing row. One self-test case, 84 → **85**; the tool is unchanged.

**Review round 2 — one finding, accepted; it is round 1's fix, which had been too loose.** A lone `wifi-retry` row with the status *Unable to Check* is not only the aggregate reader failure: a reset counter, a failed statistics query, an interface absent from the baseline or two readings with no PHY entry in common each write a per-interface row with the same tag and status, so on a two-adapter machine one such row beside a missing one would have passed the oracle. The shape is now read off the row itself: the aggregate row's first details line ends with the reader's reason code — `addtype`, `open`, `enumerate`, `error` — a language-neutral token like a tag, which the per-interface rows' first line, the connection-state sentence, never carries; the oracle relaxes the count only for that shape. Two unit assertions per language hold both scripts to it (the three aggregate shapes end that way, the three per-interface error shapes do not; unit 745 → **747 × 2**), the self-test gains the negative case (85 → **86**), and the two scripts gain a comment where the line is written; the tool's behaviour is unchanged.

**Review round 3 — one finding, accepted, and the first in the tool.** A wireless adapter enabled or disabled during the sampling window leaves one reading with no interface and the other with one, and the analysis returned the wired-machine Information row — *no wireless interface on this computer* — on the strength of whichever reading came first, discarding the other and hiding that no delta could be calculated. The wired-machine row is now reserved for two readings that both list none; a one-sided none is an *Unable to Check* row, weightless, that names the reading which listed nothing, says the adapter was enabled or disabled during the test or the other reading failed, and carries the other reading's failure where it had one. It is the aggregate shape — its first details line ends with `none` — so the oracle's token list gains that word; the both-none row is Information and never matches. Three unit assertions per language (unit 747 → **750 × 2**): the two one-sided shapes, their sentence not being the wired one, the other failure named — and the shape count now five.

**Review round 4 — one finding, accepted, and it was this record's and tests/README's own count.** Round 3's commit said 751 unit assertions where the run says 750 — the aggregate-shape assertion was replaced, not added, so three were new — and the chain compares each unit summary against tests/README's table, which would have failed both unit steps on CI with every assertion passing. The correction had been written before the push and did not land: the step that applied it failed on a path error, and the shell line that followed was not chained on its success, so the commit went out with the wrong number. Both places now say 750, this paragraph is the only other change, and the lesson is the one PR #51's round 2 already taught this record — read the count off the run, and chain every step that a commit depends on.

**Review round 5 — one finding, accepted; round 3's case one interface further.** On a machine with two wireless interfaces, one disabled or removed during the window leaves both readings free of errors — the other interface is still there — and the analysis walked the ending reading's interfaces only, so the one that had gone got no row and its transition was silent. Every interface listed at the start and not at the end now gets its own *Unable to Check* row, weightless, per-interface shaped (its first details line is the connection-state sentence, so the oracle does not read it as the aggregate row), while the interface that stayed is measured exactly as before; the method lines moved out of the loop to be shared. Three unit assertions per language (unit 750 → **753 × 2**).

**Review round 6 — one finding, accepted; the oracle's side of round 5.** The oracle expected one `wifi-retry` row per interface listed *before* the launch, and the tool now writes a transition row for an interface that appeared or disappeared during the run, so a second interface enabled while the run was sampling — one measured row and one transition row — or one interface replaced by another — two transition rows — would have failed the chain on a valid report. The machine facts now keep the interface GUIDs themselves, read before the launch and again after the report, and the expected count is the union of the two lists; the aggregate-failure relaxation stands beside it. Three self-test cases (86 → **89**): the union with a measured row and a transition row, the same facts without the transition row, and a replacement's two transition rows. The tool is unchanged.

**Review round 7 — one finding, accepted; the self-test's own fixture.** Round 6's replacement case supplied the same *appeared* row twice and passed, because the oracle counted rows and nothing else: a tool that forgot the *gone* row and wrote the appearing one twice would have passed the result-set tests. Rather than teach the oracle the two directions' sentences, every per-interface row now names its interface GUID on a details line — *Interface GUID: …* — and the oracle holds each GUID either reading listed to exactly one row and refuses a row naming a GUID neither reading listed; a replacement therefore needs one row for the interface that went and one for the one that came, and the aggregate rows, which name no GUID, are untouched. Two unit assertions per language (753 → **755 × 2**: every per-interface shape names its GUID, no aggregate shape does); the self-test's fixtures carry the lines, the replacement case has one row in each direction, and the duplicated-row case is the new negative (89 → **90**).

**Review round 8 — one finding, accepted; the self-test's fixture once more, and the more serious of the two.** Round 7's fixtures named the reference machine's interface GUID while the fixture builder kept the live report's `wifi-retry` row, which names whatever interface the machine running the self-test has — so on any other machine the oracle would have found the fixture's GUID missing and the live one unlisted, and the self-test would have failed for the machine rather than for the tool, against its own stated rule that nothing in the tampered cases depends on the shape the live report happened to have. The fixture builder now drops the live row with the other environment-dependent rows and rebuilds one measured row for the one wireless interface the synthetic facts declare. The count is unchanged (90). On a report written by this tree the self-test passes whole; on one written before the GUID line existed, every tampered case still passes and only the positive control — the live report against the live facts — fails, as it should, because that report carries no GUID line: the machine-independence the rule asks for is of the tampered cases, and the control is meant to read the live report as it is.

**Review round 9 — one finding, accepted; the oracle had not been holding the rows to the decision that defines them.** Every `wifi-retry` row decides nothing — that is the fifth of the decisions this change was built on — and the unit tests hold both scripts to it, but the oracle never read the `Weightless` field, and the self-test's synthetic rows, copied from the configuration row, carried weight without anyone noticing: a regression that wrote a weighted reader-failure row would have turned a healthy report *Test Incomplete* and passed the result-set tests. The oracle now refuses a `wifi-retry` row that is not weightless, the synthetic rows carry the marking, and a weighted reader-failure row is the new negative case (90 → **91**).

**Review round 10 — one finding, accepted, and the cap the owner set.** The unit test's one real reading of the machine demanded PHY entries on the first interface, and a listed interface whose statistics query fails — a disconnected or driver-incompatible adapter — is a reading the tool supports (the query's error kept on the interface, no PHY entries, an *Unable to Check* row): on such a machine the unit step would have failed for the machine. The assertion now accepts, for every interface, PHY entries or a query error; the count is unchanged (755 × 2). **Ten rounds, ten findings, all accepted, none rejected or deferred**, one finding a round from first to last, every one since round 3 an edge of the fix before it: the tool's transition rows (3, 5), the oracle's expectation of them (1, 2, 6, 7, 9), the self-test's fixtures (8), the record's own count (4) and the unit test's live reading (10). The ten-round cap is the stop-loss the owner set on 2026-09-12; this fix is unreviewed by construction, and whether to extend the cap is the owner's call.

**Self-audit before round 11, on the owner's decision of 2026-09-12 to extend the cap by two rounds after one.** Ten rounds had walked one cell at a time through a matrix nobody had written down — the interface set at the start against the set at the end (the same, one more, one fewer, replaced, mixed) times the reader's outcome at each reading (a listing, none, a refused compile, a silent service, a failed enumeration, a failed query) — because every fix had closed the cell the reviewer named and no more. The matrix was walked whole this time, on three sides. *The tool:* two failed readings give one row for the first that names the second; a failure beside a one-sided none reports the failure and names the none; an interface that stayed beside one that came and one that went is three rows, each naming its own interface once, the two transitions *Unable to Check* and the one that stayed measured exactly as alone; readings with no PHY entry in common are an *Unable to Check* row that names its interface; a query that failed at the start names that failure — seven unit assertions per language, none of which found the tool wanting (755 → **762 × 2**). *The oracle and its self-test:* a wired machine's one Information row is clean where nothing is listed and a missing interface where one is; a one-sided none is the aggregate shape; the aggregate row beside a single listed interface is clean without the relaxation; a measured row beside a per-interface error is two interfaces' two rows; a row naming an interface neither reading listed is refused — six self-test cases, none of which found the oracle wanting (91 → **97**), and tests/README's inventory of the tampered variants now lists every Wi-Fi retry case. *The documents:* the technical guides' 4.11 and the IT deployment manuals' `WifiRetryCounters` row now say what ten rounds had built and no document had: an interface enabled, disabled or replaced during the run gets a weightless *Unable to Check* row of its own, and every per-interface row names its interface GUID. Nothing in the tool changed; what the audit found was coverage and words.

**Review round 11 — one finding, accepted; the first of the two extended rounds, and outside the matrix the audit walked.** The oracle read the interface GUIDs off `netsh wlan show interfaces` as every GUID-shaped value in the output, and a network or a profile named like a UUID is printed in the same block, so such a network would have given the oracle a phantom interface and a valid report a missing row. The GUIDs are now read by block: netsh separates interfaces with blank lines and prints the interface's GUID before anything else GUID-shaped in its block, so the first GUID-shaped value of a block is the interface and a later one is not — labels still unread, because they are localized. `Get-WlanInterfaceIds` is a function of its own so that the self-test can hold it to a synthetic listing: a UUID-shaped SSID and profile beside two interface blocks give two interfaces, and a listing with no block gives none (97 → **99**). The tool's own `netsh` parser for the `wifi` row treats any GUID-shaped value as the start of an interface block and has the same exposure to a UUID-shaped network name; it predates this change and is left for an item of its own.

**Review round 12 — one finding, accepted; the last of the two extended rounds, and round 11's rule one case further.** An adapter renamed to a UUID-shaped alias is printed, as the repository's own Windows 11 fixture shows, with its name before its GUID, so the first GUID-shaped value of a block would have been the alias and the real interface missing. The identifier is now read from the line whose label is `GUID` — the one label besides `SSID`, which the connected-interface count has relied on since the chain existed, that netsh does not localize — after a half-width or full-width colon, and the shape of every other value in the block no longer matters; the self-test's listing renames its second adapter to a UUID and gains a full-width-colon case (99 → **100**). **Twelve rounds, twelve findings, all accepted, none rejected or deferred**; the two extended rounds found the oracle's reading of netsh wanting twice, outside the matrix the audit had walked, and the tool itself has not had a finding since round 5. The cap the owner extended is reached; this last fix is unreviewed by construction, and the decision is the owner's.

**Chain on the tree the loop ended on (same machine), `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Package -RequireHealthy -WorkDir $env:TEMP\nhc-1212-chain-j` on `6bb8324`, a fresh directory: 34 / 34 in 918 s.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 113 / 113; guards OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43 (219 s); unit **762 × 2**; report-stage **211 × 2**; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 132 / 132 in 116 s; headless GUI 4 / 4, the IT panel's 24 fixed-size controls in their boxes and its three spinners at 4 / 21 / 8 — no control was added; the four real-window `gui` runs all *Overall Healthy*, **33 results** each, the two IT ones over the configured 30 probes and 125-second sample; five acceptance runs, all *Overall Healthy*, at 33 / 35 / 33 / 38 / 38 results; result-set **100 / 100** on the live report; the package **689 256 bytes**, SHA256 `983b05610a7bad21d6c8c80fe9e9b9ded368c78ccd59835c06ba30dc2a7ae225`, the validator 113 / 113 from inside the extracted package and its four entries opened as real windows — the same caveat as always: those figures are the step's own, and a release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit. CI on `6bb8324` (run 34674484657) passed. **During the two extended rounds a chain on `108374d`, the round-10 tree, from `$env:TEMP\nhc-1212-chain-h`, came out 33 / 34 in 1,163 s**: every step passed except the zh-TW IT entry opened from the extracted package, whose report read *Fault Detected* on the TCPv4 row alone — 1,300 segments sent, 62 retransmitted, 4.769 % in a 9-second window, over the 2 % warning threshold and the 50-retransmission floor together — while the new row in the same window read 63 of 615 wireless frames retried (10.2 %). The third such reading of this record, on the tree of a change about exactly that: the system-wide counter measured this machine's own traffic, over a congested air, and the row that separates the air from the rest was there beside it, weightless, saying so. Not a defect; recorded as the measurement it is.

## v1.2.12 — the near-end rung, and a fingerprint that reads what the row measured · 2026-09-12 — backlog #60, #67, #32 — the first change of a batch that ships together; not yet released

**What and why.** Three items with one subject — what is near this machine, and what a failed rung is allowed to say. #60 asked for the rung the shipped ladder never had: a host on the users' own subnet that is not the gateway, so that the local path is measured without the gateway's control plane or the WAN in it, and for the rule that a ladder's rungs cannot be subtracted into segment figures, stated in the documents and not only in a comment. #67 was raised by 1.2.11's review: a gateway that answered every probe slowly was titled *Gateway does not answer*, because the fingerprint read a row's status and nothing else about it. #32 asked for the server that answered the DHCP lease, which the report had never named. They ship together because the near-end rung redefines how the gateway row is read — a near-end host that answered leaves the gateway itself, one that lost its replies points at the local path before it — and that reading is what the summary's gateway lines had to learn at the same moment they learned to tell a slow gateway from a silent one. **This is the first pull request of a batch, on the owner's decision of 2026-09-12: #60 with #32 and #67 here, then #61's retry-counter half, then #52, released together as 1.2.12.** The version moves here so that the packaged validator holds every document to it; the release, and the dates on the closed lines, come with the batch.

**Version.** 1.2.12 in both scripts, the packaged validator (`TOOL_VERSION`; `FUNCTION_COUNT` 108 → **111**: `Get-DhcpServerTable`, `Get-DhcpServerText`, `Test-NearEndTargetPlacement`), `README_BILINGUAL.md`, the four manuals in both formats, the six guide copies and the repository README; `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated over the same 28 files, 20 of which changed. The manuals name no version but their own; the guides keep their history sentences and gain 1.2.12 ones.

**#60 — the near-end rung.** `Tests.NearEndTarget` — `Name`, `Address`, `Required`, one object and not a list — in the default configuration and in both shipped files, with an empty address, which is *absent by default*. Its address has a narrower rule than the other ping targets': an IPv4 address, never a name or a placeholder, because the run has to place the host before anything is sent and a name would put the rung behind the resolver; the configuration check says so in the *Configured Targets* row. It is placed before it is probed — `Test-NearEndTargetPlacement`, against the primary adapters' gateways and their address/prefix subnets. One of the gateways is refused as a configuration mistake — and, since round 1 of the review, so are this computer's own addresses and a subnet's network or broadcast address: a weightless `ERROR` row naming which, and the weighted *did not run* row where the target is required, the shape every unusable required target has had since 1.2.8. An address outside every primary subnet is reported as *not probed*: a weightless `INFO` row naming the machine's subnets — a fact about where the machine is, a laptop at home with the office's file, rather than about the network — and the same weighted row where it was required. Only an on-subnet address is measured, by exactly the path the other targets use — `Invoke-PingMeasurement`, the route lookup before and after, the adaptive sample — under its own tag, `ping-near-end`, first in the ladder. The entry is built by the run and never read from `PingTargets`, so a list entry cannot promote itself to the rung and the traceroute, which walks the list for its target, never meets it. The near-end row and the gateway row each carry a **rung line** — which segments their probes crossed and which they did not: the local path only, answered by an ordinary host; the local path, answered by the gateway's own control plane — and the near-end row carries the reading rule: a rung that passes clears what it crossed at that moment, the first rung that fails puts the problem beyond the last rung that passed and no closer, and the figures of two rungs are separate traffic at separate moments and cannot be subtracted into a loss figure for the segment between them. A far-end row claims no rung, because where an extra target sits relative to the gateway is not something it measured. The *What to tell IT* summary's `gateway-unreachable` key chooses its second line from the near-end row — passed, lost its replies, or neither — and its line count is unchanged, so the user manual's *two to six lines* stands. The documents: the IT deployment manual's section 3.3 says how to choose one, why the gateway cannot serve as it, how to read the rungs and what it costs, and its section 4 says why there is no switch for it; the user manual's title table changes and its section 9 gains a question; the technical guides' 4.4 gains the paragraph and section 8 a limitation 16; `sop/network-troubleshooting-sop.md` gains the segment probe — the three-way comparison, its wired-host prerequisite, its source-bound probe, and the refusal of per-hop loss from TTL-limited probes; the field manual gains a `ping-near-end` row and the lane's new first move. **Not built, on the item's own instruction:** per-hop loss from TTL-limited probes, and any figure made by subtracting one rung from another. **Not built, by decision:** a switch or a panel field. The near-end host is a property of the site, chosen once by whoever knows which host on that subnet is stable and answers ICMP; it belongs in the file, where `-ConfigPath` can point a console run at a candidate, and the IT panel's fixed layout — every control measured by the headless GUI step — is not the place for a value nobody types per run. The manual says so where the switches are listed.

**#67 — the fingerprint reads what the row measured.** Every result carries a `Rule` field — additive under JSON schema 2, the way `Weightless` was in 1.2.8 — empty on every row but a ping row, where it names the measurement the status follows from: `loss` where the loss band decided it (a row nothing answered included, and a withheld one, since the band it reached was a loss band), `latency` where the replies that did arrive did. `Get-FingerprintSummary`'s gateway predicate excludes a `FAIL` row whose rule is `latency`, and nothing else about the gateway keys moved: a gateway row that lost its replies, and a failed gateway row that names no rule — the *No testable target* shape — read exactly as they did. **Decided, and recorded as the item asked: a slow-but-answering gateway is a quality fingerprint, not a connectivity one.** The gateway was reached; `ping-gateway` is one of the four quality tags already; so a slow gateway alone is `quality`, and beside another failure `mixed`. The `gateway-up-internet-dead` key still requires a gateway row that *passed*, so a slow gateway beside a failed group is `mixed` — which names neither, and is right to, because two things are wrong and the summary must not pick one. The `gateway-unreachable` summary's first line now says what a loss failure is — *did not answer the pings sent to it, or lost too many of them* — and its #58 line no longer says *or answering them slowly*, since that case no longer reaches it; the user manuals' cells, the IT manuals' section 7 and the guides' 4.4 follow, and every sentence that named #67 as open is gone.

**#32 — the server that answered the lease.** `Get-DhcpServerTable` reads `Win32_NetworkAdapterConfiguration` once, through the `Get-CimOrWmiInstance` every CIM query of the tool passes through, and keys `DHCPServer` by interface index; the NetTCPIP snapshot looks each adapter up in it, the CIM snapshot reads the field it already had. `Get-DhcpServerText` decides the four shapes in order: a static address names no server whatever the class holds; a class that could not be read is *unavailable*, named as such, because a blank would read as *no server*, which is a claim; a lease names its server; a configuration holding no server address says so. **The acceptance asked for one row per adapter, and it lands as a line in each adapter row's own details instead** — beside `DHCP: Enabled`, the mode it qualifies — because the reader on the DHCP lane is reading that row already, and a second row per adapter in the collapsed IT section would repeat the adapter's identity to put one address further from the mode it belongs with. The three shapes the acceptance named are all there, in both languages; `tests/environment_probe.ps1` records `DHCPServer` beside `DHCPEnabled` so that a campaign can compare; and the field manual's *the one thing the report cannot tell you on this lane* is now *the report tells you which server answered*. The query is not attempted twice — it is not a performance-counter read, and 1.2.8's decision about the adapter-configuration queries stands.

**What the tests hold, and the mutants written before they were trusted.** Unit 587 × 2 → **629 × 2**: the rule on eight rows; the rung line by line count and position in both languages; the placement's nine cases — the gateway, inside the subnet, outside it, the subnets reported, a multihomed machine placing by either subnet and refusing either gateway, an adapter with no prefix placing nothing, and the gateway refused before any subnet is consulted; `Test-IPv4InCidr` on its own, because the chain's oracle now loads it from the package; the DHCP text's four shapes; and four assertions on the functions' own bodies — the fingerprint's gateway predicate reads the rule, the adapter row prints the server text, the near-end entry is built by the run, the traceroute never sees the key — plus one that the probe records the field. Report-stage 169 × 2 → **187 × 2**: scenario D gains the slow gateway alone (`quality`), the slow gateway beside a failed group (`mixed`), the gateway that lost its replies (`gateway-unreachable`), the rule field on every row, the near-end rung choosing the summary's second line — passed, lost, slow and not probed — and, in a healthy run, a degraded near-end host (`quality`), an optional one that answered nothing (named as such) and one that was not on this network (named among the rows not measured, never as one that did not answer). The result-set oracle expects a `ping-near-end` row only where the configuration names one, judged against the machine's gateways and subnets the way the run judges it, so the shipped configuration expects none. **Six mutants of the en-US script, every one caught:** the gateway predicate reading the status alone (3 report-stage assertions and 1 unit assertion fail), the near-end rung line never written (1), the placement never refusing the gateway (3), a static address printing the stale server the class holds (1), the second pass of a continued sample keeping the first pass's rule (1), and latency never setting the rule (3).

**Chain on the tree before the review (Windows 11 10.0.26200, Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, Python 3.14.4), `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Package -RequireHealthy -WorkDir $env:TEMP\nhc-1212-chain` on `f7a2541`: 34 / 34 in 861 s, clean at the first attempt.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 113 / 113; guards OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43; unit **629 × 2**; report-stage **187 × 2**; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 132 / 132 in 117 s; headless GUI 4 / 4 with the IT panel's 24 fixed-size controls in their boxes and its three spinners at 4 / 21 / 8 against their configured maxima — the panel gained no field, which is the decision above; the four real-window `gui` runs all *Overall Healthy* under `-RequireHealthy`, the two IT ones over the configured 30 probes and 125-second sample; five acceptance runs, all *Overall Healthy*; result-set 79 / 79; and the package built from the tracked files, **624 804 bytes**, SHA256 `b0d160eb67e379945f5f7ec90c95319ab2ef074ad5cbfac3aad4c8c922a228d0`, the validator 113 / 113 from inside the extracted package and its four entries opened as real windows. As in 1.2.8 to 1.2.11 those package figures are the step's own: this record is a tracked file and this paragraph changed the asset's bytes after it was built, which is why a release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit.

**What the runs proved, and what only the tests did.** The chain's thirteen reports — the four window runs, the five acceptance runs and the four package windows — carry the `DHCP server:` line on every one of their 65 adapter rows: the reference machine's Wi-Fi adapter names `192.168.1.1`, which is its gateway, as the lease's server usually is on a small network and which is exactly the address the IT manual says is not a near-end candidate; its four virtual adapters with static addresses read *none (static address, no lease)*, 52 times across the two languages. The *unavailable* and *none recorded* shapes were seen only in the unit cases, since the class answered on every run. All thirteen gateway rows carry the rung line. `Rule` is on every one of the thirteen reports' 428 rows: 426 empty and 2 `loss`, the two being the silent optional extra target of the two *unreachable targets* runs, one per language. **No run produced a near-end row** — the shipped configuration names no host — so the placement, the refusal rows, the near-end rung line and the summary's chosen second line have been seen only in the unit and report-stage cases and in the mutants that remove them; and no run produced a failed gateway row, so the fingerprint's new predicate, like #58's sentences before it, is proved by the report-stage cases and the mutant alone. The run that would show the rest is one with `NearEndTarget` set to a host on the reference machine's subnet, and one on a machine where that host is not on the subnet, which the next campaign can make.

**Self-audit after the chain, before the review: four sentences claiming more than they could.** Read back against the code and the machine the way PR #43's audit was. *The traceroute never traces toward a host one hop away* — false, since a site that lists an on-subnet host first in `PingTargets` (the IT manual's own example does) has always had that host traced; what is true, and what the guides and the two scripts' comments now say, is that the traceroute never traces toward the near-end key. *The IT section names the gateway's neighbour* as a near-end candidate — the `gateway-neighbor` row names the gateway's own MAC, not a host, so the IT manual now names the DHCP server alone and says that on a small network it is usually the gateway. *A healthy run is longer by a few milliseconds* — four probes to a host on the subnet and two route lookups are tens of milliseconds; *well under a second* now. And the user manual's *the trouble is at the router* — a conviction the rest of the manual refuses; now *beyond your cable or Wi-Fi: at the router, or in the way it answers pings*. The SOP's three inferences of the segment probe were softened to *points at … a suspect to corroborate* for the same reason. These edits came after the chain above, so the tree it ran on differs from the branch head by those sentences and two comments; the chain on the tree that ships is run at the end of the review, as 1.2.11's was.

**Review round 1 — one finding, accepted, and it was a hole in the placement.** The placement refused the gateway and then let the subnet test speak, so one of **this computer's own addresses** — inside its own subnet by definition — was placed as the near-end host, probed, answered by this machine's own stack without crossing a cable, a radio or a switch, and reported under a rung line claiming the whole local path; a gateway failing beside it would then have had the summary clear the local path and blame the gateway on a probe that never left the machine. Two more shapes of the same mistake were closed with it, before a reviewer had to name them: the **network and broadcast addresses** of the subnet the value falls in, which no host holds (a /31 and a /32 have neither, RFC 3021, and are left to the other tests). All three are refused before the subnet test can accept them — `self`, `not-a-host`, beside `gateway` — as weightless `ERROR` rows naming which, with the weighted *did not run* row where the target is required. The chain's result-set oracle now places a configured near-end target with the tool's own `Test-NearEndTargetPlacement`, loaded from the package over the primary adapters the facts carry, rather than with a copy of the rule that this finding would have had to be fixed in twice. Nine unit assertions per language on the three refusals — the own address before and after a subnet is known, either own address on a multihomed machine, the broadcast and the network address, the last host before the broadcast, a /31's all-ones host left to the gateway test, a /8's broadcast found across the octets and a host whose last octet is 255 inside it — and a mutant that drops the own-address branch fails three of them. The IT deployment manual's key row and near-end paragraph, the guides' 4.4 and the field manual's row name the two new refusals.

**Review round 2 — two findings, both accepted; the first is round 1's fix one parser further out.** *(1) The refusals compared strings.* `Test-IsValidIPv4Address` and the subnet test parse, and .NET's parser accepts spellings a person does not mean — measured on the reference machine: `3221225994` is `192.0.2.10`, `0xC0.0.2.10` is too, `192.0.2` is `192.0.0.2`, and `192.0.2.010` is `192.0.2.8`, a leading zero read as octal — so a configured `3221225994` matched neither the own-address list nor the gateways as text, passed the subnet test as an address, and was probed as an ordinary near-end host: round 1's false pass, spelled differently. Two things close it, and the second is the one that matters. `Test-NearEndTargetPlacement` compares parsed, dotted-decimal forms on every side (`Get-CanonicalIPv4Text`) and reports the form it placed. And the near-end target's rule is narrower than *an IPv4 address* now: `Test-NearEndAddressSyntax`, applied by the configuration check and by the run, accepts the dotted-decimal spelling and nothing else — because the octal case shows why normalising alone is not enough: a file that says `192.0.2.010` and a row that probes `192.0.2.8` are the confusion this key exists to prevent, and the only reading that cannot go wrong is the one where what the file says and what the probe is sent to are the same string. The chain's oracle applies the same rule. Sixteen unit assertions per language — seven on the rule, three on the canonical form, three on the placement given a numeric or hexadecimal spelling, one on the form it reports, two on the function bodies that the run and the configuration check both apply the rule; `FUNCTION_COUNT` 111 → **113**. *(2) The summary called a near-end host that lost some replies silent.* `$nearEndLost` is a `FAIL` row whose rule is `loss`, which a required near-end host reaches with critical loss and some replies back as well as with none, and the second line said *did not answer either*; it now says *lost its replies too - all of them, or too many*, says that this *points at* the local path rather than that the fault *is* there, and names the host itself as the other possibility. The user manuals' cell, the guides' 4.4, the field manual's two rows and the *What and why* paragraph above follow it.

**Two corrections to the round-2 comment, made as soon as they were seen.** *CI on `7b71416` was not green*: it failed its two `unit` cases on the chain's own check that `tests/README.md` advertises the total the run produced — the round-1 commit raised the count to 638 × 2 and left the README at 629 × 2, and the round-2 commit raised it again to 654 × 2 with the README still unread; the README now says 654 × 2, and the check that caught it is exactly the one PR #9 added for this. The comment said *green* from the run of the commit before, which is the mistake PR #47 recorded — a number read off the wrong row. And *result-set 79 / 79* in the round-2 comment was measured against the chain's staged copy of the script from before the round, which lacks the two new functions the oracle now loads; against the current tree it is **79 / 79**, run with `-ConfigDir` pointing at the repository's own `en-US` folder, where the two functions are; the chain restages the script on every run, so its `resultset` step never sees a stale copy.

**Review round 3 — two findings, both accepted, and the first is the one that decides what the near-end row may claim at all.** *(1) Subnet membership is not the path.* The probes are sent unbound — 1.2.9 established that a ping row's route line is a selection and never an observation — so a host inside the attached subnet can still be reached through a VPN, a second connection or a more specific route, and the rung line was claiming the local path on the strength of an address. The row now claims the rung only where the route table selected, before and after the probes, a source address on the target's own subnet — the connected route, which is the adapter attached to that subnet — and says which source and interface it was. Where it did not, or the selection changed between the two lookups, or the lookup was unavailable, the row keeps its title and its measurement, says why it cannot claim the rung, and is **tagged `ping-target`**, so that `Get-FingerprintSummary` never reads it as a witness for a path it may not have crossed — the tag assignment stays a literal for the document-fact step, and a continued sample's second pass can withdraw the claim its first pass made. The placement now returns the subnet the target fell in, which is what the row tests the selection against; the chain's oracle asks `Get-RouteSelection` the same question once, and a route that changes during a run is the one shape it cannot predict, which the resultset note is for. Binding the probe instead was considered and is not available on this path: the .NET `Ping` class takes no source address, and a raw socket would want the rights this tool promises not to need. Sixteen unit assertions per language: the attested row and its tag, the source it names, a source off the subnet, a changed selection, an unavailable lookup and a missing subnet each declining the claim with the measurement unchanged, the second pass withdrawing it, the run and the second pass handing the subnet on, and the placement reporting it. *(2) A required near-end target with no address was dropped from the ladder*, so the run could read Healthy without the check it had been told to require, and the configuration check said nothing either, since it skipped blank addresses. Blank and required is now a required check that did not run — the weightless notice and the weighted row, the shape every unusable required target has had since 1.2.8 — and a *Configured Targets* finding; blank and optional stays the shipped, disabled state. Report-stage scenario L, eight assertions per language, holds both shapes and that the first makes the run *Test Incomplete*. The IT deployment manual, the guides, the field manual and the user manuals say both.

**Review round 4 — two findings, both accepted; both are round 3's rule applied where round 3 had not.** *(1) The gateway row's rung line was still unconditional.* The same argument — the probes are unbound, and a VPN or a more specific route can carry a probe for the gateway's address through another interface — holds for the gateway row, and its line claimed the local path and the gateway's control plane on the strength of the address. It now claims its rung on the same terms as the near-end row: only where the selection before and after the probes is a source on the subnet of an adapter that supplied that gateway, which `Test-PingTargets` collects per gateway address from the primary adapters that list it; otherwise the line says it cannot claim the path. The tag stays `ping-gateway` — the gateway is still the target the row measured, and the keys that read the gateway row have read it that way since 1.2.3 — so what round 3 made a tag change for the near-end row is a sentence change here. The parameter that carried one subnet became `-RungSubnets`, a list, because a gateway can be supplied by more than one adapter. *(2) The summary paired any near-end row with any failed gateway.* On a multihomed machine with gateway rows for adapters A and B both failing, a near-end host that answered through A cleared the local path for B as well. Every ping row now carries **`Path`** — the interface alias the route table selected where the two lookups agreed, empty where they did not or the target was a name; additive under schema 2 like `Weightless` and `Rule` — and the summary takes the near-end line only where the near-end row's `Path` is set and equals every failed gateway row's; a failed gateway on another adapter, one whose lookups did not agree, or several failed gateways not all on the near-end row's adapter, keep the neutral line. The reviewer's other suggestion — keep the neutral line whenever more than one gateway failed — was the weaker one, because two gateways on the same adapter can both fail and a near-end host on that adapter does speak to both. Thirteen unit assertions per language (the gateway rung claimed and not claimed with the tag kept, `Path` on seven rows in both states, the gateway's subnets handed on, the fingerprint reading the field) and six report-stage assertions (the pairing on another adapter, a gateway with no path, two gateways on two adapters, two gateways on the near-end row's own adapter, a pathless near-end row, and the field on every row); a mutant that pairs without reading `Path` fails three. The IT deployment manual, the guides (4.4 and the JSON list), the field manual, the user manuals' cell and the README follow.

**Review round 5 — one finding, accepted, and it is the last term the attestation was missing.** The same source address and interface can still be a route *through a router*: a `/32` to the near-end host via the default gateway on that adapter keeps both and crosses the gateway all the same, and rounds 3 and 4 had let it attest a rung. `Get-RouteSelection` was already handed the route object with its next hop and threw it away; it now keeps `NextHop` and says whether the route is `OnLink` — a next hop of `0.0.0.0` (or `::`), the connected network the adapter is attached to — and both rung claims require the selection to be on-link as well as on the right subnet, with the next hop part of what the before-and-after comparison has to agree on. The attested rung lines say *on-link with no next hop*; the unclaimed ones name *a more specific route through a router* among the reasons; the chain's oracle asks the same. Ten unit assertions per language — the next hop kept and a router route not on-link, a connected route on-link with the unspecified next hop, a near-end row and a gateway row reached through a router declining the claim with the tag kept and the adapter still named, a next hop that changed between the lookups as a changed selection, the attestation reading the flag — and the manuals, the guides and the field manual say *on-link*. **This is round 5, the loop's budget, and this fix is unreviewed by construction**; whether to run a sixth round is the owner's call.

**Chain on the tree the loop ended on (same machine), `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Package -RequireHealthy -WorkDir $env:TEMP\nhc-1212-chain-b` on `fb727d8`: 34 / 34 in 870 s.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 113 / 113; guards OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43; unit **693 × 2**; report-stage **201 × 2**; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 132 / 132 in 109 s; headless GUI 4 / 4, the IT panel's 24 fixed-size controls in their boxes and its three spinners at 4 / 21 / 8; the four real-window `gui` runs all *Overall Healthy* under `-RequireHealthy`, the two IT ones over the configured 30 probes and 125-second sample; five acceptance runs, all *Overall Healthy*; result-set 79 / 79; the package **648 340 bytes**, SHA256 `3faa727bd4395524b3fc8bd5a5c390727a7541f1bdaa0fe0f253a98f3268e4ab`, the validator 113 / 113 from inside the extracted package and its four entries opened as real windows — the same caveat as above: those figures are the step's own, and a release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit. CI on `fb727d8` (run 34659301927) passed. **A first attempt in the directory the `f7a2541` chain had used came out 33 / 34**: every step passed except the campaign self-test, 116 / 16, and each of the sixteen read state the earlier run had left under `campaign\` — an answers log with three gate entries where two were expected, an attempt *recorded earlier at 06:24*, exit codes shifted by a remembered invocation. The same step alone in a fresh directory passed 132 / 132, and the run above is a fresh directory. So that step's state outlives an invocation, tests/README's sentence that a reused work dir *always runs the current files* is true of the staged copies only, and this record's rule from here on is a new directory for every run it cites; the self-test's own clean-up is a tools item, not this change's. **And the first report from a real machine with a near-end target**, which no chain run produces because the shipped configuration leaves it empty: the reference machine's shipped en-US configuration with `NearEndTarget` at `192.168.1.250` — an address on its Wi-Fi subnet that nothing answered from, so that the probes would be lost — through `-ConsoleOnly -ConfigPath`, four probes, three timeouts and one *DestinationHostUnreachable*. The row is tagged `ping-near-end` with `Rule` = `loss` and `Path` = `Wi-Fi`; its rung line names *source 192.168.1.106 via Wi-Fi - an address on that subnet, on-link with no next hop - before and after the probes*; the gateway row's rung line says the same of its own subnet; the run reads *Overall Healthy* because the target is optional, and the fingerprint is `healthy`, since an optional target that lost its replies is an *Info* row and the fingerprint reads failures. What that run does not show is a near-end row *passing* on a real machine — the subnet has no other host that answers — so the attested-and-passed shape rests on the unit fixtures and the chain's oracle.

**Review round 6 — two findings, both accepted; both are round 5's own additions read one field further out.** *(1) A gateway row that could not claim its rung was still paired.* `Path` was set wherever the two lookups agreed, and round 5 had just established that an agreed selection can still run through a router: a failed gateway row whose stable route went through another router carried the same interface alias as a passing on-link near-end row, and the summary paired them and blamed the gateway itself for probes that had crossed an additional router. `Path` now means what the pairing needs — the adapter a near-end or gateway row claimed its rung on, set only where the rung was claimed — so a gateway reached through a router, and every far-end row, which claims no rung, carries none, and the summary's own test is unchanged: one field, one meaning. The IT deployment manual's field list and its reading paragraph, the guides' 4.4 paragraphs and field list, and the field manual's row say so. *(2) The route line could read as unchanged beside a rung line refusing the claim because the selection had changed.* Round 5 made the next hop part of the before-and-after comparison, and the route line still compared source and interface and printed neither hop, so a selection whose only change was its next hop was described as one route. `Get-RouteSelectionText` now names the hop — *on-link* for the connected network, *through* the router otherwise — and `Format-RouteSelection` compares it, so that pair reads as a change with both hops named; a name's several addresses are still compared on the adapter alone (`-NoHop`), because the question that branch answers is *which adapter*, and two addresses of one name reached by different hops through one adapter are not a routing difference. Twelve unit assertions per language — the hop named and not named, both readings distinct from a hopless shape and equal to it without the hop, an on-link pair reading as one selection, a changed hop reading as a change with both hops, the name branch agreeing across hops and not calling them a difference, the routed near-end and gateway rows carrying no `Path` and their route lines naming the hop, the changed-hop row's route line, and the field reading the attestation — and three mutants: `Path` set on agreement (5 assertions), the formatter ignoring the hop (2), the reading omitting it (6). Unit **693 × 2 → 705 × 2**.

**Review round 7 — a clean pass on `76c4c7b`, and the loop's end.** Seven rounds, ten findings, all accepted and none rejected or deferred. The loop went past the skill's five-round budget on the owner's decision of 2026-09-12, under stop-loss conditions the record can state because none of them fired: a finding recurring after its fix, a rejection raised twice, a finding outside the change's scope, three rounds without narrowing, a silent reviewer, or ten rounds in all. What the seven rounds were is one argument carried to its end — the near-end rung may claim the local path only for what the probes are attested to have crossed: not the machine's own address (round 1), not a spelling the parser reads differently from the file (2), not subnet membership (3), not the gateway's address either (4), not a route through a router (5), and not a field that meant something weaker than the pairing that read it (6). Every fix round but the last produced the next finding, which is 1.2.10's and 1.2.11's pattern again and the reason the loop runs to a clean pass rather than to a count. The commit that records this pass is the last on the branch and is unreviewed by design, as 1.2.11's was.

**Chain on the tree the loop ended on, `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Package -RequireHealthy -WorkDir $env:TEMP\nhc-1212-chain-c` on `76c4c7b` — a fresh directory, per the rule above: 34 / 34 in 1,024 s.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 113 / 113; guards OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43; unit **705 × 2**; report-stage 201 × 2; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 132 / 132 in 143 s; headless GUI 4 / 4, the IT panel's 24 fixed-size controls in their boxes and its three spinners at 4 / 21 / 8; the four real-window `gui` runs all *Overall Healthy* under `-RequireHealthy`, the two IT ones over the configured 30 probes and 125-second sample; five acceptance runs, all *Overall Healthy*; result-set 79 / 79; the package **651 873 bytes**, SHA256 `46f65bd98f7abe1980b1b340e123cc00709f5d8fc6e79fbad3bd37569e13d23d`, the validator 113 / 113 from inside the extracted package and its four entries opened as real windows — the same caveat as above: those figures are the step's own, and a release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit. CI on `76c4c7b` (run 34662619097) passed. The near-end run on the reference machine was repeated on this tree: the near-end and gateway rows read *source 192.168.1.106 via Wi-Fi, on-link* with `Path` = `Wi-Fi`, and the far-end row reads *through 192.168.1.1* with an empty `Path` — round 6's rule in a real report.

## v1.2.11 — the words the numbers were owed · 2026-09-11 — documents release, carrying two sentences in the tool — backlog #56, #57, #58, #64

**What and why.** Four items, every one of them decided on 2026-09-10 or 2026-09-11 before a line of this release was written, and every one of them closing here because what it still owed was words in a packaged document — the tables that present the thresholds, the paragraph that describes the route selection, the sentence that says what the retransmission percentage divides by, the sentence that says what a failed gateway ping establishes. Two of the four owed a sentence to the tool as well, which is why this is not a documents release in the sense 1.2.4, 1.2.5 and 1.2.6 were: #57's acceptance asks the **row** to state its denominator, and #58's asks the failed gateway **row** and the *What to tell IT* summary to say what the failure does and does not establish. Both are additions to text the tool already prints. Nothing the tool measures changed: no check was added or removed, no threshold, sampling window, verdict rule or JSON field moved, and `FUNCTION_COUNT` stays 108.

**Version.** 1.2.11 in both scripts, the packaged validator (`TOOL_VERSION`; `FUNCTION_COUNT` stays **108**), `README_BILINGUAL.md`, the four manuals in both formats, the six guide copies and the repository README; `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated over the same 28 files, 18 of which changed. The manuals carried six sentences that named 1.2.10 as history — the adaptive-sampling heading, the starting count, the count no longer warning on its own, the small-sample Information row, the ping samples the tool goes back for, and the third small-sample row that was a warning — and a manual may name no version but its own, so they now say *earlier versions* or name none, which is the move 1.2.8 made for the same reason; the guides keep their history sentences, as the label rule allows.

**#56 — every threshold table names each value for what it is.** The decision was road (1) of the three the item set out: the twelve values are operational defaults chosen without an external basis, said per value rather than under a table-wide disclaimer, and no number changes. Three things ship it. *The IT deployment manual's section 3.5*, in both languages and both formats, opens by saying so — the two warning deltas of 1 are the policy *any at all is worth showing*, the rest are magnitudes somebody chose — and its table gains a **Basis** column, one cell per row, so that the reader who sees *2 % warning* is told beside it that 2 came from nowhere; the thirteenth value, `MinimumTcpRetransmissionsForVerdict`, which 1.2.10 added after the item was written, is named there too, with its reasoning recorded under closed item #51 and no external basis either. *The technical guides' limitation 9*, all six copies, now says **why** calibration is necessary rather than leaving it as advice, and points at the third thing: `docs/thresholds.md`, a page in the repository and not the package, version-free like the application-control page and linked by tag from the guides and from the IT manual's sections 3.5 and 10. It holds what the item's body held — the thirteen values one by one, the search that found nothing so that nobody repeats it, the six published documents with what each actually defines and the date each was verified against its source, the two things Y.1541's Table 1 says that sharpen the point, the latency values' quantity mismatch with the first draft's backwards conversion kept on record, and the rule that an unverified citation may not be the basis of a threshold, a sentence or a test. It had to move: a closed item keeps one line on the backlog page and its body goes to the repository's history, and the acceptance asked for the standards record to be stated once in a place the guides can point at. **What it does not do:** change a value. A later calibration is now a documented improvement to a value labelled as chosen without a basis, and the page says how to make one — the source into its table first, then the number.

**#57 — the row says what its percentage divides by, and the arithmetic is pinned to the sentence.** The sub-question the item refused to prescribe was answered on 2026-09-10 from two sources: `Segments Sent/sec` counts every segment this computer sent, acknowledgements included, and excludes segments containing *only* retransmitted bytes, while `Segments Retransmitted/sec` counts every segment containing one or more previously transmitted bytes, so a mixed segment is in both counts. The decision was the first of the item's two ways to close — no data-segment rate, and the figure that prints says what it divides by — with the Win32 path refused on what it costs: the Administrators group, collection that is off until something turns it on, and a turning-on Microsoft advises against undoing. So every TCP retransmission row that prints the figure now carries one more line, in both languages, naming the counter and saying that the ratio is the tool's own and not comparable with a published retransmission rate — and saying nothing about which way it errs, because the two biases point opposite ways and neither has been quantified.
