The technical guides' section 4.8 carries the same in a paragraph under the formula, with the RFC 4898 and RFC 6349 quantities it is not; the IT deployment manual's table cell says what *sent* means in one sentence; the user manuals gain a question in section 9. **The unit test pins the arithmetic to the statement** (five assertions per language): a fixture of 5 000 → 9 000 sent and 100 → 171 retransmitted has to print 4000, 71 and **1.775%**, name `Segments Sent/sec`, and name it on the row without traffic too, since that row prints the same figure.

**#58 — what a failed gateway ping establishes, said wherever the person reads it.** The gateway ping stays `Required` (decided 2026-09-10: a machine that cannot reach its own gateway usually does have a problem worth reporting, and the alternative — closed item #39's weightless marking — would leave a Wi-Fi-only machine's one near-end measurement unable to move anything). What the item still owed was words, derived from `sop/support-engineer-field-manual.md`'s `gateway-unreachable` entry so that the two cannot drift: a gateway that answers promptly is good evidence that the near-end path works; one that does not answer is a suspect, not a conviction, since a device that forwards traffic can still drop or rate-limit ICMP addressed to itself. They land in five places. *The failed gateway row's details*, both languages — and only the `FAIL` row: a row that passed has nothing to qualify, a row whose verdict was withheld says why already, and a failed target that is not the gateway is not answering for itself. *The What to tell IT summary* for `gateway-unreachable`, a fourth line before the send-to-IT one, in the HTML and the text report alike. *The user manuals'* title table, whose *Gateway does not answer* cell is that summary's lines and now carries the fourth; the sentence above the table said *two to four lines*, which was already false for a healthy run with an optional target that did not answer and a row that could not be measured, and now says two to six — this release first wrote five, and round 1 below counted the sixth. *The IT deployment manuals'* section 7, beside the sentence that already sent IT to the field manual. And *the technical guides'* section 4.4, which states the rule and the decision. **The tests** (unit six per language, report-stage two): the token `echo`, which the method line carries once in both packages, counts twice on a failed gateway row — three lost of four, and four of four — once on the passing row and once on a failed row whose target is not the gateway, and the failed row is exactly one line longer than the passing one; the summary for a silent gateway has five lines and the summary for a gateway that answered has four.

**#64 — the guides' lead paragraph states its rule for the target it is true of.** All six copies: the paragraph that opened *Each ping row's details name the source address and the interface … before and after that target's probes* now opens with **a ping row whose target was given as an address**, hands a target given as a name to the paragraph under it — which was already correct and is untouched, as the item required — and names the three shapes of ping row that carry no route line at all, because nothing was sent: a configured value that cannot become a target, a placeholder that expands to nothing on this machine, and an attempt that threw, each by the message its row carries. No change to the tool, none to the name paragraph. `tests/doc_facts.ps1` still cannot see this and still says so about itself: it reads identifiers, and this was prose from one side only — #33's tier 2 and 3 territory, unchanged by this release.

**What the tests hold, and the mutants written before they were trusted.** Unit 564 × 2 → **575 × 2** before the review, **585 × 2** after round 2 and **587 × 2** after round 3, report-stage 165 × 2 → **167 × 2** before the review and **169 × 2** after round 1, the packaged validator 113 / 113 with its version and manifest checks over the bumped files, doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43 over the edited manuals — the new code spans (`Segments Sent/sec`, the counter names in the guides) are none of the identifier kinds it reads, and no file name a document quotes changed. **Four mutants of the en-US script, all caught:** the gateway row's sentence never appended (3 unit assertions fail), the counter's name dropped from the denominator sentence (2), the summary's fourth line removed (1 report-stage assertion), and the rate divided by sent plus retransmitted — the third road #57's decision found not to be there — (1, the 1.775 the sentence exists to pin).

**Review round 1 — two findings, both accepted, and both the mistake this release exists to remove: a sentence claiming more than the evidence behind it.** *(1) The forwarding inference was made on any passing row.* The failed gateway row's sentence and the summary's fourth line said that a target beyond the gateway which passed shows that it forwards — and `Get-FingerprintSummary` correlates nothing: the connection targets are configured independently, so a same-subnet host, a VPN or a proxy can succeed without touching the gateway, and on a multihomed machine `AUTO_GATEWAY` writes one row per gateway. The field manual's own entry, which this release claimed to derive from, states the condition — *at least one of them was off-subnet by a route through the gateway in the row you are reading* — and the derivation had dropped it. The row, the summary, the user manuals' cell and the IT manuals' section 7 now carry it: a passing target proves forwarding only if its route ran through this gateway. *(2) The documented maximum was still one short.* The user manual's *two to four lines* became *two to five* in this release, and six is reachable: the gateway summary's four lines, the sentence naming the rows that were not measured, and the send-to-IT line. It now says two to six, and a report-stage case pins that shape — a silent gateway beside a weightless row is six lines, and the weightless row moves the count and not the key — so the next time the summary grows, the number in the manual fails a test instead of waiting for a reviewer. Report-stage 167 × 2 → **169 × 2**; the unit cases are untouched, because the `echo` token the #58 assertions count is still in the sentence once.

**Review round 2 — two findings, both accepted, and one of them raised an item.** *(1) The failed gateway row said the gateway did not answer, whichever way it had failed.* A required gateway row fails two ways — replies that did not come back, and replies that came back at or above `LatencyCriticalMs` with the loss verdict passing or withheld — and the new sentence, gated on `FAIL` alone, wrote *did not answer* on a row reading *0% loss (4/4 successful), average 300 ms*. The sentence is now chosen by cause: a row that lost replies names how many of how many, a row that answered slowly names the average and says that a device answers pings sent to itself from its control plane, at a lower priority than the traffic it forwards; the summary's fourth line says *not answering, or answering slowly*, and the user manuals' cell follows it. **What the finding also pointed at predates this release and is not its to fix**: the summary's title and first line — *Gateway does not answer*, *does not answer pings* — are selected from the row's status alone and have been since the fingerprint of 1.2.3, so a slow gateway has always been titled as a silent one. Changing that is a change to a key the manuals, the field manual and scenario D all name, and it is open item **#67**; the IT manuals' section 7 and the guides' section 4.4 say so where they name the title. Five unit assertions per language: a gateway that answered everything slowly fails, carries the sentence, names its average on the last line, the row that lost three of four names those counts on its own, and the two sentences are not the same sentence. *(2) A window in which nothing was counted as sent printed 0% and then said what the percentage divided by.* `$rate` stayed 0 when the division was skipped, the details printed it as a rate, and the new sentence described a division that had not happened — and under the counter semantics this release documents, a window with retransmissions and no segment counted as sent is possible, because the sent counter excludes segments carrying only previously sent bytes. Such a row now says the rate is not computable and why, names the retransmissions it did count, and carries no denominator sentence; the small-sample rule reports it as it always did. Six unit assertions per language over the 0 / 0 row and a 0-sent, 2-retransmitted one; the guides' rule 3 states the case. Unit 575 × 2 → **585 × 2**. **Two more mutants, both caught:** the cause split removed, so the slow row says *did not answer* again (1 assertion), and the zero-sent branch removed, so 0% and the denominator sentence come back on a window that sent nothing (4).

**Review round 3 — one finding, accepted, and it is round 2's second finding one field further out.** The zero-sent row's *details* no longer printed 0%, and its *message* — the line the report shows first — still did: the small-sample branch says *approximately N%* for any sample under the rating floor that carried a retransmission, and a window with nothing counted as sent reaches that branch. The message now says the retransmissions were observed and that no rate can be computed from them, in both languages, with the phrase the user manual quotes kept intact; two unit assertions per language read the message rather than the details, which is where round 2's assertions had stopped. Unit 585 × 2 → **587 × 2**. A mutant that leaves the message as it was fails the first of the two.

**Review round 4 — a clean pass on `3ae0a2b`.** Four rounds, five findings, every one accepted and none rejected; one of them, round 2's first, also raised open item #67 for the half that predates this release. Every finding was the same shape, and it is the shape this release exists to remove: a sentence claiming more than the evidence behind it — a forwarding inference made on any passing row, a documented maximum one short of what the code produces, a *did not answer* written on a row that had answered slowly, a ratio printed as 0% where nothing had been divided, and the same 0% again one field further out. Three of the five were fixes needing the round after them, which is 1.2.10's pattern again: a change to a branch is not finished until every sentence that branch carries — and every field the row prints — has been read again.

**Review round 5 — scoped to the record commit; one finding, accepted: the recorded `-WorkDir` had lost its backslash.** The path was written through a shell that collapses a doubled backslash into one, so the recorded path reached the file split at its separator — the variable, then a line break — a command a reader would copy and have fail, in the one paragraph whose point is which directory the chain ran from. Restored; nothing else in that commit changed.

**Review round 6 — scoped to the one-line commit; one finding, accepted: the restored path was written in the wrong shell's syntax.** `%TEMP%` is what cmd expands; the chain is a PowerShell script that resolves its work directory itself (`$env:TEMP`, in its own default), so a reader pasting the recorded command into PowerShell would have created a folder literally named `%TEMP%` under the current directory — a long path again, which is the failure the paragraph exists to explain away. The invocation now reads `$env:TEMP\nhc-1211-final`, with the directory it resolved to beside it, and the round-5 sentence above no longer quotes the old form; those two edits and this paragraph were the whole of that commit, which touched the record alone. Two rounds on one path, and the lesson is the manuals': the record is a document people copy commands from, and a command in it gets the same reading a command in a manual gets.

**Review round 7 — scoped to the record commit; one finding, accepted: the record had described a commit before it existed.** Round 5's paragraph promised that its sentence would be the only change in the next commit, and the next commit carried the round-6 paragraph and the rewritten invocation as well — a forward-looking claim in a file whose job is to say what happened. The promise is gone, and the round-6 paragraph now accounts for everything its commit changed. This round's fix touches the record alone: that sentence, that paragraph, and this one.

**Review round 8 — a clean pass on `68d81e5`, and the loop's end.** Eight rounds, eight findings, all accepted and none rejected: five on the tool and the documents in rounds 1 to 3, and three on this record's own sentences in rounds 5 to 7 — a separator lost to a shell, a path in the wrong shell's syntax, and a claim about a commit that did not yet exist. Every commit on the branch had a round scoped to it; the one that records this clean pass is the last, and is unreviewed by design, as 1.2.10's was.

**Chain on the tree before the review (Windows 11 10.0.26200, Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, Python 3.14.4), `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Package -RequireHealthy`: 33 / 34 in 806 s, and the one failure is the work directory's, not the tree's.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 113 / 113; guards OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43; unit **575 × 2**; report-stage **167 × 2**; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; headless GUI 4 / 4 with the IT panel's 24 fixed-size controls in their boxes and its three spinners at 4 / 21 / 8 against their configured maxima; the four real-window `gui` runs all *Overall Healthy* under `-RequireHealthy`, the two IT ones over the configured 30 probes and 125-second sample; five acceptance runs, all *Overall Healthy*; result-set 79 / 79; and the package built from the tracked files, **583 780 bytes**, SHA256 `37238386dc830800de5021dbc8da718514ed75a39914cd6e0fdeed9bc2d3b59b`, the validator 113 / 113 from inside the extracted package and its four entries opened as real windows. As in 1.2.8 to 1.2.10 those package figures are the step's own: this record is a tracked file and this paragraph changed the asset's bytes after it was built, which is why a release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit. **The failure was `campaign`, and it is a path-length failure of the harness, not of the change.** The chain was given a work directory under this session's scratchpad rather than its default `%TEMP%\nhc-tests\<timestamp>`, 83 characters deeper, and the campaign self-test's deepest fixture — case 26's crafted Windows 11 compressed-folder view, `<guid>_NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.11.zip.bc4\NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.11\en-US\` — already ran to 263 characters — past the 260-character limit — before the launcher's own file name, so `Copy-Item` reported *could not find a part of the path* for a folder it had just created; a second attempt from the same root failed at case 1 instead, `Expand-Archive` losing an extracted manual under a path of 264 characters. **Re-run from a short directory the step is 132 / 132 in 115 s** — the same self-test, the same tree, no file changed between the two. The chain's default work directory is short for a reason, and this is the paragraph that says why.

**What the runs proved, and what only the tests did.** Every TCP retransmission row of every report in the chain — both languages, both protocols, the eight-second and the 125-second samples — carries the new line naming `Segments Sent/sec`, under the same *Approximate retransmission rate* the row always printed. No run produced a failed gateway row: the reference machine's gateway answered every probe in every run, so the sentence #58 puts on that row, and the fourth line it puts in the summary, have been seen only in the unit and report-stage cases and in the mutants that remove them. They want a machine whose gateway does not answer, which no scenario of the acceptance campaign constructs; a run on such a machine is the evidence this paragraph does not have.

**Chain on the tree that ships, from a short work directory: 34 / 34 in 862 s, clean at the first attempt.** `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Package -RequireHealthy -WorkDir $env:TEMP\nhc-1211-final` — the machine's own temp directory, which resolved to `C:\Users\m3575\AppData\Local\Temp\nhc-1211-final` — on `3ae0a2b` with the round-4 paragraph above already in the record. Every figure is that run's own: parser 0 × 2; validator 113 / 113; guards OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43; unit **587 × 2**; report-stage **169 × 2**; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test **132 / 132** in 116 s — the step the first run lost to the long path, passing here for the reason the re-run passed; headless GUI 4 / 4 with the IT panel's 24 fixed-size controls in their boxes; the four real-window `gui` runs all *Overall Healthy* under `-RequireHealthy`, the two IT ones over 30 probes and the 125-second sample; five acceptance runs, all *Overall Healthy*; result-set 79 / 79; and the package built from the tracked files, **591 128 bytes**, SHA256 `fd7c548ea6eb5701a66baf858bdc98500cf2d1eb646d5aaf63c80eceb5b16b8a`, the validator 113 / 113 from inside the extracted package and its four entries opened as real windows. This paragraph was written after that build, so the released asset is CI's build of the tagged commit, as always.

**What that run's 19 reports show, read rather than assumed.** Of the 26 rows that print a retransmission rate line, 13 carry the denominator sentence and 13 do not — and the 13 that do not are every run's **TCPv6** row, a window in which nothing was counted as sent on this machine, each printing *not computable* under the *not enough TCP send traffic* message. That is round 2's branch exercised by every run of the chain, in both languages, where the first chain's reports had printed 0% and the sentence on the same rows. No row had retransmissions with nothing counted as sent, so the message round 3 corrected is still proved by its unit case and its mutant alone; and no run produced a failed gateway row, so #58's sentences are too. Both want a machine that produces those rows.

## v1.2.10 — a quality verdict may not rest on a single packet · 2026-09-11 — backlog #51, #63

**What and why.** Two items of the same shape, and the second one could not ship without the first because both change `Compare-TcpCounters`' classification. **#51:** with the settings as shipped a run sends four echo requests per ping target, so one lost reply is 25 % against a 20 % critical threshold — a verdict on a measurement of four packets. `MinimumTcpSegmentsForRate` has the same defect written into it: it is 50, which is exactly `100 ÷ TcpRetransmissionWarningPercent`, so at the smallest sample the tool will rate, **one retransmission is the warning threshold itself**. The guard that exists to stop the tool rating a sample too small permits precisely the least informative case it could. **#63:** `TcpRetransmissionCriticalCount` warned on its own whatever the rate was, and on this machine that turned 57 retransmissions in 3 832 sent segments — 1.487 %, below both of the tool's own thresholds — into *Attention Required*.

**Version.** 1.2.10 in both scripts and in the packaged validator (`TOOL_VERSION`, and `FUNCTION_COUNT` 97 → **108** for the eleven functions these two items add: `Get-PingCountForThreshold`, `Get-LossBand`, `Get-PingLossClassification`, `Get-PingExtensionPlan`, `Get-PingSampleInterval`, `Get-PingRouteAfter`, `Add-PingTargetResult`, `Complete-PingSamples`, `Test-TcpSampleNeedsExtension`, `Merge-TcpEndingSnapshot` and `Get-CountThreshold`, the last of them from round 1 of the review). `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated in the existing line order.

**`PingCount` becomes a starting count, and the first pass decides what follows.** Every reply arriving ends the sample, so a healthy run is no slower than it was. Nothing answering also ends it, because 100 % loss is conclusive at four probes and this is the one case where every extra probe costs a whole timeout — the expensive case is the one that needs the fewest probes. Some replies lost but not all is the ambiguous case, and only there does the sample continue: to the smallest *n* at which one lost reply is below the warning percentage **as the row prints it**, **21** at the shipped 5 %, or to the new `PingCountMaximum` where that is lower. Twenty is exactly 5 % and still warns, which is the same off-by-one-factor that put 50 in `MinimumTcpSegmentsForRate`, so the arithmetic lives in one function that both the plan and the classification ask.

**The extra probes are spread, not fired off together.** `Ping` here sends with no delay between echoes, so twenty of them land inside a fraction of a second and measure one instant twenty times, while an intermittently unstable connection needs span rather than count. They are sent immediately before `Wait-ForMinimumTcpSample` sleeps out the seconds the retransmission window still owes, so the span costs wall-clock time the run was going to spend anyway; where that budget is gone they go back to back and the run is longer by what they cost. The row is written **where it belongs** when the first pass ends and rewritten when the sample is finished, so the report's ping section is never left in two pieces and a run that ends early still reports what it measured.

**A verdict is withheld only where both halves of the defect are present.** The sample must be smaller than the count the warning threshold needs — one packet worth a whole band — **and** the classification must change if one fewer reply had been lost. Either alone leaves the verdict standing, which is what keeps this from being a blanket demotion: three lost of four is 75 % and two lost is still 50 %, so that row still fails a required target, while two lost of twenty-one turns on a packet but 9.5 % on twenty-one probes is a measurement. A target that answered nothing at all reaches the rule too and passes it at the shipped count, three lost of four being critical still; only a whole sample of one probe has that verdict withheld, which is round 5's finding. A withheld loss verdict hands the row to the latency rules rather than keeping it, because only the loss half is what #51 found too coarse.

**The retransmission half, in two parts.** A count floor, `MinimumTcpRetransmissionsForVerdict` (5), gates **both** branches — a floor on the warning branch alone would leave three retransmissions in fifty segments failing on the rate alone, which is the case #51's acceptance names. Its cost is bounded by its own arithmetic: suppression needs a rate at the threshold with fewer events than the floor, `(floor − 1) × 100 ÷ warning percentage`, so nothing above two hundred sent segments is ever suppressed — the sample size at which one retransmission is a quarter of the warning threshold. And the window is **extended once** where a sample ends below `MinimumTcpSegmentsForRate` with at least one retransmission in it, which is the only case a longer window can settle; an idle machine that retransmitted nothing is not extended, and the second reading is merged per protocol so a failed read cannot cost a reading the first one already had. The below-floor warning becomes Information: closed item #39 made it weightless in 1.2.8, so the badge already decided nothing and all it did was colour a row *attention* beside a sentence saying the row was not evidence.

**#63's implementation, and what it takes away.** The standalone count trigger in the `WARN` line goes; the conjunction in the `FAIL` line stays. The argument is the boundary `TcpRetransmissionCriticalCount ÷ TcpRetransmissionWarningPercent`, 2 500 sent segments at the shipped 50 and 2 %: below it, reaching fifty retransmissions means the rate is already at or above the warning threshold and the standalone trigger changed nothing; above it, all it added was a warning on a rate the tool's own threshold table calls healthy. **A TCP retransmission row that carried a `WARN` from the count alone now carries `PASS`, and a run whose only weighted warning was that row reports *Overall Healthy* where it reported *Attention Required*** — the second time this project has taken something away from the verdict, after closed item #39 in 1.2.8. What it gives up is the only signal that fired on a burst inside an otherwise healthy window; a total over the window cannot tell a burst from an even spread, and that loss is recorded as open item #65 rather than argued away. Every classified row now names the rule that decided it — the rate, or the rate and the count together — which the item asked for because a reader could not tell.

**The configuration surface, and the number that was never a limit.** `PingCountMaximum` (21) and `MinimumTcpRetransmissionsForVerdict` (5) join the file, its validation and the IT deployment manual; `-PingCountMaximum` joins the switches; the IT panel gains a third spinner. The two ping counts are an ordered pair checked like the threshold pairs, and a ceiling below the starting count is reported rather than silently clamping what was asked for. The panel's ping spinners opened at **20** until this release — the spinner's own default, with no reason anywhere in the repository or the package, while the configuration validated no upper bound at all and the code clamped only the lower one. The range is now the configured ceiling, so the three surfaces say one thing; v1.2.1's decision that a configured value **widens** the range rather than being clamped is unchanged.

**The tests, and the cases the acceptance names.** Unit 430 × 2 → **530 × 2**, 100 of them these two items'. The pure rules are tested directly — the count a threshold needs (21 at 5 %, and 20 is not enough), the band, the two-condition withholding rule, the extension plan's three cases, the spread's interval — and so is the row they produce, because a rule computed somewhere is not a verdict leaving a row. The three cases #51's acceptance names are there by their numbers: one retransmission in a small sample, several in a small sample, and **three in fifty segments — 6 %, which failed on the rate alone** — asserted on **status and marking** rather than on wording, since a change guarding the warning branch alone satisfies the first two and leaves the third. #63's two measured cases are there as measured: 57 of 3 832 and 482 of 27 594 are `PASS`. Report-stage 160 × 2 → **165 × 2** for the panel's third spinner and the run profile. Every assertion is language-neutral, which is the rule this file has: six of the new ones read English prose on their first draft and failed against the zh-TW package.

**Review round 1 — three findings, all accepted, and every one of them a number that looked right until the arithmetic was written out beside the code that consumes it.** That is this release's own thesis arriving one layer in. *(1) The count a threshold needs was computed exactly and applied to a rounded figure.* `Get-PingCountForThreshold` used `floor(100/w) + 1`, while the band is read off the figure the row prints, rounded to one decimal — so at a warning threshold of 4.8 % it returned 21, one reply of twenty-one is 4.7619 %, that prints as **4.8** and still warns, and `Sent == RequiredCount` meant the sample was no longer even considered coarse: a single packet producing a weighted warning, which is exactly what adaptive sampling exists to stop. It is not only a decimal threshold's problem — at the shipped **2 %** the exact arithmetic gives 51, and one of fifty-one prints as 2.0. The count is now stepped up until the printed figure really is below the threshold, and the question is put to `Get-LossBand`, so the rule tested there can never drift from the rule the row will use. *(2) A negative count threshold threw at the unsigned cast.* `[uint64]` of a negative value throws, and the configuration check reads a count threshold's type and its integrality, never its sign — so `TcpRetransmissionCriticalCount = -1` turned the whole retransmission analysis into an *Unable to Check* row rather than reporting the value. The finding was raised against the key this release adds; **two of the three call sites predate it**, and the defect is the cast, so one function does it for all three and a negative falls back to the built-in default, with the *Configuration Thresholds* row now naming it. *(3) An extension's failed reads were lent to windows that had already closed.* Where the extended read failed for one protocol, that protocol kept the first reading and the stamp that came with it, while every failed attempt of the later snapshot was merged in — so the row could print more seconds of failed reads as *inside* its window than the window is long. Whose window those seconds fall inside is not the same question for every protocol: the merge now marks them and names the protocols the extension actually closed, the note counts them only there, and the protocol that kept the first reading gets a line of its own saying what they were and that they fell outside — because backlog #38's promise is that every attempt which failed is kept with the seconds it spent.

**Three mutants were written before the round's tests were trusted**, and the second of them found a hole in the test file rather than in the product: the exact-arithmetic count fails four assertions, the raw unsigned cast fails on a throw, and the unfiltered window note claims nineteen seconds inside nine. The cast mutant **stopped the run where it stood** — no `Summary` line, exit 0 — which the chain's step rejects but a person running the file by hand would read as a stack of passes. A trap now ends that run with a failure and a summary, which is the same hole the file already guarded from the other direction. Unit 530 × 2 → **550 × 2**; validator 113 / 113, doc facts 96 / 96, report-stage 165 × 2, guards clean.

**Review round 2 — three findings, all accepted, and two of them are the first round's fixes not carried all the way through.** *(1) The coarse-sample sentence claimed what the row does, before the row knew.* It ended on *this row does not change the overall result*, and it was assembled in the loss branch — but a withheld loss verdict hands the row to the latency rules, and one lost reply of four at an average of 300 ms on a required target comes out a weighted `FAIL` with that sentence still on it. The consequence clause is now chosen after the row's status is, and says instead that what decides the row is its latency. **The finding's own example was wrong and the defect was not:** three of four is 75 %, two of four is still 50 %, so that row never reaches the withholding rule at all — one of four does. *(2) The extension sentence read a snapshot-wide flag.* `Merge-TcpEndingSnapshot` sets `Extended` once for the merged snapshot, so a protocol whose extended read failed — which keeps the first reading and the stamp that came with it — was told its window had been extended, and then told two lines later that its window had already closed. It is now the per-protocol `closedByExtension` that round 1 introduced for the window note. *(3) The documents still stated the exact arithmetic.* Round 1 changed the implementation from `floor(100/w) + 1` to the smallest count whose **printed** figure is below the threshold; the technical guide's section 4.4, all six copies, and the IT deployment manual's `PingCountMaximum` row, all four files, still gave the old formula — and an administrator following it at a calibrated 4.8 % would have set the ceiling to 21 and left exactly the case this release exists to remove. Both now describe the rounded comparison and say that the tool computes the count while the ceiling only caps it. The same sentence is corrected in this record and in the repository `README.md`.

**Two mutants again**, and both caught: a note that claims the row decides nothing whatever decided it, and the snapshot-wide flag put back. Unit 550 × 2 → **555 × 2**.

**Review round 3 — two findings, both accepted, and both the user manual.** *(1) The capped-sample sentence claimed more than the rule does.* It said that where `PingCountMaximum` stops a sample short the row *does not change the overall result*, which is true only of a loss classification that turns on one packet: three lost of ten is 30 % and two lost is still 20 %, so that row keeps its verdict, and one lost of four at an average of 300 ms fails a required target on its **latency**. The paragraph now says which case is withheld and gives both counter-examples by their numbers. *(2) The panel gained a control and the manual did not.* Section 7 listed the six fields it always had, so the walk's own W33 — rewritten in this release to say **seven** — was no longer a walk of what the manual claims, and the person in front of a panel with seven controls had six described. **Ping ceiling** is now named there with what it is for, in all four copies. The same omission was one paragraph further on and neither the finding nor the round comment had it: the report's own inventory in section 5 lists the run options the report carries, and the ping ceiling is one of them since this release, so it is named there too.

**Review round 5 — two findings, both accepted, the first of them a claim this release made and then leaned on.** *(1) "100 % loss is conclusive" is an argument about four probes, and the branch it justifies runs at one.* `PingCount` 1 is a value the configuration check and the IT panel both permit, and there *100 % loss* and *one lost packet* are the same event — so a required target was failed on a single timeout by the branch this release had just finished writing a rationale for. The fix invents no number and sends no extra probe: the silent branch now asks the same question the loss branch does, and the rule already answers it — at four probes the classification does **not** turn on one packet (three of four is still critical) and the verdict stands; at one it does, and the row reports its 100 % with no verdict. The sentence about an optional target that may simply be blocking ICMP was gated on the status, which was the same thing until a withheld silent verdict became Information as well; round 5 moved it to the case and **put it one branch too deep**, so a one-probe optional target lost the very guidance it most needs - round 6 caught that, and the sentence now belongs to the case alone, which is what it was always about: an optional target that answered nothing may be blocking ICMP whether or not its verdict was withheld. *(2) The technical guide's run-option inventory was still the 1.2.9 one.* Section 6 listed the switches without `-PingCountMaximum`, the JSON `RunOptions` fields without `PingCountMaximum`, and the IT panel's ping spinner as *1–20* — the number this release removed. All six copies now carry the two new fields and say the spinners open at the configured ceiling. **Every sentence that said a silent target never reaches the withholding rule went with it**: two script comments, six guide copies, four IT-manual files and this record, which is the same sweep round 2's third finding asked for and the reason it was cheap to do.

**Three mutants across the two rounds, all caught**: the silent branch without the rule (the one-probe row fails a required target again), the ICMP sentence gated on the status (it appears on a row that is not an optional target), and round 5's own gating put back (the one-probe optional row loses the sentence). Unit 555 × 2 → **564 × 2**. **Round 6 is one finding and it is round 5's**, which is the third time in this review that a fix needed the round after it - the pattern to read from that is that a change to a branch is not finished until every sentence that branch carries has been read again.

**The rounds that left no paragraph of their own: 4, 6 and 7.** Round 4 was a clean pass on `c461d09`; the commit after it raised backlog #66, and the round scoped to that commit is round 5 above. Round 6 was one finding and it was round 5's, folded into the paragraph above because that is where it belongs — the sentence about an optional target blocking ICMP, moved off the status correctly and put one branch too deep, so a one-probe optional target lost the guidance it most needs. Round 7 is a clean pass on `c68cc14`. **Seven rounds, eleven findings, all accepted, none rejected and none deferred** — and three of the eleven were a fix needing the round after it, which is the pattern this review leaves behind: a change to a branch is not finished until every sentence that branch carries has been read again.

**What the run's own length says, measured rather than argued.** #51's acceptance asks that a healthy run be no slower and that a run against a target that answers nothing end no later than before. Three console runs of each on the reference machine, the 1.2.9 tree against this one, `-ConsoleOnly` with the shipped configuration: **10.2, 9.9, 12.7 s (mean 10.9) against 10.1, 10.0, 10.3 s (mean 10.1)**; and with an extra ping target that answers nothing (`192.0.2.1`, traceroute off), **10.1, 10.6, 10.4 s (mean 10.4) against 9.9, 9.9, 10.1 s (mean 10.0)**. Neither is slower, and the mechanism is why: both cases end the sample in `Get-PingExtensionPlan` — `complete` and `silent` — so nothing extra is sent in either.

**Chain three times** (Windows 11 10.0.26200, Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, Python 3.14.4). Twice clean at the first attempt — `d078200` before the review, **34 / 34 in 824 s**, and `c461d09` after four rounds, **34 / 34 in 791 s** — and once on the tree that ships, `c68cc14`, **32 / 34 in 836 s**, whose two failures are `-RequireHealthy` refusing a verdict the tool was right to give. Every other figure below is that third run's. Parser 0 × 2; validator 113 / 113; guards OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43; unit **564 × 2**; report-stage **165 × 2**; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 132 / 132; headless GUI 4 / 4 — the IT panel measuring **24** fixed-size controls against their boxes where it measured 22, with `PingCount=4/max 21, PingCountMaximum=21/max 21, SampleSeconds=8/max 120`; five acceptance runs, all *Overall Healthy*; result-set 79 / 79; and the package built from the tracked files, **561 519 bytes**, SHA256 `040d6d9682dd35e1835764237f31463b69095d363d6b388f83c7f81f159e36cc`, the validator 113 / 113 from inside the extracted package and its four entries opened as real windows. As in 1.2.8 and 1.2.9 those package figures are the step's own: this record is a tracked file, so the sentence you are reading changed the asset's bytes after it was built, which is why a release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit.

**What failed, and why it is the tool working.** The reference machine's Wi-Fi was losing packets through the last hour of this work, and `-RequireHealthy` — whose own help says *on a machine with a real network problem the warning is the tool doing its job* — turns each such run into a failed step. The third chain's two: a `gui` run measuring **6.7 % and 10 % loss over 30 probes**, and a `package` run measuring **14.3 % over 21**. The permitted re-run of those two steps put `package` at **5 / 5** and left `gui` at 1 / 4, its three failures being 6.7 % loss on both targets with a **2.213 %** retransmission rate over 723 sent segments, a **3.819 %** rate over 707, and the 14.3 % again. Run without `-RequireHealthy`, the `gui` step is **4 / 4** — every other assertion it makes holds — with two of its runs still reporting quality: a 2.105 % rate over 665 segments, and **46 retransmissions in 747 sent segments, 6.158 %**, a `FAIL` from the rate alone whose row names the rate as what decided it. Sixteen, twenty-seven and forty-six events are all far above the new floor of five, so none of these is the floor letting something through: they are a degraded link, measured.

**And the branch that had never run on a real machine ran twice, which closes the item raised for it.** Backlog #66 was raised an hour earlier because both of the first two chains took `complete` or `silent` every time; it is closed by this release, and the evidence is this paragraph. The third took `extend`: the gateway's first four probes lost one, seventeen more were sent, and the row reports **18 / 21 — 14.3 %**, with the attempts numbered 1 to 21, *Adaptive sample: the first 4 echo requests lost 1 of their replies, so 17 more were sent, spread across the rest of the run instead of back to back*, a method line saying 21 ICMP echo requests and a manual check saying `ping -n 21`. `1.1.1.1` did the same in another run, 18 / 21. **Two of the three timeouts fell inside the spread** — attempts 11 and 14 — which is the case the spreading exists for: four back-to-back probes would have reported 25 % on one packet or nothing at all, and twenty-one across the run reported 14.3 % on a sample the threshold fits. The row sat at index 13 of the result set, between the company-standard rows and the DNS rows and immediately before the other ping row, so the section was not left in two pieces. **It cost nothing measurable:** that step took 24.9 s against 24.6 s and 24.9 s for the same step in the two chains that lost nothing.

**The run before it failed, and the step that failed it is the point.** The two spinner tooltips had been written beside the four text-field ones — which run **before** the loop that creates the spinners — so `SetToolTip` was handed `$null` and threw, and `Initialize-Gui`'s own catch would have sent the IT entry silently to console mode. The headless GUI step caught it in **both** languages, which is the failure mode that step exists for and the one it was written for in 1.2.0.

**What the chain proved, and what it did not.** The count floor fired for real, twice in each chain, in both languages: the unreachable-targets acceptance runs measured **2 retransmissions in 67 sent segments — 2.985 %** and **2 in 93 — 2.151 %** in the first chain, and **2 in 62 — 3.226 %** and **2 in 70 — 2.857 %** in the second, all four above the shipped 2 % warning threshold and all four now *Information* carrying their numbers and deciding nothing. Before this release each was a weighted `WARN` and each run read *Attention Required*; both read *Overall Healthy* here. That is #51's case, on this machine, in the shipped configuration. **What no run entered is the extension itself:** every ping target answered every probe (`complete`) and the unreachable one answered none (`silent`), so no sample was continued, and no TCP window was extended because no sample ended below the rating floor with a retransmission in it. Those branches have their unit cases — the plan's three outcomes, the interval, the row that is rewritten rather than added — and, like 1.2.9's changed-route branch, they want a machine that is actually losing replies, which this one is not. An attempt to force partial loss by shrinking `PingTimeoutMs` failed for a stated reason and is recorded rather than dropped: `Test-PingTargets` clamps the timeout to a floor of 250 ms, and every reply here arrives in 6.

## v1.2.9 — every ping row says which adapter the route table picked for it · 2026-09-11 — backlog #59

**What and why.** One item, and it is the cheapest half of a question this project could not answer at all: on a machine with a wired and a wireless adapter both up — a docked laptop, which is exactly the machine most likely to be in trouble — nothing in the report said which of the two a ping row had measured. `Invoke-PingMeasurement` recorded loss, latency and one line per attempt with the *reply from* address, and never the **source**. So a gateway row could not be placed, and neither could the person reading it.

**Version.** 1.2.9 in both scripts and in the packaged validator (`TOOL_VERSION`, and `FUNCTION_COUNT` 93 → **96** for the three functions this release adds: `Get-RouteSelection`, `Get-RouteSelectionText` and `Format-RouteSelection`). `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated in the existing line order.

**What the row says, and what it refuses to say.** Each ping row's details now name the source address and the interface the route table selects for that target, read with `Find-NetRoute -RemoteIPAddress <target>`. It is a **selection, not an observation**, and the wording carries that: the lookup answers what the system would choose at the moment it is asked, while `Invoke-PingMeasurement` sends its probes unbound, so the row never claims the echoes used what the lookup returned. Binding a source would make it an observation and needs `ping.exe -S`, which backlog #60 carries and this item does not.

**Twice, so that a change is measured rather than caveated.** The lookup runs before and after each target's probes — the same before-and-after shape the adapter counters already use. When the two disagree the row reports the change and states that it cannot say which of them carried the probes; and a disagreement includes one lookup resolving where the other did not, because that is still a change in which adapter was in play.

**What it cost, measured on the reference machine before any of it was written.** `Find-NetRoute -RemoteIPAddress` takes **about 13 ms** once the CIM subsystem is warm (`1.1.1.1`, `192.168.1.1`, `8.8.8.8` and `203.0.113.7`, second pass each: 14.2, 14.0, 13.5, 12.7 ms). The first call of a session cost **364 ms**, and an earlier cold one 1 078 ms — CIM warm-up, which a run has already paid by the time it reaches the ping checks, since the adapter inventory runs first. Two lookups per target against the shipped two targets is therefore about 50 ms on a run the manuals describe as *about ten seconds*. No traffic is sent and no new target is contacted.

**What the cmdlet actually returns, which is not what the item assumed.** Two objects, not one: an `MSFT_NetIPAddress` carrying the source and the interface, and an `MSFT_NetRoute` carrying the next hop, with the address empty on the second. The source is therefore read from **whichever object has an `IPAddress`**, never from the first, because the order is the cmdlet's to choose; the unit case that proves it feeds the two back in the opposite order and fails a selector that trusts position. `Find-NetRoute`'s documentation (checked 2026-09-11) describes a lookup, groups it with `Get-NetRoute` rather than with `New-`/`Set-`/`Remove-NetRoute`, and states no elevation requirement; it ran unelevated here.

**Absence is the datum being unavailable, never the row failing.** The cmdlet missing, the route table returning nothing, and the lookup throwing are three separate reasons and each says which one it is — closed item #5's shape, and what #32's acceptance asks of a different fact. The ping measurement itself never depends on the lookup: a row whose route selection is unavailable still reports its loss and latency.

**The tests, and the mutants they were checked against.** 25 assertions in each language inside the existing `unit` step, 371 × 2 → **396 × 2**, stubbing `Find-NetRoute` itself so that what runs above the stub is the shipped selector and the shipped formatter, and stubbing `Get-Command` for the fourth shape before removing it again, because two other loaded helpers ask `Get-Command` for their own names. Every assertion is language-independent by design — the selector returns data rather than sentences, and the formatter is checked on the values it interpolates — which is the rule the error-cause cases already followed. **Two mutants were written before the tests were trusted:** a selector that takes the first object instead of the one carrying an address fails *source is read from the object that has one*, and a formatter that never reports a change fails *changed names the address after*, *changed names both interfaces* and, after one more assertion was added for it, *resolved then unresolved names both readings* — that last rule is the one this release invented rather than inherited, so it needed its own mutant.

**The documents.** The technical guide's section 4.4 states the rule and what it does not claim, in both languages and all three copies; the user manual's report inventory names the field with the same qualification, in both languages and in the HTML beside the markdown. The IT deployment manual is not touched, because its section 7 delegates the report's inventory to the user manual's section 5 rather than repeating it.

**Chain six times, once per tree a review round left behind. Runs 1 to 3 were 34 / 34 (866 s, 855 s, 874 s); run 4 was 33 / 34 and its one permitted re-run failed a different case; runs 5 and 6 are 34 / 34 again (905 s, 936 s); run 7, on the tree that ships, failed four `gui` cases whose numbers are below, and its one permitted re-run of that step passed 4 / 4. The two failures are kept below with their numbers, because what they measured was real** (Windows 11 10.0.26200, Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, Python 3.14.4) — clean at the first attempt, which the two releases before this one were not. Parser 0 × 2; validator 113 / 113; guard self-test OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43; unit **430 × 2**, 58 of them this release's; report-stage 160 × 2; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 132 / 132; result-set 79; headless GUI 4 / 4; the four real-window runs with the window titled 1.2.9; five acceptance runs, all *Overall Healthy*; and the package built from the tracked files: `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.9.zip`, 503 051 bytes, SHA256 `2283e88dd18cdc10a12eb64344b7c0ba532d53f3bf4b8b9ff223ade7a67183ef` on the sixth, the validator 113 / 113 from inside the extracted package, and its four entries opened as real windows. As in 1.2.8 those package figures are the step's own: this record is a tracked file, so the sentence you are reading changed the asset's bytes after it was built, which is why a release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit.

**And the run proved the feature rather than only the tests.** Every ping row in every report of that chain carries a route line — 2 to 6 of them per report, in both languages. The **unavailable** branch was exercised by a real run rather than by a stub: in the two unreachable-target acceptance runs the extra ping target `nhc-no-such-host.invalid` resolves to nothing, so the route table has no route for it, and the row reads *Route selection: unavailable (the route table returned no route for this target). This row cannot say which adapter the probes left by.* beside its own measurement of 100 % loss — the datum unavailable, the measurement unaffected, which is what closed item #5's shape asks for — and after round 1 that row's reason is the true one, *this target is a name and nothing replied, so no address was available to look up*, where the first run had called it a missing route. On this machine every resolved lookup named `Wi-Fi` with source 192.168.1.106 and no lookup disagreed with its pair, so **the changed branch has not yet been seen on a real machine**; it is covered by the unit cases and wants a docked laptop, which is the machine this item was written for and one this project does not have.

**Review round 1, two findings, both accepted, and both about what an empty lookup is allowed to mean.** The first: a ping target may be a name, `Test-PingTargetSyntax` accepts one and `.NET Ping` resolves it, but `Find-NetRoute` takes an address — so a name came back empty and the row said *the route table returned no route for this target*, which is false. **The chain run above had already published that sentence** for the extra target `nhc-no-such-host.invalid`, and nobody had read it as a defect. The second: the cmdlet reports its failures as NON-terminating errors, so `-ErrorAction SilentlyContinue` sent every one of them down the same empty-result path and the `error` branch was unreachable.

**The measurement that settled both, on the reference machine, 2026-09-11.** `Find-NetRoute` distinguishes them by error identifier, and the identifier is the discriminator rather than the message, because the message follows the machine's locale — which is the rule backlog #27 settled for CIM errors, applied where it applies again. `www.microsoft.com` gives **Windows System Error 87** (invalid parameter, category InvalidArgument); `169.254.99.99` gives **Windows System Error 1231** (the network location cannot be reached). So an unroutable address, a value that is not an address, and a failure of the lookup itself are now three reasons that each say which one they are, with a fourth for a name whose probes got no reply at all.

**What was rejected in the fix, and why.** Resolving the name inside the tool — the shape round 1 suggested — would put the ping check behind the resolver, on a machine whose resolver is very often the thing being diagnosed, and would pay a DNS timeout twice for a name that does not resolve. The address the **replies came from** costs nothing and is the address the measurement actually used, so `Invoke-PingMeasurement` now returns it and a name target is looked up by it, after the probes. The price is stated in the row rather than hidden: for a name there is no address to ask about before the probes, so no before-and-after pair is taken and the row says so.

**Measured on this machine after the fix**, one run with `-PingTarget www.microsoft.com`: the two address targets read *source 192.168.1.106 via Wi-Fi — looked up before and after the probes*, and the name target reads *source 192.168.1.106 via Wi-Fi, looked up for 23.39.61.232 after the probes — this target is a name, so there was no address to ask about before them and no before-and-after pair was taken.* Before the fix that row would have claimed there was no route to it. Unit assertions 397 × 2 → **406 × 2**, the nine new ones covering the three error identifiers, the empty-and-silent case, and the name shape in the formatter.

**Review round 2, one finding, accepted, and it is the first round's fix leaving a contradiction one line below itself.** The Method line still said the selection came from `Find-NetRoute -RemoteIPAddress <the name>`, *read before and after the probes* — the address case's account — so every successful name row carried a correct route sentence followed immediately by a false description of how it had been obtained. The line now describes the lookup that happened, and it is a function (`Get-RouteMethodText`) rather than an inline string so the unit step can hold it to that: `FUNCTION_COUNT` 96 → **97**, assertions 406 × 2 → **411 × 2**.

**Measured, one console run with `-PingTarget www.microsoft.com`:** the two address rows read *… -RemoteIPAddress 192.168.1.1, read before and after the probes* and *… 1.1.1.1, read before and after the probes*; the name row reads *… -RemoteIPAddress 23.39.61.232, the address the replies came from, read once after the probes.* A name nothing answered claims no lookup at all rather than naming one.

**Review round 3, one finding, accepted, and it is about a case this machine could not produce.** .NET resolves a name on every send, so a name behind round-robin DNS, or one whose TTL expires mid-run, can answer from more than one address — and the row named the first as *the address the replies came from*, which is a claim about all of them, while the attempt lines above it could show a different one. **Not reproducible here:** eight sends to `www.microsoft.com` and six to `outlook.office365.com` each answered from a single address, the resolver cache holding it for its TTL. So the handling is written and pinned by unit cases rather than by a run.

**What the row does now.** Every distinct address that replied is kept, in the order they first answered, and each is looked up. Where they all select the same source and interface the row says so and gives the count; where they do not, it names each address with its own selection and states that it cannot attribute the measurement to one adapter — which is the honest end of this item, since two addresses through two adapters is exactly the case it was written for. The Method line names no single address once there is more than one. Assertions 411 × 2 → **418 × 2**; a console run with `-PingTarget www.microsoft.com` still reads *source 192.168.1.106 via Wi-Fi, looked up for 23.39.61.232 after the probes* for the one address it got.

**What this machine's network cost the fourth run, recorded rather than excused — and between the two attempts it demonstrated both of the open items this release's group is about.** Run 4 (830 s) failed one case: the en-US IT window, whose 125-second sample measured **8961 segments sent and 186 retransmitted, 2.076 %** against a 2 % warning threshold, with the critical count of 50 also passed. That is **not** [#51](../docs/backlog.md)'s shape — 8961 segments is a large sample and the link really was retransmitting at that rate for those two minutes. It is [#56](../docs/backlog.md)'s: a verdict decided by a number nobody can source. The `gui` step was re-run once, which is the whole allowance, and it failed a **different** case: the zh-TW user window's eight-second sample measured **70 segments sent and 2 retransmitted, 2.857 %**, which is #51's shape exactly — a verdict resting on two packets, in a sample barely above the 50 the tool will rate at all. Neither failure touches what this release changed: both are TCP retransmission rows, and #59 changed the ping rows. No third attempt was made, because shopping for a green run is how a record stops meaning anything; every other case passed in both runs, including all the deterministic steps, the five acceptance runs, the package and its validator.

**Review round 4, one finding, accepted, and it is round 3's fix leaving the same kind of gap round 2's did.** Where the first address's lookup came back unavailable, the formatter returned that one sentence and never read the others — so a usable adapter behind a second address stayed invisible, while the Method line went on claiming every address had been read. The set is now built before anything returns: whether the first address resolved decides the **wording**, never whether the rest are consulted. Four shapes come out of it — one address; several that agree; several that all fail the same way, said once with a count; and several that differ, named one by one with the row stating it cannot attribute the measurement to a single adapter. Assertions 418 × 2 → **422 × 2**.

**Review round 5, one finding, accepted, and it is the same fault as all the others in its fifth costume: claiming more than the data supports.** Where one address resolved and another's lookup failed, the row said *the route table does not treat them alike* — but a lookup that did not answer is not a decision the route table made, and a transient provider failure was being published as a conclusion about the network. Only where **every** address answered is the difference between them a routing difference; where one did not, what differs is the lookups, and the row now says that instead. Assertions 422 × 2 → **426 × 2**.

**The shape of this whole review, which is worth more than any single round.** Rounds 2 to 5 each landed on the round before's fix and never on the original change: the name case was fixed, then the Method line still described the address case; then one address was named for many; then the many were skipped when the first failed; then a failed lookup was called a routing difference. Every fix opened the next input shape. What they have in common is not the ping code — it is a sentence that has to be true for a reader, written across several input shapes and two languages, where each shape's wording is a claim of its own.

**Review round 6, one finding, accepted, and it lands on this item's own subject.** The comparison that decided whether the addresses agreed was made on the whole rendered sentence, so two addresses reaching the **same interface** from different source addresses — an IPv4 and an IPv6 reply over one adapter, which is the ordinary way it happens — produced *this row cannot attribute the measurement to one adapter*, when every lookup had selected the same adapter. The question this row exists to answer is which adapter, so that is what the comparison is on now: differing sources through one interface are reported as what they are, and the adapter is stated rather than disclaimed. Assertions 426 × 2 → **430 × 2**.

**Chain run 7 failed four cases, and what it found is a defect in the shipped verdict rather than in this release.** All four were `gui` runs with the fingerprint `quality`, and the numbers are the point: en-US User measured **3832 segments sent, 57 retransmitted, 1.487 %** over a window of **10.1 seconds** (configured minimum 8), and en-US IT measured **27 594 sent, 482 retransmitted, 1.747 %** over **128.6 seconds** (configured minimum 125). Both rates are **below** the tool's own 2 % warning threshold — by its own rule the link was fine — and both rows warned anyway, because `Compare-TcpCounters` reads `elseif ($rate -ge $warningPercent -or $retransDelta -ge $criticalCount)`: the absolute count of 50 triggers a warning on its own, with no reference to how many segments were sent.

**The re-run bounds the claim, and it is kept because it does.** The `gui` step re-run, the one permitted attempt, passed 4 / 4 — en-US User measured 139 sent and 0 retransmitted over **8.8 seconds**, en-US IT 947 sent and 1 over **128.0 seconds** — the durations are given because the configured value is a minimum and a comparison between the two pairs has to normalise by what was actually measured. Same machine, same configuration, **29 times less traffic in the same 125-second window**. So the general statement *a busy machine will always exceed 50* is **not** established by anything here: what is established is that on this machine, in the busy run, the verdict tracked how much the machine was sending rather than how well the link was carrying it, while the rate the tool itself computes said the link was healthy.

**Why this is neither [#51](../docs/backlog.md) nor [#56](../docs/backlog.md).** #51 is a sample too small for the threshold applied to it; this is the opposite end — samples large enough that a fixed count stops meaning anything. #56 is that the twelve thresholds have no recorded basis; this is a threshold whose **shape** is wrong for the quantity it guards, which would still be true if someone sourced the number 50. The tool's own sample sizes span 70 to 27 594 segments across its shipped configurations, a factor of nearly 400, and one absolute count is applied across all of it. It wants an item of its own, which this release does not open because a tool change and the backlog page are separate pull requests here.

**A trap the bump walked into again, recorded so the next one does not.** The packaged validator holds five files — the package README and the four manuals — to the rule that *every* version-shaped token in them equals `TOOL_VERSION`. So a manual sentence that names the release a field arrived in cannot survive a bump: the IT deployment manual's `Weightless (added in 1.2.8, additive under schema 2)` had to lose its version rather than be silently moved to 1.2.9, which is exactly the failure PR #40's round 7 caught in three sentences at once. The release it named survives in the technical guide, which the validator holds only to its first line.

## v1.2.8 — the counter read gets a second attempt, and the window says what it cost · 2026-09-09 — backlog #38

**What and why.** One item, and the walk of 2026-09-08 found it on a machine: the TCP retransmission counter read fails intermittently where nothing is wrong, and a read that failed took the measurement with it. It also took the run's length — 25.9 seconds against 11.7 for the same work minutes later — and it reported the seconds it had spent inside the *other* protocol's sample window: 13.3 against a configured 8, with no word about why. The verdict half of the item went to #39, which took quality rows out of the verdict; what was left is the measurement, the window, and the run. **#38**, whose first acceptance clause stays open on a machine that reproduces the failure.

**Version.** 1.2.8 in both scripts, the packaged validator (`TOOL_VERSION`, and `FUNCTION_COUNT` 86 → **89** for the three functions this release adds: `Get-TcpReadFailureLines`, and the `Format-TcpAttemptList` / `Get-TcpAttemptSeconds` pair that round 1 of the review below made one place each), `README_BILINGUAL.md`, the four manuals in both formats, the six guide copies and the repository README; `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated over the same 28 files, 18 of which changed. The guides' history sentences gain one for 1.2.8.

**The second attempt, and where it is not.** `Get-CimOrWmiInstance` — the one place every CIM/WMI query of this tool passes through — takes `-Attempts` and a list to record failures in. The two counter reads of a snapshot ask for **two**; nothing else in the tool asks for more than one, and a read that fails twice is not attempted a third time. Its row is the row it always was: same status, same check name, same message, because a retry that hides a real failure is worse than no retry. No attempt is classified as a timeout or anything else — a read that fails instantly for a permission or a missing class is attempted again too, at no cost, where classifying it would mean reading a message the operating system writes in its own language, which is the lesson of #14. **The three queries that do not pass through the helper stay as they are, decided rather than discovered:** `Win32_OperatingSystem` in `Get-SystemSummary` and the `Win32_NetworkAdapterConfiguration` / `Win32_NetworkAdapter` pair of the CIM fallback are not `Win32_PerfRawData_*` reads, no failure of theirs is on record, each already sits on a path that handles its own failure, and a second attempt would double their worst case on no evidence.

**The read before the window.** `Get-TcpCounterSnapshot -WarmUp`, on the baseline snapshot only: one throwaway read per class, in a pass of its own before either counter is read, its reading discarded, its failure neither an error nor a measured attempt. The separate pass is what round 3 of the review below established: interleaved with the measured reads, TCPv6's warm-up fell inside TCPv4's window, and a warm-up that is merely slow — the start-up cost this feature exists to absorb — would have lengthened it with no failure to explain the seconds. The ending snapshot takes none, because inside the window such a read would lengthen the very thing it is there to protect. The warm-up's failures are still recorded, kept apart from the measured ones and shown in the row of the protocol they belong to: whether the first read of a session is the one that fails is the question the item leaves open, and throwing that evidence away is what #47, #48 and #53 are all about.

**The trace, without which the retry could not be explained.** Every attempt that failed is kept with the seconds it spent, *even where a later attempt succeeded*. That was the gap the item named: a read that timed out and then worked would have handed back a clean counter and no record, and the row for the other protocol would have had nothing to say about why its sample ran long. A row for a counter that could not be read now names the attempts behind it; a row whose window carried a read that **failed** names those seconds and those reads — and only those: a read that was merely slow, or the checks between the two samples, lengthen a window without a line of their own, which is why the row prints the duration it truly measured rather than an accounting of it (round 13). A row that says a counter could not be read carries that protocol's failed reads from **both** snapshots when the other snapshot wrote no row of its own for it, because a protocol without a reading has no quality row and that row is the only other place they are named (PR #40, round 4); each line says which snapshot it belongs to, so a baseline failure can never read as an ending one.

**Three ways the guarantee still leaked, all found in review round 5 and all closed.** A query that returns **nothing**, or an instance without the counter fields, is a read that failed as surely as one that threw — and that check sat outside the retry loop, so a timeout got a second attempt and an empty answer did not. It is inside the attempt now (`-RequireProperty`), which the pre-window read does not ask for, its reading being discarded. The **counter-reset** row returned before the evidence lines were appended, so a read that failed and was redeemed vanished from a run whose counters had reset; every row of a protocol now carries them. And a **baseline where both classes failed** was thrown away by its own step guard: `Invoke-CheckStep` caught the throw, wrote its generic step-error row and returned `$null`, taking four attempts, their seconds and any pre-window failures with it, and leaving the analysis to say only that complete data was unavailable. The snapshot is returned whatever it holds, and one row per read that failed is written out of it. **The acceptance validator had to learn the new shape** (PR #40, round 9): `Test-ResultSet` still expected a step-error row and one generic row on a machine where neither class could be read — the shape 1.2.7 produced — so an end-to-end run on exactly the machine this path exists for would have been rejected as malformed. It now expects one row per read that failed, per sample, and no step error at all; both protocols are named whatever their counters did; and `selftest_resultset.ps1` pins the new shape and rejects the old one (41 → 42 cases). **And the chain's own probe had to learn the retry too** (round 10): `Get-MachineFacts` asked each counter class once, so a transient failure there would have called a class unreadable that the run reads on its second attempt, and the result-set check would have rejected a correct report on exactly the machine this release is for. The probe now mirrors the shipped read — two attempts, the same two fields — and an AST assertion in `unit_tests.ps1` fails if the shipped call stops saying what the probe assumes. **The reset row was refined once more in round 7**: it prints the window it spans, so the note's *these N seconds* has something to refer to, and it no longer borrows the sentence about the deltas above — a row that reports an uncalculable delta has none. That sentence now belongs to the rows that do.

**And the version bump rewrote history until round 7 caught it.** Replacing every `1.2.7` label in the manuals also rewrote three provenance statements — the extra-TCP label's width, the stray positional value, and the summary's *All required checks passed* — which describe what changed in **1.2.7**, not in this release. They cannot simply be restored, because the packaged validator requires every version label in a manual to be the version being built; so they now state the behaviour without a version at all (*in earlier versions*, 「早期版本裡」), which is what a manual that describes the tool as shipped should have said. Six files: both manuals, both formats, both languages, minus the two HTML twins that never carried the sentence.

**The duration, and the label that no longer contradicts the manual.** Each row's sample duration is computed from **that protocol's own two stamps**, not from the stamps of the enclosing snapshots, and the configured length stands beside it as what it is — a minimum, which is what `Wait-ForMinimumTcpSample` enforces. The deltas were always this protocol's own and the rate is a ratio, so the duration was the only figure a failed read ever corrupted. Which reads count against a window took a review round to state correctly (round 1 below): a window is lengthened by a read that delayed the stamp closing it without delaying the stamp opening it — in the **ending** snapshot every read at or before that protocol, and in the **baseline** snapshot every read *after* it, because a stalled TCPv6 baseline read pushes TCPv6's own opening stamp and the rest of the run alike but not TCPv4's. The pre-window reads are in neither list since round 3 put them all in front of both baseline stamps.

**The cost, stated rather than discovered.** On a machine whose counters answer, the two pre-window reads are the whole addition and they cost milliseconds. On one where every read runs out of time, a run's six reads spend up to 80 seconds between them against 32 before — the price of not losing the measurement — and the manuals say so: a counter read that runs out of time is tried once more before the tool gives up on it.

**What the tests hold now.** 93 assertions in each language inside the existing `unit` step, 280 × 2, and they stub `Get-CimInstance` itself, so what runs above the stub is the shipped retry loop and the shipped bookkeeping, failing with the exception the walk recorded (`CimException`, *Timed out*). Two hand-built snapshot fixtures pin the arithmetic: the shape of the walk's failure — TCPv4's two timeouts inside TCPv6's window — makes the row say **24.5** seconds, its own, and not the 26 the enclosing snapshots span; a clean pair says nothing about attempts at all, because a row explains what happened and not what did not. **Mutation-tested seventeen ways, every one caught**, and the list is the reviewed tree's rather than the first draft's. Seven before the review: the duration back on the enclosing stamps (3 assertions fail), no second attempt (8), no pre-window read (2, both on the AST — the call site is the only part of it a test can assert without a machine), the window note not written (2), an attempt that a later one redeems not recorded (9), the unreadable-counter row losing its attempts (1), the warm-up taking two attempts of its own (2). Four for round 1's window rule: the pre-fix reckoning that counted no baseline failure against any window (3), a protocol's own baseline failures counted against its own window (2), the pre-window reads of later protocols left out (3), a pre-window read not marked as one where it is quoted (1). Two for round 3's: one protocol left unwarmed (2), a pre-window read counted against another protocol's window again (4). Four for round 4's: the other snapshot's attempts appended unconditionally (2), never appended (4), an ending line labelled as the baseline's (2), a borrowed line labelled with the borrowing row's snapshot (1) — **and the last two assertions exist because that third mutant passed the whole suite when it was first run**: nothing asserted which snapshot a line belonged to until it did. **Three trees of the review are measurements of their own**: run against the tree as it stood before round 3, before round 4 and before round 5, the suite fails with exactly the 5, 4 and 8 assertions that describe those defects and nothing else.

**The rate, measured here and not reproduced here.** `tests/tcp_counter_rate.ps1` is the statistical level, and it is deliberately **not** a step of the chain: whether the read fails at all is a property of a machine under load, and a step that fails one run in twenty teaches nothing while eroding trust in every other step (#25 is this project's own instance of that). It takes N snapshots per condition — idle, and under deliberate local file I/O it generates itself — and reports first-attempt failures, reads lost after both attempts, and the seconds spent on failures. On the reference machine on 2026-09-09: **0 first-attempt failures in 160 reads** — 50 idle, and 110 under load with every worker verified copying before the first sample (3 of 3 after 0.4 s; 6 of 6 after 1.5 s over 800 × 512 KB). The load halves were measured twice: round 14 found the harness sampling without waiting for its jobs to reach a first copy, so some of the original load samples may have been idle ones under a load label, and these are the re-measured figures. The failure does not reproduce here, which is the honest reading rather than evidence that the fix works; the machine that showed it three times was `win11-enUS`, each time on the first run inside a freshly extracted copy. That is where the item's first clause gets answered, and until then it stays open.

**The documents.** The user manuals' section 9 says the tool tries a second time and that the row means both attempts failed, and gains a paragraph for the question the new label invites — why the row shows more seconds than IT configured — while section 2's *How long it takes* names the retry. The technical guides' 4.8 gains the reads, the attempts, what a row says about them, the bounded cost, and the per-protocol duration. Eight document files in both languages, as always, plus the six guide copies.

**#39 — the overall result is decided by what the run measured.** The second item of this release, and the one a person feels: until now a single unreadable counter made a run in which every check passed read as *Test Incomplete*, and an extra target typed without its port made it *Attention Required*. A row that could not measure, a sample too coarse for the threshold applied to it, and a fact about this run's own input now keep their row, their badge and their place in the counts, are named in the summary, and do not decide the result. Everything measured keeps its weight, optional targets included. The marking is a `Weightless` switch on `Add-CheckResult`, opt-in one branch at a time; `Get-OverallStatus` and every predicate of the fingerprint read the weighted rows, while `Get-SummaryCounts` and `Get-ReportNoticeFlags` read the page, so a demoted row keeps its badge **and** the sentence that explains it. `Invoke-CheckStep` carries the marking like `-Scope`, and only the four quality collectors declare it.

**The rows that had to start existing before anything could be demoted.** Every configured target that cannot be tested writes a weightless notice where its result belonged — TCP and HTTP wrote one only when required, DNS wrote nothing, ping was validated nowhere — and a required one adds a weighted row saying the check did not run, so a run cannot read Healthy with a required measurement missing. Each dropped extra target leaves a row in its own section, which is what makes demoting its Startup Notice honest rather than concealing. `Test-ConfigurationSemantics` gained `PingTargets` and split both aggregate rows, so the standards and the thresholds keep their weight while this run's own input does not, and the same typo is no longer convicted twice. Measured on the reference machine: `-TcpTarget 8.8.8.8 -PingTarget http://example.com` ends **Overall Healthy** with both named and neither counted, and a required target configured `Port=0` ends **Test Incomplete** with the two rows the cut requires.

**Two things this release states rather than does quietly.** `Weightless` ships **additive under `SchemaVersion: 2`**, not a bump to 3, on the design note's own rule that a consumer reads only documented fields — and the guides' field list gains it in the same change. And **this reverses what the 1.2.5 documents release decided**: a retransmission seen in a sample too small to give a rate was left a `WARN` that turned the verdict on its own; it now reports the same numbers and leaves the verdict to what was measured. The manuals say so in both languages, where they used to say the opposite.
