**#39's own review: twenty-two rounds, forty-two findings, every one accepted, none rejected.** The decision came through unchanged; what the rounds took apart was the line it draws. *Rounds 1 to 4* were the validation chain's row oracle, which had re-implemented the tool's own value conversion by hand and disagreed with it about "4.0", about an explicit null, and about a whole number larger than Int32; it loads the tool's own converters now, so it can say which rows a configuration produces without also deciding what a value means. *Rounds 5 to 13 were one defect in four places, and they are the substance of this item*: a value that could never become a request was accepted, attempted, and its refusal recorded as a measurement — `foo..bar` as a ping target, then the same name as a DNS entry, then `http://foo..bar/` as a URL, then `Host = "http://example.com"` beside a valid port on a TCP target. Each produced a weighted 100 % loss or a required connectivity failure for a packet that never left, which is the confusion this item exists to remove, and each was found only because the round before it had fixed the neighbouring family. They are one function now — `Test-HostNameSyntax`, asked by all four families, by the configuration validation and by the IT panel's pre-check, so the panel cannot refuse what the run accepts or the reverse. *Rounds 18 to 22* were that function reading the wrong string: a label's limit counted in typed characters where the wire form is longer, a root dot stripped before the conversion that creates it, delimiters judged before IDNA turns a full-width solidus into `/`, and a control character that is neither a delimiter nor whitespace. The conversion happens first now, and every rule judges the form that would actually be sent. What is left of that character space is written down as [#54](../docs/backlog.md) rather than left implicit: it was closed by example, and the examples ran out before the space did.

**Three defects in this release's own tests, none of them from a finding, and each one an assertion that could not fail.** Two oracle cases for an unusable `-TcpTarget` both expected the row count a clean configuration produces anyway. Six unit cases never executed at all — the function they called was not in the file's loaded-function list, an unknown command is a statement-terminating error, and the summary counts the assertions that *ran*, so the step reported 338 passed and passed. And the fix for the control-character round put four literal control bytes into both shipped scripts, which every check accepted, because a character class written with the characters is the same class; `grep` calling the script a binary file is what caught it, and a fifth byte in the harness had already made git treat that file as binary and stop normalising its line endings. All three are guarded now: `unit_tests.ps1` fails on a call to anything it did not load, `validate_release.py` refuses a packaged file carrying a stray control byte — including a lone carriage return, which round 22 found the first version of that guard exempting — and the chain's `parse` step sweeps `tests\` for the same thing. Every one of them was run against a deliberately broken tree before being trusted, because a guard that cannot fail is the defect it is meant to catch.

**Six review rounds, eight findings, every one accepted, and four of them were this release's own subject one layer deeper.** Round 1: the window rule counted only the ending snapshot's failed reads, on the reasoning that a baseline failure moves both baseline stamps — true only of the protocol read first, so a stalled TCPv6 baseline read left TCPv4 with an unexplained window, which is the defect this release exists to remove. Round 2: the rate harness printed the sum of two failed attempts as attempt 1's duration — a false per-iteration line in the one script whose purpose is per-iteration evidence. Round 3: interleaved with the measured reads, TCPv6's warm-up fell inside TCPv4's window, so a warm-up that was merely **slow** — the start-up cost the feature exists to absorb — could lengthen a window with no failure behind it; the warm-ups became a pass of their own. Round 4: a protocol whose counter could not be read has no quality row, so its attempts from the *other* snapshot reached nothing. Round 5, three at once: an empty or fieldless answer was a failure the retry loop never saw, the counter-reset row returned before the evidence was appended, and a baseline where both classes failed was thrown away by its own step guard. Round 6, on this record: `FUNCTION_COUNT` 86 → 89 rather than 87, and a mutation list that still described the first draft. Round 7, on the manuals and one row: the version bump had rewritten three statements about what **1.2.7** changed, and the counter-reset row borrowed a sentence about deltas it does not have. Round 8: the user manuals' worst-case runtime still predated the retry, and this record quoted a chain run that predated three of its own assertions. Round 9: the acceptance validator still expected the shape 1.2.7 produced on a machine with no readable counter class, so a correct report from exactly that machine would have been rejected. Round 10, the same seam one step further out: the chain's fact probe read each class once where the product reads it twice, so a transient failure in the probe alone would have failed a correct run. Rounds 11 to 13 followed that seam into the documents — the test README's contract, the guides' claim that a clean row was unchanged, and three records promising an accounting of a long window that only failed reads receive. Rounds 14 to 19 were the on-demand rate harness, and its own guards failing open one after another: load measured before the jobs were making any, an invocation that could make no load at all, a readiness flag meaning *tried* rather than *copied*, then meaning a count rather than the bytes, a `Start-Job` failure stranding the workers before it — and, at **P1**, a `-WorkDir` the harness was only lent and deleted whole. Round 20 found nothing. **One mutant of my own passed the whole suite** while I was answering round 4 — an ending line labelled as the baseline's — and the two assertions that now catch it were written for it.

**Chain run six times across the two items of this release, and the sixth is the one that counts, because it is the tree the review left behind — twenty rounds, twenty-three findings, every one accepted (Windows 11 10.0.26200, Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, Python 3.14.4). `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Package -RequireHealthy`, everything, with the real windows and the package: 33 cases, **33 passed**, 754 s.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 109 / 109; guard self-test OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43; unit **280 × 2**, 93 of them this release's; report-stage 128 × 2; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 132 / 132; result-set self-test **42 / 42**; headless GUI 4 / 4; the four real-window runs with the window titled 1.2.8; five acceptance runs, including the two against unreachable targets that must end Attention Required and carry a cause; and the package built from the tracked files: `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.8.zip`, 461 372 bytes, SHA256 `ac0183491b0d3fc7642a0c19df7b311f042cd3b12f07a5635a031dea4c8ec21f`, the validator 109 / 109 from inside the extracted package, and its four entries opened as real windows. As in 1.2.7, those package figures are the step's own: this record is a tracked file, so the sentence you are reading changed the asset's bytes after it was built, which is why the release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit.

**Run 6 (2026-09-10), on the tree #39's first implementation left behind — before its review, which then ran to twenty-two rounds: 33 cases, 33 passed, 795 s** — clean at the first attempt, with unit **301 × 2**, report-stage **160 × 2**, result-set 42 / 42, campaign 132 / 132, the four real windows and `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.8.zip` at 474 467 bytes, SHA256 `8ae613663b0c6807c6d71a571ed186cc1653e954875b89f8fdde7eb60725d76c`, validated 109 / 109 from inside the extracted package. **Run 7 (2026-09-10), on the tree the review left behind, which is the one that ships: 34 cases, 33 passed, 1 failed, 1,205 s.** The failure was measured and it is this machine's: the zh-TW user acceptance run sent **98 TCP segments and retransmitted 4** in its eight-second sample — 4.082 % against a 2 % warning threshold, which `-RequireHealthy` counts as a failed case. That is [#51](../docs/backlog.md)'s shape exactly, a verdict resting on a sample too small for the threshold applied to it, and the second time this record has had to say so. `-Steps acceptance,resultset -RequireHealthy` immediately afterwards: **6 cases, 0 failed**, all five acceptance runs Overall Healthy. Everything else passed at the first attempt: unit **371 × 2** (280 at the start of this release, 301 when #39 was first implemented, 371 after its rounds), report-stage **160 × 2**, result-set **79**, campaign 132 / 132, doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43, launcher 114 / 114, envguard 18 × 2, the validator **113 / 113** including the stray-byte check this release adds, the four headless GUI cases and the four real windows, and `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.8.zip` at 482 516 bytes, SHA256 `5e47accd8228629319cc08cf4af6e5dee413692a98ebdf216f58188c4e35d7ed`, validated 113 / 113 from inside the extracted package. As in 1.2.7 that asset figure is the step's own: this record is a tracked file, so the sentence you are reading changed the bytes after they were measured, which is why the release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit. The five runs below belong to #38 and are kept because what failed in them was measured, not excused.

**What this machine's own network cost across the five runs, recorded rather than excused.** Run 1 (20:04) failed eleven cases: nine on quality — the `gui` case with the configured 125-second sample measured 48 579 segments sent and **256 retransmitted** over 129.3 seconds, 0.527 %, far under the 2 % warning threshold and over the absolute count of 50, which is the rule [#51](../docs/backlog.md) is about — and two on this record, which had no 1.2.8 entry for the validator to find. Run 2 (20:23) failed one, the same noise in the en-US case while its zh-TW twin ended Healthy; the step re-ran clean. Run 3 (21:54) was 33 / 33 in 793 s. Run 4 (22:33) failed one again, and that one was #51's other half: **767 segments sent and 16 retransmitted** over 128.1 seconds, 2.086 % — a rate over the threshold on a sample a two-minute window on an idle machine could not make bigger; the step re-ran clean, 4 / 4 in 323 s. Run 5, above, is clean throughout. Nothing in any of the five failed for the change itself, and each row that carried the noise printed its own window correctly — 129.3 against a configured 125, and 128.1 against 125 — which is the behaviour this release adds.

## v1.2.7 — six items about what the screen says · 2026-09-09 — the first tool release since 1.2.3

**What and why.** Six backlog items, and one sentence covers all of them: the tool knew something and the person did not, because of where it was written. The IT panel carried a format in a label it clipped; it checked a typed target only after spending a run on it; the window named a folder's worth of files and not the one to send; the summary said *All checks passed* whatever the Information rows held; the report opened by explaining a badge it did not carry; and a stray value on the command line became the configuration path. **#49, #45, #46, #35, #40 and #36** — five of the six raised by the two user-manual walks, on machines, by a person following the manual.

**Version.** 1.2.7 in both scripts, the packaged validator (`TOOL_VERSION`, and `FUNCTION_COUNT` 81 → 86 for the five functions this release adds), `README_BILINGUAL.md`, the four manuals in both formats, the six guide copies and the repository README; `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated over the same 28 files, 18 of which changed. The guides' history sentences keep their own numbers and gain one for 1.2.7.

**#49 — every label carries its own width, and the chain measures them.** The panel built its six field labels in one loop with a fixed `Size(100, 22)` and no `AutoSize`. Measured on the reference machine with `GetPreferredSize`, which is WinForms' own answer: `Extra TCP (host:port)` needs **136 px** and 「額外 TCP（host:port）」 **147 px** in a box of 100, so both wrapped and lost their second line inside 22 px — and `Sample seconds` needed **109 px**, a second overflow nobody had reported. Each label now carries the width its text needs and the two columns beside it moved right; the panel is 880 px wide and the last control ends at 875. **The assertion matters more than the widths:** the `gui-headless` step now measures every fixed-size `Label`, `CheckBox` and `Button` of both windows in both languages against the box it was given, and fails naming the ones that overflow. Run against the tree as it was before this release it fails with exactly the two en-US overflows, which is the defect this release removes; run against this tree it reports 23 controls in the IT window and 8 in the user window, every one fitting. Width only, and the reason is in the code: the *Reset to config* button's preferred **height** is 3 px more than its box and renders correctly on every machine this project has captured, so asserting height would fail on something nobody can see. **And a control that fits its box can still be out of reach:** the panel is anchored left and right, so shrinking the window shrinks it, and the form's minimum width of 780 px left the grid's third column outside the panel entirely — true of the old layout too, at that width. The IT window's minimum width is now the 940 px its grid needs, the user window keeps 780, and the headless step asserts the invariant: the panel at the narrowest allowed window (880 px) against the grid it carries (875 px).

**#45 — the panel checks a typed target before the run, with the rule the run uses.** `Test-TcpTargetSyntax` is the one place the `host:port` rule lives; `Set-RunOptions` rejects with it after a run has started, and the panel checks with it on **Start Test**, so a panel that disagreed with the run is not constructible. A value that is not `host:port` marks the field, is named on the screen with an example — *Extra TCP: '8.8.8.8' is not host:port - for example 8.8.8.8:443.* — and the run does not start; a second press starts it without that target, which is exactly what the tool did before this check existed, Startup Notice and all. Nothing blocks and nothing is refused, because a person being guided by IT over the phone must not be stranded behind a dialog. The other three free-text fields are deliberately left without a rule — a name or an address the resolver refuses is a result, not a typing mistake — and all four now show an example on hover (`1.1.1.1`, `www.example.com`, `8.8.8.8:443`, `https://www.example.com/`): an example value rather than a notation, which is what the walk of 2026-09-08 asked for and what #49 made room for.

**#46 — one file to send, named once and quoted twice.** `Get-SendToItLine` writes the sentence, the window's line under the buttons shows it — *Send this file to IT: …*, where the file is the one **Open Report** opens, the HTML unless it could not be written — and the console prints the same sentence after its three paths. The report's own summary last line no longer offers a choice between two files: *Send this file to IT as it is*, about the file in the reader's hand. Three sentences that used to differ now say one thing.

**#35 — the summary claims what the verdict actually made.** The default branch said *All checks passed during this run* whenever every required check passed, whatever the Information rows held — and an optional target that failed outright is an Information row by design. It now says *All required checks passed during this run*, and when an optional ping, TCP or HTTP target did not answer it names them on the next line and says it does not change the result. **Each added target carries its own value in its row title** — `Extra TCP fileserver:445`, not a generic `Extra TCP` — so two targets of the same kind are told apart in the table and in that line; round 2 of the review below found the summary naming one where two had failed. The ping rows are the exception and keep the plain name, because `Test-PingTargets` already writes their title as `<name>: <target>` — round 3 found the address printed twice, and the test that missed it fabricated its rows instead of asking the run what it writes. A run with all four kinds now answers that directly: `Extra ping: 127.0.0.1`, `Extra DNS localhost`, `Extra TCP 127.0.0.1:9`, `Extra URL https://www.example.com/`.

**#40 — a notice for what the run produced.** The HTML report and the text report opened with the same two claims whether or not the run had produced either: an explanation of the *Unable to Check* badge, and a qualification of a retransmission rate. Each half is now asked for separately. The badge half appears when a row carries `ERROR`. The rate half turns on `$script:RetransmissionRateComputed`, set where a rate is actually computed (`$sentDelta -gt 0`) — not on a tagged row existing, because a run whose counter reads both failed emits tagged `ERROR` rows and never obtains a rate. A healthy report now opens with its table. The flag is cleared with the results at the start of every run, not once at process start: the window is reused, so a rate computed by one run would otherwise be explained in the report of a later *Run Again* whose counters failed — round 1 of the review below found that, and it is the same defect the item is about, one run later.

**#36 — a stray value is refused, not bound.** `[CmdletBinding(PositionalBinding = $false)]`: `-PingTarget a b` now fails to bind with PowerShell's own message — *A positional parameter cannot be found that accepts argument 'b'* — instead of binding `b` to `-ConfigPath`, running on the built-in defaults, recording *Configuration file not found: b* as an *Unable to Check* row, ending Test Incomplete and losing the second target in silence.

**What the tests hold now.** The report-stage tests gain scenario J — 21 assertions in each language, 103 → **124** — and it covers the shapes rather than the strings: a healthy run with an optional target that did not answer (the summary says *required*, names the target, names one file, and the report carries neither half of the notice), then the other shape (an `ERROR` row and a computed rate bring both halves back), then the panel rejecting `8.8.8.8` before a run while `Set-RunOptions` drops the same value after one, and an AST assertion that the rule has one implementation called from both. The `gui-headless` step carries #49's measurement. Every existing assertion is unchanged and still passes: unit 178 × 2, launcher 114, guards, envguard 18 × 2.

**The documents.** The user manuals' section 5 now says the window names the file to send and quotes the new line; section 4's summary table carries the *All required checks passed* wording; section 7's paragraph, which 1.2.6 wrote to work around the clipped label, is replaced by what the panel now does — the label says the format, the field shows an example, and a bad value is named before the run starts. The IT deployment manuals' section 4 describes the check, the examples and the label widths, and its command-line note is reduced to the syntax advice now that PowerShell refuses the stray value itself. Eight files, as always. **No sentence was needed for #40:** the item's own rule is that a conditional notice needs no documentation and an unconditional one does, and the manuals never described it.

**Chain run from the branch, twice: before the review and on the tree it left behind (Windows 11 10.0.26200, Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, Python 3.14.4) — `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Package -RequireHealthy`, everything, with the real windows and the package.** The second run is the one that counts, because rounds 1 to 3 changed what the scripts do: **33 cases, 32 passed, 846 s.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 109 / 109; guard self-test OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43; unit 178 × 2; report-stage **128 × 2**; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 125 / 125; headless GUI 4 / 4, each case naming the controls it measured and the panel's width at the narrowest allowed window; the four real-window runs at 1175 × 875 and 1175 × 1090 with the window titled 1.2.7; five acceptance runs; result-set self-test 41 / 41; and the package built from the tracked files: `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.7.zip`, 438 373 bytes, SHA256 `2788958013dbb2017c8d2e074c45576d785dc8aa68193263fba6096251abd57e`, the validator 109 / 109 from inside the extracted package, and its four entries opened as real windows. Those package numbers are from the step's own re-run, and this record is a tracked file under `healthcheck/`, so every sentence added here changes the asset's bytes again: that is why the manifest leaves `VALIDATION.md` out, and why the release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit rather than any build made on a branch.

**What failed in each run was the machine, and it is worth writing down as a measurement rather than an excuse.** Three cases across the day's runs ended above a threshold, each on a different quality row, each on this one host: the first chain run's `gui zh-TW IT` at *TCPv4 sent 2906, retransmitted 251, 8.637%*; an acceptance run at *sent 84, retransmitted 2, 2.381%*; and the second chain run's `package en-US IT` at *Public IP 1.1.1.1: 25% loss (3/4 successful)*. Every one of them is a `-RequireHealthy` failure and nothing else — the flag exists because on a machine with a real network condition the warning is the tool doing its job, which is why it is off by default. Each was re-run and passed: the `gui` step 4 / 4 in 329 s, the `package` step 5 / 5 in 115 s. Nothing in this release touches a threshold, the sampling or the counters, and what it does touch is measured by the 128 report-stage assertions and the headless GUI step, both green in both runs.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #35, rounds 1 to 4 · 2026-09-09: five findings, all accepted, and a clean pass on the fourth.** Every one of them landed on code this release wrote, and three of the five were the release's own subject seen once more. *Round 1 on `037ccb7`, three P2s:* `$script:RetransmissionRateComputed` was set once at process start and never cleared, so in the reused window a rate computed by one run would have explained the notice of a later *Run Again* whose counters failed — #40's defect one run later; the panel is anchored left and right, so the third column, moved right by #49, sat outside the panel at the form's 780 px minimum, and the IT window's minimum is now the 940 px its grid needs, asserted in the headless step (against the previous commit it fails: *the panel is 720 px wide at the window's minimum width and its rightmost control ends at 875 px*, which was true of the old layout at that width too); and all six packaged technical-guide copies still said a bare second value binds to `-ConfigPath`. *Round 2 on `2a35cbb`, P2:* the summary named `Extra TCP` whichever endpoint had failed, and two of a kind collapsed under `Select-Object -Unique` — the generic row title was older than this branch, and every added target now carries its value. *Round 3 on `bb72a34`, P2:* the ping row's title is written as `<name>: <target>` by `Test-PingTargets`, so round 2's fix printed the address twice there; the ping entry keeps the plain name, and the finding's sharper half was about the test — scenario J fabricated its rows instead of asking the run what it writes, which is why a real run of all four kinds is now in the record. *Round 4 on `852d92b`:* no findings. Each round re-ran the validator, the unit and report-stage tests and the headless GUI step, and regenerated `SHA256SUMS.txt`; the real-window, acceptance and package steps were re-run at the end, which is the second chain run above.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #33, rounds 1 and 2 · 2026-09-09: one finding, accepted, and a clean pass on the second.** *Round 1 on `f295e63`, P2:* the same mistake as the three above it, one layer up in the new code. `IAsyncResult.IsCompleted` is true whether `ShellExecute` returned or the invocation threw — a folder that is not there, an item the shell will not parse — and `Stop-ShellVerb` swallowed the `EndInvoke` exception that said so, so W5's note would have recorded *the launcher was started* for a launch that never happened. It now answers `returned`, `failed` with the error, or `blocked`, and the row says which. Measured with the two functions lifted verbatim out of the harness: a path that does not exist gives `failed` with its error, no opener gives `not started`, and the real W5 row still captures the warning and closes it. *Round 2 on `505616c`:* no findings.

## v1.2.6 — the two document findings of the zh-TW walk · 2026-09-09 — documents release (the tool unchanged)

**What and why.** The walk of the zh-TW user manual, whose entry follows this one, produced four findings; two of them are the manuals' and are this release. The other two are the tool's and wait in `docs/backlog.md` as #49 and #50, with the walk's second instances of #44 and #46 beside them. Neither script changed but its version string, `FUNCTION_COUNT` stays 81, and nothing the tool does differs from 1.2.5.

**Version.** 1.2.6 in both scripts, the packaged validator (`TOOL_VERSION`), `README_BILINGUAL.md`, the four manuals in both formats, the six guide copies and the repository README; `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated over the same 28 files, 18 of which changed. The guides' history sentences keep the numbers they were written under — 1.2.4 and 1.2.5 are history — and gain one of their own for 1.2.6, as the label rule allows: an older number may be named in the body, this one is required in the first line and in the applies-to statement.

**Finding 1 — the zh-TW manual put a phrase in the dialog's mouth, and it is not the phrase.** Section 3's step and the section 6 table both told the reader that the security dialog's publisher line reads 「不明的發行者」. On the walked machine it reads 「發行者不明」. Both places now say so, in the form the walker read: 「發行者那一行寫著「發行者不明」」. The entry below said this manual *described* the dialog rather than quoting it word for word, and that nobody had yet read it on a zh-TW machine; it did in fact quote a phrase, which is what made the gap invisible — a translation of the English one, reading like a quotation. The en-US half was confirmed word for word on 2026-09-08 and is untouched. **Where the evidence for this one is thinner than the rest, and it is said here rather than left to be discovered:** the phrase comes from the person reading the screen, with no capture beside it, because the harness's own dialog capture is the first of the three defects that walk found in it (`InvokeVerb('open')` blocks until the dialog is answered, so the row was recorded as *not produced* while the dialog stood there — backlog #48). The next walk with a repaired harness should photograph that dialog, and this sentence is the one to check against it.

**Finding 2 — four manuals name a field by a label the panel cannot show.** The user manuals' section 7 and the IT deployment manuals' section 4 name the IT panel's extra-TCP field as **Extra TCP (host:port)** / 「**額外 TCP（host:port）**」, which is the label's text in the source and not what a person sees: the panel gives that label a 100 px box, the text needs 134 px in en-US and 153 px in zh-TW, and a `Label` that is not `AutoSize` wraps and clips its second line. The manuals were not wrong about the tool — the label really does carry the notation — they were wrong about the screen, which is the same shape of defect as finding 4 of the walk below, where sentences true about the tool were false about the machine they were read on. Each of the four documents now carries a paragraph where it names the field: the user manuals say the label is cut short, that the format is therefore in the text, and what to type — `8.8.8.8:443` — with what a value without a port costs; the IT deployment manuals carry the measurement as well, so an IT department reading it knows why the person on the phone cannot see the format, and are pointed at backlog #49, which is the panel's half. This is a document fix that stops being needed the day #49 lands, and it is worth making now because the manual is what the person has today.

**What was left alone, and why.** The IT deployment manual's *What an extra target counts for*, which already says that a target which is not `host:port` is dropped with a Startup Notice and quotes the message: it was accurate, and the walk's run produced exactly that row. The user manual's general rule that a Startup Notice on its own turns the verdict to Attention Required, for the same reason. The en-US publisher sentences, measured on their own machine. And everything the other two findings touch: the launcher's timestamp (#44), the window naming one file (#46), the console launcher's last line (#50) and the panel's own geometry (#49) are the tool's, and a manual that describes a defect does not remove it — the rule this project has followed since the timeout of 1.2.5's finding 1.

**The zh-TW half, and the en-US half.** Finding 1 is zh-TW only, because it is about a Windows dialog in Chinese. Finding 2 landed in all eight files — two manuals, two languages, markdown and HTML — because the label it is about is clipped in both languages, which is the whole of backlog #49.

**Chain run from the branch (Windows 11 10.0.26200, Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, Python 3.14.4) — the set GitHub Actions runs, `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Steps parse,validator,guards,docfacts,unit,report,envguard,launcher,campaign,gui-headless -Package -SkipGui`: 19 cases, 0 failed, 302 s.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 109 / 109; guard self-test OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43; unit 178 × 2; report-stage 103 × 2; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 125 / 125; headless GUI 4 / 4; package built from the tracked files: `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.6.zip`, 420 099 bytes, SHA256 `4c6a47127bffa8d21c047629183175443252cc14165d9375e30908523eaf36f5`, the validator 109 / 109 from inside the extracted package — measured before this entry's closing paragraphs were written, and this record is itself a tracked file under `healthcheck/`, so every further sentence added here changes the asset's bytes: that is why the manifest leaves `VALIDATION.md` out, and why the release asset is CI's build of the tagged commit rather than any build made on a branch. The real-window steps are not needed for a change that leaves the scripts' behaviour untouched — the four headless GUI cases still build both windows in both languages, which is where a manual sentence about a panel would break if the panel had moved — and the release asset will be CI's build of the tagged commit, as for 1.2.3, 1.2.4 and 1.2.5.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #32, round 1 · 2026-09-09: no findings on `3c9b7d7`.** The chain numbers above stand, and the clean first round is recorded the way the rounds of PR #28 and PR #31 are, so the entry says what reviewed it and on which commit. What a review of this branch could not have caught is worth naming beside it: both findings are sentences measured against a machine, and the only thing that can falsify them is another walk.

## Walking the user manual in zh-TW · 2026-09-09 — a zh-TW machine, the released 1.2.5 asset, and the capture harness's first walk

**What this is.** The half the entry below named as outstanding: the same sheet (`tests/user-manual-walk-checklist.md`), the zh-TW manual, on a machine whose display language is Chinese, with the released 1.2.5 asset. It is also the first walk conducted with `tests/walk_capture.ps1` — [backlog #48](../docs/backlog.md), written after the en-US walk and never yet run on a machine it was not written on. It produced one finding that reaches back into the entry below and corrects it, three more that are the tool's, two sentences for the next documents release, and three defects in the harness itself.

**Machine and asset.** `DESKTOP-CO7QIMR`: Windows 10 22H2 (10.0.19045), display language and culture zh-TW, short-date pattern `yyyy/M/d`, Windows PowerShell 5.1.19041.3803, 96 DPI on a 1668 × 999 desktop, unelevated. The asset is the released `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.5.zip`, downloaded with that machine's own browser — its `Zone.Identifier` carries `ZoneId=3` and names the `v1.2.5` release tag it came from — extracted through Explorer to `C:\Users\test\Desktop\NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.5\NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.5\`, the doubled folder the entry below describes. This Desktop is **not** the OneDrive one: `%OneDrive%` exists on the machine, the Desktop resolves to `C:\Users\test\Desktop`, and the harness's report-directory row reads `synced: no` — so the finding-4 case of the walk below did not repeat here, and was not made to. Seven runs, three entry points.

**How it was walked, and what the harness changed about walking.** One run of `walk_capture.ps1` answered the 51 rows it owns — **29 captured, 22 not produced**, each *not produced* naming the precondition that was missing — and wrote `manifest.md` binding every answer to its row. The person's time went where #48 said it should: the six *Was it usable?* questions, the rows the sheet keeps for a person, and the places where the harness was wrong. The bundle was carried off the machine by hand, as that item requires, and it is not in this repository: 29 artefacts beside the seven runs' reports, and what this entry keeps is what they measure. That also settles the question the entry below left open — the sheet's *Observed* column stays empty by design now, because the manifest is that column, and this record is where the findings live.

**Finding 1 — the panel cannot show the label that carries the format, and that is why the field was mistyped.** The IT panel's six field labels are built in one loop with a fixed box — `$label.Size = New-Object System.Drawing.Size(100, 22)` (`NetworkHealthCheck.ps1:3877`), no `AutoSize`, with the text box beside them starting at x = 115 against a label at x = 12. Measured at the form's own font on the reference machine at 96 DPI (`TextRenderer.MeasureText`, `Segoe UI` 10 pt and `Microsoft JhengHei UI` 10 pt, with padding and without — the numbers are the same): **`Extra TCP (host:port)` is 134 px wide and 「額外 TCP（host:port）」 is 153 px, in a box of 100.** A `Label` that is not `AutoSize` wraps rather than truncates, and a line of that font is 18–19 px inside a box 22 px high, so the second line is clipped to a sliver. That is what this walk saw on the screen in Chinese at 96 DPI, and what cropping the en-US capture of 2026-09-08 at 120 DPI shows in English: both languages, both machines, because the label and its box scale together and the DPI never enters into it.

**The whole chain, measured on one machine for the first time.** Run 06:17:54, the IT entry: `RawTargets.Tcp` `["8.8.8.8"]` → `ExtraTargets.Tcp` `[]` → one `WARN` row tagged `startup`, 「已忽略額外 TCP 目標「8.8.8.8」：格式應為 host:port。」 → counts of 17 Pass, 1 Warn, 4 Info out of 22 → 整體結果「需要注意」 → the `attention` fingerprint, whose lines are 「把報告交給 IT。」 and 「把 HTML 報告（或 JSON 檔）交給 IT。報告內含電腦名稱、使用者名稱、網卡 MAC 位址與 Wi-Fi 網路名稱。」 A typo in the one field whose format hint the panel does not show costs a run, produces a verdict about the operator's typing on a machine where every network check passed, and tells the person to hand IT a file that names the computer, the user, the MAC address and the Wi-Fi network. [Backlog #39](../docs/backlog.md) is the verdict half of that, [#45](../docs/backlog.md) the too-late-check half, and the label's own geometry is now [#49](../docs/backlog.md).

**The sentence this falsifies, in the entry below.** That entry reads the same defect as a reading error: *“The label does carry something — `Extra TCP (host:port)` — and that something is a **notation**, not an example … the walker read past the notation.”* It is wrong, and [backlog #45](../docs/backlog.md)'s premise with it. The notation is in the source; it is not on the screen to be read past. The person who walked 1.2.4 and the person who walked this are the same, and the port was left off both times; the reading that they had read past a notation came from the code rather than from the machine, and this measurement of the same label is what corrects it. The prescription survives for a different reason than the one recorded: an example value in the box beats a notation in a label whether or not the label can be read. It is the second time this project has met a sentence true about the source and false about the screen — finding 4 of the walk below was the first — and both times a walk found it and no review round did.

**And the same measurement finds a second label nobody has reported.** `Sample seconds` is 106 px in the same 100 px box — en-US only, since 取樣秒數 is 64 — so it wraps the same way, and nobody has complained because a clipped line under *Sample* still reads as the field it is. The code already carries the fix for a seventh label: `$hopsLabel` is given `Size(115, 22)` because `Traceroute hops` is 107 px wide, which says the sizing was done by eye, one label at a time. Both are in #49.

**Finding 2 — the console launcher's last line is not a sentence on this machine.** After a run that had just succeeded, `Start-NetworkCheck-Console.cmd` printed 「網路健康檢查已完成。」 and then `'◆ 代碼:0' is not recognized as an internal or external command,` / `operable program or batch file.` — its own line 45, `echo 結束代碼：0`, arriving at the screen as a command instead of as text. What is established: the line before it, in the same file and the same encoding, printed correctly, and so did every Chinese echo of the launcher-error path on the same machine (W28, W29); the six launchers are UTF-8 without a BOM with CRLF endings, and the zh-TW console launcher sets `chcp 65001` at its head; en-US's equivalent line is `echo Exit code: 0`, ASCII, and cannot fail this way; and the same bytes in a minimal batch file on the reference machine — Windows 11 26200, `ACP` and `OEMCP` both 950 — print correctly and exit 0. What is not established is the mechanism, and the two machines differ in more than one way that could carry it. The cost is entirely in what the person sees: `exit /b 0` still runs, so nothing downstream changes, and what is on the screen is an English error message in a Chinese window immediately after the sentence that says the check finished. [Backlog #50](../docs/backlog.md).

**Finding 3 — #44 confirmed in Chinese, and the fix it proposes needs one more clause.** The launcher error file written on this machine stamps itself `日期時間：週三 2026/09/09  5:39:17.42`. The date is unambiguous, exactly as [backlog #44](../docs/backlog.md) predicted for a zh-TW machine — but `%DATE%` here carries a localised weekday, 週三, that the machine's short-date pattern (`yyyy/M/d`) does not describe. So the parenthesis that item proposes would be true and incomplete, and an acceptance test that asserts the stamp matches the pattern would fail on a machine where nothing is wrong. It is in that item's status line now, before anybody writes the test.

**Finding 4 — a second instance of #46, and the two entries disagree about how much they say.** Asked again what the window should tell them, the walker answered that it should say where the report is, **which** files to send, and that 開啟報告資料夾 is what gets you there. The window's own line is 「報告：…\Reports\NetworkHealthCheck_20260909_053822_DESKTOP-CO7QIMR.html」 — a full path to the HTML file, which is a location and not an instruction; three files sit in the folder that 開啟報告資料夾 opens. The console entry is the other way round and no better: it prints all three, 「HTML 報告：」, 「文字報告：」 and 「JSON 報告：」, so a person reading that screen is choosing between three paths rather than one. Neither says which file goes to IT. [Backlog #46](../docs/backlog.md) records both.

**Two sentences for the next documents release.** *The publisher line.* The zh-TW user manual renders Windows' *Unknown Publisher* as 「不明的發行者」 in section 3 and again in the section 6 table; the dialog on a zh-TW machine says 「發行者不明」. The entry below says the zh-TW manual described that dialog rather than quoting it, deliberately, because nobody had read it on a zh-TW machine; somebody has now, and the phrase it does quote is not the machine's. *The clipped label, quoted as if it could be read.* Section 7 of both user manuals and section 4 of both IT deployment manuals name the field as **Extra TCP (host:port)** / 「**額外 TCP（host:port）**」, which is the label's text and not what the panel shows. Until #49 changes the panel, the sentence that tells a person to type a host and port into that field is the only place the format is legible, and both manuals should say the format rather than lean on a label — a document fix that stops being needed the day the panel is fixed, and is worth making anyway because the manual is what the person has today. Both are 1.2.6, four files each as always.

**What the harness got wrong about itself, which is the other half of its first walk.** *It cannot see the dialog it opens.* `InvokeVerb('open')` blocks until the dialog is answered, so the shell's security dialog was on the screen while the harness recorded W5 as *not produced* — a false negative, and the worst kind, because it is indistinguishable in the manifest from a precondition that was genuinely missing. There is therefore no picture of the zh-TW security dialog in the bundle, and the publisher-line finding above rests on the person's reading of the screen. *Its Cancel clicks do not dismiss a shell dialog*, which is the same defect seen from the other end. *And its console capture is stdout only*, so `W31-console.txt` holds the successful run and the pause prompt and not the two error lines of finding 2, which survive only because the screenshot beside it caught them — an instance, inside this project's own test code, of exactly what [backlog #47](../docs/backlog.md) says about the launcher. All three are in #48's status line; none of them is a reason to walk without the harness, which answered 29 rows in one run that a person answered by hand, one row at a time, the day before.

**What held, and it is most of the tool.** The badge and counter vocabulary of the zh-TW report — 正常, 需注意, 異常, 無法檢查, 資訊, 合計 — and the verdict line 「結果：整體正常 — 本次可執行的必要檢查均通過。」 The window title 「網路健檢工具 1.2.5」, at 940 × 700 recorded and never set. 檢測完成 when it finished, and 開始檢測 becoming 重新檢測. *Run Again* adding exactly three files and leaving the first three untouched. 開啟報告資料夾 opening the folder the run actually wrote to. The resolved report directory reported with its sync status. The launcher's missing-program-file path in Chinese, on the screen and in `LauncherError.txt`, with the reason and the suggested action the file is supposed to carry. The IT panel keeping the values it was given — 6 pings, 13 seconds, 8 hops in the run above — and 還原設定檔 putting them back.

**Was it usable — the walker's answers.** Two of the six questions produced the findings above: the invisible format, which is finding 1, and the hand-off, which is finding 4. The other four were answered *nothing*, which is recorded as an answer, as it was on 2026-09-08: nothing else had to be guessed, and no other sentence of the manual was true about the tool and wrong about the machine in front of them. **W5b, the row the sheet keeps for a person:** unblocking the ZIP before extracting produced **no dialog of any kind**, so section 2's unblock claim now holds in zh-TW exactly as the walk below measured it in en-US.

**What this walk covered, and what it did not.** Seven runs through three entry points — the user launcher, the console launcher and the IT panel — one launcher failure produced on purpose, and the two extractions of the blocked-and-unblocked pair. What it did not reach: the partial-write path and the emergency-file paths, the two parts of the harness that are still code without a measurement, because nothing here broke the report machinery; the paths that need elevation or an ACL change; and the retransmission route into the quality branch, which needs traffic made while the sample runs and did not get it here either.

**What follows.** This entry and the backlog are the first of three pieces of work, and the only one that is done: the document fixes are 1.2.6, and the harness fixes are #48's. Nothing here changes the shipped 1.2.5 package. Of the four tool findings, one is new (#49), one is new and small (#50), and two are second instances of items already open — which is what a second walk is supposed to produce.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #31, rounds 1 and 2 · 2026-09-09: one finding, accepted, and a clean pass on the second.** *Round 1 on `e0a5a6c`, P2:* the repository `README.md`'s roadmap row — the backlog page's mirror, and where this project sends a wish that has no acceptance sentence — said 22 items open while the page held 25. It had fallen behind twice: #48 was never added to it when it was raised, and this branch adds #49 and #50. The row now names all three, and its arithmetic closes against the page again. *Round 2 on `af5c39a`:* no findings. Both rounds ran the validator (109 / 109) and the document-fact step (96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43); no script and no packaged document changed on this branch, so the script-facing steps stand from the 1.2.5 release run below.

## v1.2.5 — the six findings of the walk · 2026-09-08 — documents release (the tool unchanged)

**What and why.** The user-manual walk on `win11-enUS`, whose entry follows this one, found six things: all six are the manuals', none is the scripts'. This release is those six sentences and the version they ship under. Neither script changed but its version string, `FUNCTION_COUNT` stays 81, and nothing the tool does differs from 1.2.4 — the tool items the walk raised (#38 to #47) wait their turn in `docs/backlog.md`.

**Version.** 1.2.5 in both scripts, the packaged validator (`TOOL_VERSION`), `README_BILINGUAL.md`, the four manuals in both formats, the six guide copies and the repository README; `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated over the same 28 files. The guides' "since 1.2.4" lines are history and keep their number: the label rule lets a guide name an older version, and requires this one in its first line and in its applies-to statement.

**Finding 1 — the cause the tool imposes.** The user manual's section 9 answered an *Unable to Check* on the retransmission counters with "a permission, a policy or a broken performance counter", and the three failures the walk produced were none of the three: `Get-CimInstance -OperationTimeoutSec 8` timed out. The answer now names the tool's own eight-second limit first and says where it is met — the first run inside a folder extracted moments earlier, which is what all three had in common, while the five runs that followed no extraction read both protocols — keeps the other three causes, and tells the person to press **Run Again** before sending the report either way. The tool half is [backlog #38](../docs/backlog.md) and is not in this release: a manual that describes the defect does not remove it.

**Finding 2 — the small-sample family.** `Compare-TcpCounters` has three outcomes below `MinimumTcpSegmentsForRate`, and the FAQ named one of them, under a heading that did not match the second — the one this machine produced — and said nothing of the third, which is the one that moves the verdict. The answer now carries all three, quotes each message as the tool writes it, and says of the third that a warning about a sample too small to give a rate is not proof of a problem: it is `WARN`, so on its own it makes the verdict Attention Required — `Get-OverallStatus` ranks `FAIL` over `ERROR` over `WARN` among the rows the verdict counts, so a failed or unfinished check outranks it while an IT-scoped one does not (`report_stage_tests.ps1` scenario F asserts exactly that), which the answer now says (rounds 1 and 2).

**Finding 3 — one field short.** Section 6's `LauncherError.txt` row enumerated the time, the computer, the user, the reason, the folder and the program's path; the file written beside the launcher also carries the PowerShell it used. The row names it now, inside the same conditional the launcher itself observes: the `%TEMP%` fallback file, written when the folder beside the launcher cannot be, carries the reason and the suggested action without the three path fields.

**Finding 4 — the report leaves the computer when the package sits in a synced folder.** The finding of the walk, and the one this release spends the most words on, because the sentences it corrects were true about the tool and false about the machine. *The user manual:* section 1's **It uploads nothing** now says that a folder on this computer may be one Windows copies to the cloud; section 2's extraction step names the two folders Windows 11 backs up by default, where it had recommended them without a word; section 5 gains a paragraph of its own — how to tell (the path under the buttons, the report's *Report Directory* line), what it means for a file holding the computer's and the person's name, the addresses, the Wi-Fi network and the rest of the inventory printed just below it, and what to do (move the package out of the synced folder, or ask IT); and section 9's *Is the report sent anywhere?* names the sync service as the thing that copies a report off the machine without anyone sending it. *The IT deployment manual:* section 5's *Where to put it* now says that on a default Windows 11 the Desktop and Documents are the wrong answer when the reports must stay on the machine, with what a report holds, the alternative (a folder outside the sync root, or a rooted `ReportFolderName` that is not synced), and the note that such a machine's desktop is `%OneDrive%\Desktop` and not `%USERPROFILE%\Desktop`, which a deployment script meets before any user does. Section 3.1's flag, which called a rooted `ReportFolderName` on a share *the one setting that moves reports off the machine*, keeps the sentence and gains its qualifier: it is the one **setting**; a report folder that merely sits inside a synced folder does the same without anyone choosing it.

**Finding 5 — the folder inside the folder.** The ZIP carries its own top-level folder and Explorer's *Extract All* proposes another of the same name, so section 3's first instruction — "open the extracted folder and double-click `Start-English.cmd`" — described a folder that holds one thing: another folder. Nothing breaks, because every launcher path is `%~dp0`-relative; the step now says what the reader will see and that neither the extra level nor the folder's name matters.

**Finding 6 — what the security dialog says.** Section 3's step and section 6's row treated *Open File - Security Warning* as the download mark, which it is, and had no answer to what the dialog goes on to say: the publisher could not be verified, **Unknown Publisher**, and a line about running only software from publishers you trust. Both places now say that the tool is not digitally signed — the decision recorded as [backlog #22](../docs/backlog.md), which the IT deployment manual explains to IT and the user manual had left unanswered for the person standing in front of the dialog — that what stands in for a signature is the ZIP's own digest compared with the one published with the release (the IT manual's section 6), the in-package `SHA256SUMS.txt` being a consistency check on the files and not evidence of where the package came from — round 3's correction, and a correction to the walk's own prescription, which had named the manifest, that **Run** is the answer when IT sent the file and a question to ask when its origin is unknown, and what clearing *Always ask before opening this file* does, since it is an unblock by another route and the manual described only the Properties one.

**What was left alone, and why.** Section 4's badge table, whose "…or an execution error" is broad enough to hold the timeout of finding 1, so it was already right. Section 2's unblock claim, which the walk measured both ways and found true. The `LauncherError.txt` row's silence about whom to send the file to, which is the manual's sentence and not the file's — the file's own silence is [backlog #46](../docs/backlog.md). And the verdict rows, the *What to tell IT* table and the report inventory, which the walk read against the running tool and found accurate.

**The zh-TW half.** Every fix landed in four files — two languages, markdown and HTML — as every claim in these manuals does. One difference is deliberate: the zh-TW manual describes the security dialog rather than quoting it word for word, because the walk ran on `win11-enUS` and nobody has yet read the zh-TW dialog on a zh-TW machine. The zh-TW walk is the outstanding half of this measurement, and it is what would turn that description into a quotation.

**Chain run from the branch (Windows 11 10.0.26200, Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, Python 3.14.4) — the set GitHub Actions runs, `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Steps parse,validator,guards,docfacts,unit,report,envguard,launcher,campaign,gui-headless -Package -SkipGui`: 19 cases, 0 failed, 284 s.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 109 / 109; guard self-test OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43; unit 178 × 2; report-stage 103 × 2; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 125 / 125; headless GUI 4 / 4; package built from the tracked files: `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.5.zip`, 408 891 bytes, SHA256 `be32e608858dd2ccc1f40f9e2390ed0bf42b5dad75b00f6805d7f54006fc3a40`, the validator 109 / 109 from inside the extracted package. The real-window steps are not needed for a change that leaves the scripts' behaviour untouched, and the release asset will be CI's build of the tagged commit, as for 1.2.3 and 1.2.4. An earlier run of the same set was abandoned rather than reported: the guides' history sentence was written while it was running, so it was measuring a tree that no longer existed. The numbers above are from a run made after the tree settled, with the manifest regenerated first.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #28, rounds 1 to 4 · 2026-09-08: three findings, all accepted, and a clean pass on the fourth.** Every one of them landed on a sentence this release added, and each is written into the finding paragraph it belongs to above. *Round 1 on `b519d52`, P2:* the small-sample answer said a `WARN` row turns the verdict to Attention Required, without the qualifier the same manual already used one section earlier for the Startup Notice row. *Round 2 on `734978c`, P2:* round 1's qualifier was still too broad — `Get-OverallStatus` counts only rows outside the `IT` scope, so a failed IT collection does not outrank the warning, which `tests/report_stage_tests.ps1` scenario F asserts. *Round 3 on `0145172`, P2:* finding 6's answer offered the in-package `SHA256SUMS.txt` as what IT checks instead of a signature, and a manifest inside the same unsigned archive cannot establish the archive's origin; what does is the digest published with the release, which the IT deployment manual's section 6 has required since 1.2.4. That round corrected the walk's own prescription and not only its rendering: the walk entry below names the manifest, and it stays as written, because it records what the walk found. *Round 4 on `60b21d2`:* no findings. Each round's fix ran the validator (109 / 109) and the document-fact step (96 / 96 with its self-test 43 / 43) and regenerated `SHA256SUMS.txt`; the script-facing steps stand from the chain run above, since no round touched a script.

## Walking the user manual on a machine · 2026-09-08 — `win11-enUS`, the released 1.2.4 asset

**What this is.** The walk the 1.2.4 entries named as what was left: a person following `NetworkHealthCheck_User_Manual_en-US.html` on a virtual machine, with the released asset, along the path a user takes. The sheet is `tests/user-manual-walk-checklist.md` (74 rows, merged as `2472888`); this entry records what the walk produced. It was written as the walk proceeded; the closing paragraph says what it covered and what it did not.

**Machine and asset.** `DESKTOP-5M1K8VU`, the `win11-enUS` VM. The asset is the released `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4.zip`, SHA256 `5dbd1506…`, downloaded with that machine's own browser and extracted through Explorer's *Extract All* to `C:\Users\z1983\OneDrive\Desktop\NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4\NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4\` — the nested folder Extract All produces from a ZIP that carries its own top folder, which is what section 2 step 3 describes.

**What held.** The verdict line and its description — *Overall Healthy — All required checks that could be executed passed* — word for word as section 4's table has it. The IT diagnostics collapsed at the bottom of the report, as section 4 says, holding exactly the rows the window's log showed (`Wi-Fi radio`, `IPv4 default routes`, `Gateway neighbor (ARP)`, `Proxy settings`, `Traceroute (first hops)`, `Adapter drivers`) — the mapping is one to one, because `Add-CheckResult` writes the row and the log line in the same call, and the only log lines that are not rows are the run's own narration (`Starting: …`, the opening line, and the report-writing lines, which cannot be in a report that is already written). The launcher's missing-program-file path: the window's message, `LauncherError.txt` beside the launcher, and a *Suggested action* line that fits the reason — and, as the sheet predicted, the file says what to do and **not** whom to send it to, which is the manual's sentence and not the file's. The TCPv6 row refusing to conclude anything from a cumulative 88 retransmissions out of 176 sent when the window's delta was zero, which is what *“does not establish the long-term condition”* means.

**Finding 1 — section 9 names three causes for an unreadable retransmission counter, and not the one the tool imposes.** The first run of the session lost the TCPv4 counters: *Unable to Check*, `Microsoft.Management.Infrastructure.CimException`, **Timed out**, while every connectivity check passed; the sample window TCPv6 actually measured stretched to 13.3 s, and a run minutes later read both protocols and sampled 9.1 s. The counters are read with `Get-CimInstance -OperationTimeoutSec 8`, so the timeout is the tool's own, and section 9's answer — *“a permission, a policy or a broken performance counter”* — sends the reader after three causes, none of which is this one. Section 4's badge table is broader (“… or an execution error”) and holds. The tool half is [backlog #38](../docs/backlog.md); the manual half is a 1.2.5 fix.

**Finding 2 — section 9 quotes one of the three small-sample messages, and not the one that moves the verdict.** `Compare-TcpCounters` has three outcomes below the segment threshold (`MinimumTcpSegmentsForRate`, 50 by default): a zero delta gives *There was not enough TCP send traffic during the sample* (INFO), a small delta with no retransmissions gives *The sample contains only N sent segment(s)* (INFO), and a small delta **with** retransmissions gives *The traffic sample is small, but N retransmission(s) were observed* (**WARN**, so the verdict becomes Attention Required). The manual's FAQ names the first only, under a heading that does not match the second — which is what this machine produced (11 segments) — and says nothing about the third, which is the one that changes what the person is told. A 1.2.5 fix: the answer has to cover the family, and say that the warning case is a warning about the sample, not proof of a problem.

**Finding 3 — section 6's description of `LauncherError.txt` is one field short.** It names the time, the computer, the user, the reason, the folder and the program's path; the file also carries `PowerShell: C:\WINDOWS\System32\WindowsPowerShell\v1.0\powershell.exe`. The same family as the privacy inventory of PR #20, where an enumeration was twice found shorter than the thing it enumerated. A 1.2.5 fix.

**Finding 4 — the report leaves the computer when the package sits in a synced folder, and the manual names only the other way out.** The walk extracted the package to the Desktop, which on this machine is backed up by OneDrive — the Windows 11 default, confirmed by the owner — so `en-US\Reports\` is a synced folder and every report is copied to the cloud as it is written. The manual's promise is *“It uploads nothing. With the settings as shipped, the report is written to a folder on this computer and goes nowhere until you send it yourself; IT may have pointed the report folder elsewhere”*, and its FAQ answers *“Not by the tool”*. Both are literally true — the tool writes locally and OneDrive does the copying — but the sentence a reader takes away is that the report stays on the machine until they send it, and on a default Windows 11 desktop it does not. The manual names one way the report can leave (IT pointing the folder at a network location) and not the common one (the package extracted into a folder that is already synced), while the report itself holds the computer and user names, MAC and IP addresses, SSID and BSSID, DNS servers, proxy settings, routes, driver versions and the profile paths. A 1.2.5 fix in both manuals: the user manual's sections 1, 5 and 9 name the synced-folder case; the IT deployment manual's deployment section says where to put the package, or to set `ReportFolderName` to somewhere unsynced. **This is the finding of the walk so far**, and it is the kind five rounds of review could not reach: the sentences are right about the tool and wrong about the machine they are read on.

**Finding 5 — following section 2 puts the launchers one folder deeper than section 3 expects.** The released ZIP carries its own top-level folder, `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4\`, and Explorer's *Extract All* proposes a destination folder named after the ZIP — which section 2 step 3 endorses, correctly, as “that is fine”. The result is `…\NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4\NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4\`, which is what the walk got; section 3 step 1 then says *“Open the extracted folder and double-click `Start-English.cmd`”*, where the extracted folder holds one thing: another folder of the same name. **Nothing breaks.** Every path in both launchers is `%~dp0`-relative — `Start-English.cmd` looks for `%~dp0en-US\Start-NetworkCheck.cmd`, the language launcher for `%~dp0NetworkHealthCheck.ps1` — so the depth, the folder's name and its location are all irrelevant as long as the tree stays together, which is what section 2 step 4 already requires. The defect is that the first instruction after extracting does not describe the first thing the reader sees. Half a sentence in 1.2.5 fixes it. *Provenance, so the rows are read correctly: the walk's first extraction followed the manual and produced the doubled folder — that is W2's evidence; the pristine copy used for W36 and W37 was made by extracting, renaming the inner folder and copying it to the Desktop, a reshaping the manual explicitly allows, so those two rows ran on a tree with the same 31 files in a flatter layout.*

**A note under finding 4.** The Desktop of this machine is not `%USERPROFILE%\Desktop`. Known Folder Move puts it at `%OneDrive%\Desktop`, and a command line written with the older assumption — as one written during this walk was — simply fails to find the package. It is finding 4 seen from the other side, and it reaches further than the manual: a deployment script that copies the package to `%USERPROFILE%\Desktop`, or an IT instruction that says “put it on the desktop”, meets it too. `%OneDrive%` is what backlog #43 proposes to detect with, for the same reason.

**Finding 6 — the manual explains why the security warning appears and not what it says.** Section 3 step 2 and the section 6 row both treat *Open File - Security Warning* as the download mark: “That is the download mark from step 2 above; the tool is the same either way.” True, and the walk confirmed the title word for word. But the dialog Windows actually shows on this package says something else: *The publisher could not be verified. Are you sure that you want to run this software?*, **Publisher: Unknown Publisher**, and beneath it *This file does not have a valid digital signature that verifies its publisher. You should only run software from publishers you trust.* The manual has no answer to the question that raises, and it is the question a careful person stops on. It is the documentary half of backlog #22, where the decision not to sign is recorded: the IT deployment manual explains signing to IT, and the user manual — which is what is open at that moment — says nothing. Two sentences settle it: the tool is not digitally signed and `SHA256SUMS.txt` in the package is how IT verifies it instead; if IT sent you this ZIP, Run is the answer, and if you are not sure where it came from, ask before running it. The dialog's *Always ask before opening this file*, ticked by default, is worth the same clause — clearing it is an unblock by another route, and the manual describes only the Properties one. A 1.2.5 fix.

**What section 2's unblock claim is worth, measured both ways.** The manual says unblocking the ZIP before extracting removes the security question, and until this walk nothing had tested it. Blocked: the downloaded ZIP carried `Zone.Identifier` (976 bytes, the browser's, with the source URL in it), Explorer propagated a minimal 99-byte mark to the extracted `Start-English.cmd`, and the double-click produced *Open File - Security Warning*; Run started the tool normally. Unblocked: ticking the box cleared the ZIP's stream, the extraction carried no stream at all, and the double-click produced **no dialog of any kind**. Both halves hold, and W5 and W5b are the evidence. (An incidental tell, recorded because it explains an apparent inconsistency in the timestamps: the marked extraction's `Start-English.cmd` carries the extraction time, because Explorer writes the mark after creating the file, while the unmarked one keeps the entry time the asset builder stamped — backlog #21's build-time stamping seen from the outside.)

**Finding 1 recurred twice more, and the walk turned it into a pattern.** The retransmission counter read failed at 12:47 and again at 13:03, making three failures in the day's eight runs. All three were the first run inside a copy that had been extracted minutes earlier; none of the **five** runs that followed no extraction failed — the fifth being the control taken for W54 at 13:11, which read both protocols cleanly seven minutes after the third failure. That killed the cold-provider reading recorded in backlog #38 (the 12:47 run was the sixth of the day) and replaced it with a condition that can be reproduced deliberately — which is in the item, along with the two mechanisms that fit it equally. What the three failures rule out is the cold-provider **diagnosis**, not the warm-up **mechanism**: a query before the sample window is an extra attempt like any other and can absorb a load-driven transient, with the advantage that it spends that attempt outside the measured window. Each of the three also ended *Test Incomplete* on a network where every connectivity check passed, which is the case the owner asked about before any of them happened, and is now backlog #39's third instance.

**W54 — “It changes nothing”, measured for the first time.** The tool's first promise to the person who runs it — section 1 of the user manual, the first answer of its FAQ, the opening of the IT deployment manual, and the footer of every report it writes — had never been measured by anything in this project (backlog #42). It was measured here: six captures taken immediately before a normal *Run Again* and immediately after it finished — `ipconfig /all` (1308 bytes), `route print` (1977), `netsh winhttp show proxy` (77), the WinINET proxy values under `HKCU` (1521), `netsh advfirewall show allprofiles` (2147, which reads without elevation), and `Get-NetAdapter` name, status and admin status (71) — and **all six pairs are byte-identical**. Not one field moved, including the DHCP lease timestamps, which happened not to renew inside the window. What that establishes and what it does not: this release, on this machine, on one run, changed nothing that those six commands can see. It is not the chain step #42 asks for — a measurement made by hand on the day of a release proves the release and says nothing about the next change to the code, which is the whole point of automating it.

**Was it usable — the walker's answers.** The half of the sheet no assertion reaches. *On what the screen shows and the manual does not:* “輸入欄位格式的檢查，UI 上沒有說明或是範例，如果沒有仔細看手冊的話就會忽略。” — the IT panel's four free-text fields explain nothing and show nothing, so the format is invisible unless the manual is read closely. The label of the field that was actually mistyped does carry `(host:port)`, which makes the answer sharper than a complaint about missing text: a notation is not an example, and the walker read past the notation. It is recorded in backlog #45, whose placeholders are now specified as example values rather than a restatement of the shape. The same defect had already been reached from the other side hours earlier — a run spent, a verdict of *Attention Required* on a healthy network, and a summary telling the person to send the report to IT — so the walk found it twice, mechanically and humanly, and five rounds of independent review had found it neither way.

**An observation, not yet a finding.** `LauncherError.txt` stamps its time with the shell's `%DATE% %TIME%`, which on this machine reads `08/09/2026 11:09:54.54` — a date an IT department reading it cannot resolve without knowing the machine's locale. The launcher writes that file in cases where PowerShell may be missing, so it cannot format a date with PowerShell; whether the field should be labelled as the machine's own format, or filled another way, is open.


**What this walk covered, and what it did not.** Eight runs that produced a report — through the user entry, the console entry and the IT entry, from four extractions (the manual's own, a pristine copy never run, one from a still-blocked ZIP and one from an unblocked ZIP) — plus one launcher failure produced on purpose, which produced no report and was the point. Six findings, all of them the manual's and all of them for 1.2.5; ten backlog items, #38 to #47, of which #38 was corrected twice by its own evidence and #39 collected three instances; and one claim measured for the first time in this project's life. What it did not cover: the zh-TW manual on the zh-TW machine, which is the same walk again; the retransmission route into the quality branch, which needs traffic made while the sample runs — the loss and latency routes into the same branch need no help and simply did not fire on a clean link; and the failure classes that need the report machinery to break, which nothing here provoked.

**The usability half, in the walker's own words.** Two answers, and both became items. *What the screen does not explain*: the IT panel's fields carry no example, so the format is missed unless the manual is read closely — #45. *What to send and where*: **where** was answered by the buttons and not by the manual, **which** was not answered at all — #46. And a third, which is the one that orders the others: strengthen what the tool collects when it fails, *因為發生問題時可能 user 也說不清楚* — #47. The remaining questions were answered “nothing”, which is recorded as an answer: nothing had to be guessed, nothing was explained at length that was not needed, and no sentence beyond the ones already listed was true about the machine and wrong about the situation.

**How this walk was actually conducted, because the next one should know.** The sheet's *Observed* column stayed empty. The walk ran as a conversation against the sheet — a step taken, a screenshot or a command output produced, the claim checked against the code, the finding written down here — and the evidence therefore lives in this entry rather than in the sheet. That worked, and it produced more than a filled column would have, because a finding could be chased into the source the moment it appeared; but it means the sheet cannot be read afterwards to see which rows were reached. Either the next walk fills the column as it goes, or the sheet says plainly that the record is where the answers live. The sheet itself gained two rows' worth of lessons from being walked: the quality branch needs traffic made deliberately during the sampling seconds, and the log-to-report comparison has a cheap form that only works while the window is still open.

**What follows.** The six findings are a 1.2.5 documents fix — four manual files in two languages and two formats, the version bump the rule requires of a document that ships inside the package, and the validator's label check over all of it. The tool items wait their turn in `docs/backlog.md`. Nothing here changed the shipped 1.2.4 package, and nothing here needs to: every finding is a sentence, and the sentences ship with the next release.
## The backlog leaves the record · 2026-09-08 — `docs/backlog.md` (the two scripts untouched; four package documents and the manifest)

**Change.** The backlog — the numbered list this file has carried at its end since v1.1.1 — is now `docs/backlog.md` in the repository, beside `docs/application-control.md` and version-free like it. The twelve open items moved word for word, each keeping its number, its acceptance sentence and a note naming the review or the run that raised it, which is what the old grouping by origin carried and a numeric order would otherwise have lost; the twenty-four closed ones are one line each — what it was, and the release or the date that closed it — because the reason each is closed is proved by that release's entry in this file, and what is proved here should not be restated there. A pointer paragraph takes their place, 89 lines shorter.

**Why.** The list answered "what is still open?" only to somebody who read this record to its end. It began at line 984 of 1072 under a heading that still said *Known issues — backlog for v1.1.1*, grouped by the review that raised each item, so the numbers ran 21, 25, 26–28, 29–31, 22, 24, 23, 32, 33, 36, 34, 35 with two of the sub-headings identical, and the closed items were struck through in place. Measured on 2026-09-08: asked what was still open, this session answered by grepping the repository for `backlog #<n>` and rebuilding the statuses from commit subjects and release entries, and reported that the repository held no backlog list at all — which was wrong, and is the failure the page fixes. Open items first, in number order, with what would close each.

**What the move did not do.** No item was reworded, renumbered or closed, and no acceptance sentence was rewritten. Two items carry a dated **Status** line beneath their original text. #33: its first tier is closed — the document-fact step of 2026-09-07, the entry below — and the page names the two tiers that remain, tier 2 (the manuals' quoted report strings against a real report of that language) and tier 3 (the fingerprint chain's rule order against the field manual's table). #23: two of the three things it waits on have arrived without it being re-read — #19 closed on 2026-09-06 with M7 and M8 measured on two machines, and #28 shipped in 1.2.3 the per-reason suggestion in `LauncherError.txt` that the item guessed at — so what is left of it is the decision its second sentence asks for. The status line says so and decides nothing.

**What else changed.** `docs/application-control.md`'s first paragraph pointed at "the backlog at the end" of this file and now points at the page beside it. In the IT deployment manual, section 8's AppLocker row placed backlog #31 "in `VALIDATION.md`" and now names the page; section 10's related-documents list loses "the backlog" from the record's line and gains a line of its own for the page, by the tag URL of this release, as section 8's application-control link already does — four files, both languages, markdown and HTML, the Chinese line marked （英文）, as that manual marks its other links to English pages. `SHA256SUMS.txt` is regenerated over those four; it stays 28 lines. The repository README's roadmap table gains a backlog row that names the twelve open items by number.

**Version.** 1.2.4, unchanged. Neither script changed: the package change is one table cell and one list line per IT manual file.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #24, round 1 · 2026-09-08, on `697c3ea`: clean, no findings.** What a round on a move of this kind is for is the sentence that stopped resolving when it landed somewhere else — a "this machine" with no machine beside it, a link that only worked from the folder the record sits in, a package document still saying the record holds the list — and it found none. One round, no findings, nothing rejected or deferred. This paragraph is a documentation-only commit on top of the reviewed one.

**Chain run from the branch (Windows 11 10.0.26200, Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, Python 3.14.4) — the set GitHub Actions runs, `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Steps parse,validator,guards,docfacts,unit,report,envguard,launcher,campaign,gui-headless -Package -SkipGui`: 19 cases, 0 failed, 270 s.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 109 / 109; guard self-test OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); doc facts 96 / 96 in 2.5 s, its self-test 43 / 43 in 136.6 s; unit 178 × 2; report-stage 103 × 2; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 125 / 125 (103.2 s); headless GUI 4 / 4; package built from the tracked files: `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4.zip`, 389 679 bytes, SHA256 `aab936922576035f47b9eda5799c0e310eaa1bbf92c7c0c69d152df07e69264e`, the validator 109 / 109 inside the extracted package. The `docfacts` step reads the four edited documents and passes unchanged, which is what a move of this kind can be checked by: the section numbers, the file names and the links the manuals quote all still resolve. The real-window steps are not needed for a change that leaves the scripts untouched.

## The document-fact step · 2026-09-07 — backlog #33, tier 1 (`tests/`; one heading of the zh-TW IT deployment manual)

**Change** (closes the first tier of backlog #33). The manuals, the guides and the `sop/` field manual quote what the program defines — result tags, fingerprint keys, configuration keys, exit codes, launcher and program file names, and their own section numbers — and nothing kept those in step with the scripts except a person remembering, in four files at once (two languages × markdown and HTML), which is the failure mode every documents pull request has met. `tests/doc_facts.ps1` reads the identifiers out of the two shipped scripts and the two shipped configuration files and asserts both directions wherever both are sound; `tests/selftest_docfacts.ps1` is its negative set; the chain and GitHub Actions run the two as the `docfacts` step. **96 assertions over 16 documents in 3.4 s** — no run of the tool, no network, no Python.
