- **A · the two languages define the same identifiers.** Tags, fingerprint keys, exit codes and configuration keys, set against set, `en-US` against `zh-TW`; every fingerprint key the chain can select has a case in the switch that gives it a title, `healthy` being the default; and every `-Tag` argument resolves to a literal, so a tag this step cannot read is a failure rather than a silence.
- **B · the configuration file and the IT deployment manual.** Every key is quoted in each of the four manual files — including the keys inside a target list (`Name`, `Address`, `Required`, `Host`, `Port`, `Url`, `Group`), which is where an IT person makes most of their edits — every container is named, and every key of an example that is a configuration example exists in the configuration.
- **C, D · fingerprints and rows.** Each of the nine fingerprint keys is named in all six technical-guide copies and in both field-manual files. Each result tag is documented in the field manual except the six the step names as internal (`config`, `config-file`, `environment`, `startup`, `step-error`, `system`), so a tag added to the script forces a decision instead of slipping through, and a waiver the script no longer has is itself a failure.
- **E, F · what a document sends people to.** Every launcher or program file name it quotes is in the package, and every section number a markdown document cites exists in that document.

**Identifiers are read as code spans, never as prose:** `config` and `healthy` are ordinary words and a substring search would pass for the wrong reason. The `sop/` pages badge a fingerprint name where the manuals code it, and the reader accounts for both. What the step cannot check is what the review loop is for — a sentence that states a rule from one side only — so it replaces the mechanical half of that loop and none of the judgement: of the findings Codex raised on PR #20 and PR #21, this class covers the stale check count, the wrong section reference, the IT rows whose ERROR case the manual omitted and the recipe that forgot a required DNS entry, and none of the semantic ones.

**What it found on its first run.** The zh-TW IT deployment manual never names the configuration container `Thresholds`. Sections 3.2, 3.3 and 3.4 carry their key in the heading — 公司標準（`Expected`）, 測試目標（`Tests`）, 可選檢查（`Checks`） — and 3.5 was 門檻值 alone, because the English heading *is* the key and the translation dropped it: the zh-TW reader had the leaf names and no object to put them in. The heading reads 門檻值（`Thresholds`） now, in the markdown and in the HTML page; `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated over those two files, validator 109 / 109. Nothing else in the package changed, and no version label moved.

**Acceptance** (`tests/selftest_docfacts.ps1`, the step's second case). A copy of the package, of `sop/` and of `docs/` without the report folders, then eighteen mutations, each of which must fail **its own** check and be caught by that check's name: a tag added to one script; a fingerprint renamed in one script; a configuration key added to one file; a fingerprint the switch has no title for; a configuration key nobody documented; a renamed container; a phantom key in an example; a fingerprint dropped from a guide copy; an internal-tag waiver the script no longer has; a documented tag dropped from the field manual; a launcher name that is not in the package; a section number that does not exist; and, since round 1 of the review, a tag reached through a variable drifting between the languages, a `-Tag` argument that resolves to nothing, a section reference in the zh-TW spelling, the second number of a compound reference, a launcher name that carries its folder, and a program the package does not carry. The untouched copy passes before all of them and again after, which is what proves every mutation was put back, and it passes in both scopes (`-PackageOnly` drops the two `sop/` documents, for a run against an extracted asset).

**Three of the checks were wrong before the documents were.** The copy said so each time: the configuration walk did not descend into target lists, so seven real keys were reported as phantoms; the field manual's HTML badges its fingerprint names rather than coding them, so two keys were reported as undocumented; and the cross-document skip rule of F was too greedy in its first form — a 60-character window before the number swallowed a real mistake because the prose above it contained the word “manual”, which the self-test's own case caught. A check that has never been seen to fail is a check nobody has tested, and the mutation set is what makes the difference visible.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #23, round 1 · 2026-09-07, on `31ca3ed`: three findings, all accepted — every one of them a place where the step was reading less than it claimed to.** *P1 — a tag reached through a variable was invisible:* both scripts set `$pingTag` to `ping-target`, or to `ping-gateway` when the address is `AUTO_GATEWAY`, and emit it as `-Tag $pingTag`, so a pattern over the file text never saw those two live tags: A1 and D2 would have stayed green while they drifted between the languages or fell out of the field manual. The tags now come off the AST — every `-Tag` argument, with a variable resolved through the string constants assigned to it — and the ground truth goes from 24 tags to **26**; a new check, A6, fails when an argument cannot be resolved to a literal at all, because a tag this step cannot read is a tag it is not checking. *P2 — the section-reference matcher read one English form:* the zh-TW manuals write their references between the two characters that bracket a section number in Chinese, and English documents also write compound ones (`sections 3.6 and 8`), so a mistyped number in a zh-TW manual met no check whatsoever and the second number of a compound was never read. Both spellings are matched now and every number of a reference is checked; the four zh-TW markdown documents contribute 32 references where they contributed none. *P2 — the file-name check was anchored to bare basenames and excluded `.py`:* `tools\validate_release.py` and `en-US\Start-NetworkCheck-IT.cmd` were filtered out before the existence check, so a misspelling passed. A quoted name is now resolved with its folder when it carries one, `.py` is in the set, and a path that resolves in the checkout counts as well — the technical guide points at `docs/design-v1.2-triage-wizard.md` *in the repository*, which is a real file the package does not carry.

**Two of the three fixes needed a second correction, and the copy said so.** Widening the file-name shape turned the IT manual's own shorthand into failures — its table of paths writes `…\Start-NetworkCheck.cmd`, `-IT.cmd`, `-Console.cmd`, and a name has to begin with a letter or a digit for a suffix fragment not to be read as one — and the Chinese skip rule did not know the word the zh-TW manuals use for the technical guide, so two genuine cross-document references were reported as broken. Neither reached a commit. The self-test gained six cases for the forms the review named (a variable-backed tag drifting, a `-Tag` that resolves to nothing, a reference in the zh-TW spelling, the second number of a compound one, a launcher name carrying its folder, a program the package does not have) and one of the twelve it already had turned out to be measuring nothing once the AST replaced the regular expression: it appended a commented-out call, which the AST is right to ignore. Eighteen mutations now, and `docs/` joins the copy because the step resolves a repository path against it.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #23, round 2 · 2026-09-07, on `847e85c`: three findings, all accepted — the same theme one layer in, each of them a place where round 1's own fix stopped short.** *P1 — a variable was called resolved on the strength of one literal:* the assignment scan collected the string constants it found and ignored everything else, so a tag variable that is `"ping-target"` in one place and `Get-CustomTag` in another looked resolved, and the tag that call returns would have sat outside A1, A6 and D2 while all three stayed green. A variable now resolves only when **every** assignment to it is a plain string literal; any other assignment is recorded with its text and its line, and A6 fails naming it. *P2 — the zh-TW compound repeats its marker:* the manuals write the equivalent of "sections 3.6 and 8" with the marker in front of both numbers, and round 1's pattern allowed only a bare number after the first one, so it matched the last reference alone and a typo in the first number passed; the marker may repeat now, and the zh-TW IT manual's reference count goes from 32 to 33. *P2 — an executable inside a command span was skipped whole:* the field manual quotes `powershell -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File NetworkHealthCheck.ps1 -ConsoleOnly …`, and an anchored pattern rejects that span before any check, so misspelling the program in the one command line a reader copies left E1 green. Command-shaped spans are tokenized now and their `.ps1`, `.cmd` and `.py` tokens resolved like any other name — the field manual goes from 5 names to 6. Three cases were added for the three forms; the mutation set stands at **twenty-one**.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #23, round 3 · 2026-09-07, on `9697466`: four findings, all accepted — four more places where a document said something the step never read.** *P2 — exit codes:* only a literal `exit N` was collected, so both scripts reported the single code 3 while their entry points end on `exit $exitCode`, which is 0, 1, or whatever `Start-ConsoleMode` returned; changing one language's `$exitCode = 1` to 2 left A3 green. Exit statements are read off the AST now, with the variable resolved through the literals assigned to it — **0 / 1 / 3** — and A3b compares the two languages on the *shape* of what is not a literal (`Start-ConsoleMode`), so a change in either form is caught. *P2 — D2 read the markdown field manual alone:* a tag dropped from the HTML page and not from the markdown was precisely the drift this step exists for, and it passed. D2 runs over both formats now, and the page needed fixing to meet it: its row inventory named the rows in plain text where the markdown codes them, so five of them (`adapter`, `dns-config`, `proxy`, `routes`, `traceroute`) were invisible to a reader that only accepts identifiers as code. Twelve rows now carry `<code>`, which is what the page already did everywhere else. *P2 — parent-relative references:* every technical-guide copy points at `../VALIDATION.md`, and the pattern accepted only a bare name or one of four folder prefixes, so a typo there passed; a path is now read from the referring document's own folder first. *P2 — the section sign:* the field manual writes `§4`, `§6`, `§3` throughout and F1 covered only the English word and the Chinese marker, so `§99` would have passed. Five self-test cases were added for the five forms; the mutation set stands at **twenty-six**.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #23, round 4 · 2026-09-07, on `c1d2855`: four findings, all accepted; the step goes from 59 assertions to 80.** *P2 — the exit code was followed one step and stopped:* round 3 resolved `$exitCode` to 0, 1 and 3 and recorded `Start-ConsoleMode` as a shape, but the function's own `return 0` and `return 1` were never read, so changing one language's return to 2 would have changed what the operating system is told while A3 and A3b both stayed green. A call to a function of the same script is followed into it now, and its literal returns are exit codes too. *P2 — fenced blocks were deleted before the file names were read:* both IT deployment manuals put `NetworkHealthCheck.ps1`, `Start-NetworkCheck-IT.cmd` and `tools\validate_release.py` inside blocks a reader copies whole, and those were the one place E1 did not look. *P2 — link destinations were never checked at all:* the field manual links to `network-troubleshooting-sop.md`, `support-report-template.md` and their pages, and a broken `href` left readers with a dead link and the step silent. **E2** resolves every local link from the folder its document sits in — 16 more assertions, and it covers the HTML pages as well as the markdown. *P2 — F1 ran on markdown only:* the shipped HTML manuals carry their own references, in both languages, and the HTML field manual its `§` ones, all maintained by hand beside the markdown; they are read now from the page's own headings, with the stylesheet and any script removed first because neither is prose. Four self-test cases were added; the mutation set stands at **thirty**.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #23, round 5 · 2026-09-07, on `53facb6`: four findings, all accepted; 80 assertions become 96.** *P2 — the exit codes were compared between the scripts and never against the documents:* a manual that mistypes “exit code 3”, or a code that changes in both scripts while the manuals stay as they are, met nothing. **A7** reads every exit code a document quotes — in English and in the zh-TW form — and requires it to be a code the package can produce, the two scripts and the launchers together (0, 1, 3). The other direction is deliberately left alone: 0 is not a fact a manual is expected to name. *P2 — a link was resolved from anywhere in the package:* E2 fell back to the leaf-name list, so a `sop/` link to a technical guide passed because that name exists under `healthcheck/en-US`, although the browser would look beside the document and find nothing. A link resolves from its own folder now and nowhere else — the check was agreeing with itself. *P2 — a dot-relative program name was rejected before the check:* `.\NetworkHealthCheck.ps1` is how a copied Windows command spells it, and quoting is normal too, so both are stripped before a token is matched. *P2 — a single-quoted `href` was not collected at all,* which would have made a broken link *reduce* the reported link count instead of failing. Four self-test cases; **thirty-four mutations**. One of the fixes needed the two document readers moved up to the other helpers, since A7 calls them and Windows PowerShell resolves a function only after it is defined — the checker died on its first run and said so.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #23, round 6 · 2026-09-07, on `2c84d97`: two findings, both accepted, both on A7 — the check round 5 added.** *P2 — the codes were pooled across producers:* A7 asked whether anything in the package could return the number, so the validator's documented failure code could have been 3, which only the PowerShell script produces. The validator is read as a third producer now (`sys.exit` in `tools/validate_release.py`: 0 and 1), and the paragraph a code sits in decides which set it is measured against — a look-back window of 600 characters for the file's own name, the same shape as F1's rule for a reference into another document. *P2 — the extraction read a subset of the sentence forms the documents already use:* the switches table says “the process exits 0 when a report was written, 1 when none could be” and only the 0 was read; the technical guides write “exits with code 1”, which matched nothing; and the zh-TW manuals write the number before the verb rather than after “exit code”, so that row contributed nothing at all in Chinese. All three forms are read now, and the IT deployment manual goes from 2 quoted codes to **4** in each language. Four self-test cases, one per form and one for the producer scoping; **thirty-eight mutations**.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #23, round 7 · 2026-09-07, on `4b7965d`: two findings, both accepted, both on A7 again.** *P2 — a number inside a code span was not a number:* the field manual writes the launcher's code as `` `1` ``, and a pattern that wanted a digit after the space read the markdown and the HTML page differently — the page, whose tags are stripped, was checked and the markdown was not. Markup is removed before the scan now, and both formats report the same six codes. *P2 — the launcher is a producer too:* a sentence that gives a code to the launcher was still measured against everything the package can return, so “the launcher exits 3” would have passed on a code only the program produces. The launcher's own set applies now, and **the verb is what decides**: “the launcher exits 1” is its own code, while “the launcher explains exit code 3” and “‘exit code 1’ in the launcher and ‘exit code 3’ in the message” are the program's, which the launcher only reports — the pattern binds the number to the verb rather than to the word, which is what keeps those two sentences from failing. Two self-test cases; **forty mutations**.

**Chain run from the branch (Windows 11 10.0.26200, Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, Python 3.14.4) — the set GitHub Actions runs, `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Steps parse,validator,guards,docfacts,unit,report,envguard,launcher,campaign,gui-headless -Package -SkipGui`: 19 cases, 0 failed, 283 s.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 109 / 109; guard self-test OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); **doc facts 96 / 96 in 3.4 s, its self-test 43 / 43 in 151 s** — the self-test is the most expensive step in the chain now, ahead of the campaign one, because every mutation gets a process of its own; grouping them would cost that independence, so it is left as it is until the cost matters — the self-test spends its time starting one Windows PowerShell per mutation, which is what keeps each case independent; unit 178 × 2; report-stage 103 × 2; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 125 / 125 (111 s); headless GUI 4 / 4; package built from the tracked files: `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4.zip`, 400 954 bytes, SHA256 `d5ff589bb0c9c7c11614cfca6e84ef5be56ca6cf0f5275755c1c8ece6752bfd7`, the validator 109 / 109 inside the extracted package.

**Version.** 1.2.4, unchanged. The step lives in `tests/`, which ships nothing; the one package change is a heading.

**Not in this change.** Tier 2 (the manuals' quoted report strings against a real report of that language, which the `acceptance` step already produces) and tier 3 (the fingerprint chain's rule order against the field manual's table). The Codex loop on the pull request follows this entry.

## v1.2.4 — the IT deployment manual inside the package · 2026-09-07 — documents release, part 2 (the tool unchanged)

**What and why.** The second half of the decision recorded in part 1 below: the IT deployment manual joins the package in both languages, as markdown and as one HTML page, and `README_*.txt` — the IT configuration notes since part 1 — is retired, its content absorbed. Neither script changed; `FUNCTION_COUNT` stays 81; the version stays 1.2.4.

**The manual.** `en-US/NetworkHealthCheck_IT_Deployment_Manual_en-US.md` with its `.html`, and `zh-TW/NetworkHealthCheck_IT_Deployment_Manual_zh-TW.md` with its `.html` — ten sections: what you are deploying (requirements, the three entries, what it contacts as shipped), before you hand it out (a seven-step checklist), the configuration file key by key (identity and the report folder; `Expected`; `Tests` with the required / optional / group table, the timeouts and the recipe for a site without outbound internet; `Checks`; `Thresholds` with the rule each row applies; what the report says about the configuration), run options (the IT panel and the switches, what an extra target counts for, where a run's options are recorded), deploying (the whole folder, the two ways it arrives and the Mark of the Web, where to put it, upgrading, the program file), verifying the package (the asset digest, the manifest, the validator, the record), the reports (the handling rule, the JSON for tools), security policy (one row per mechanism with what happens and what reaches IT, allowing by hash, signing), the files, related documents. Every rule was read off the function that implements it before it was written — `Load-Configuration` and `Merge-ConfigObject`, `Test-ConfigurationSemantics`, `Initialize-OutputDirectory`, `Test-ExpectedNetworkConfiguration` with `Get-PrimaryAdapters`, `Test-PingTargets`, `Test-DnsNames`, `Test-ConnectivityTargets` with `Add-ConnectivityGroupResult`, `Compare-AdapterStatistics`, `Compare-TcpCounters`, `Set-RunOptions` with `Get-RunProfileText`, `Add-TracerouteResult`, `Add-ProxySettingsResult`, `Write-EnvironmentReport` and the guard ahead of it, `Save-Reports`, `Start-ConsoleMode`, the six launchers — and every statement about policy is taken from the two machine entries below (scenarios M2, M3, M7, M8, M9) and backlog #31, with "not measured" said where it is so: a WDAC-enforced machine, AppLocker taking effect, an EDR product.

**The volatile facts stay outside the package.** `docs/application-control.md` in the repository holds what has been measured mechanism by mechanism, the AppLocker facts (KB 5024351, the M9 outcome and its open questions) and how the tool can be allowed by hash or by signing. The manual links to it by the tag of its release (`blob/v1.2.4/…`), which is a version label the validator checks, and the page names `main` for its current version. The reason was recorded on 2026-09-06: the AppLocker premise was overturned twice in one day with the tool unchanged, so the in-package manual states only what the tool does and sends the reader to the page for what Windows does.

**What moved.** `README_en-US.txt` and `README_zh-TW.txt` are deleted; their run options, company standards, service tests, thresholds and deployment notes are the manual's sections 4, 3.2, 3.3, 3.5 and 5. The technical guides' package table (six copies) describes the IT manual "since 1.2.4" where it described the README, their 1.2.4 sentence in §7.2 says both manuals joined and the READMEs retired, and §9 points at the manual; the user manuals (four files) point at the IT manual where they pointed at the README; `README_BILINGUAL.md` names it; the repository README's IT paragraph and its policy note point at the manual and at the repository page; the field manual's two "package README" references and its related-documents list; the package-acceptance runner's layout check requires the four files.

**The validator.** The four IT manual files are required, the two English ones are checked for CJK (the English README leaves that list), and the every-label rule covers the four IT manual files (both READMEs leave it). 100 → 109 checks; the manifest 26 → 28 lines. Proved on copies of the package: the en-US markdown's footer at 1.2.5 fails its label check (`1.2.4, 1.2.5`); the zh-TW HTML's repository link at `v1.2.3` fails its label check (`1.2.3, 1.2.4`); the en-US HTML missing fails required-file, CJK, label and manifest; one CJK character appended to the en-US markdown fails the CJK check; a stray `README_en-US.txt` saying 1.2.3 is no longer checked, because it is no longer shipped; the untouched copy passes 109 / 109. Each mutation also fails that file's manifest line, as it must.

**Checked two ways.** Against the code, above. Against the tool: a walk of the manual's configuration claims from a staged en-US copy in console mode — 11 cases, 56 assertions, 56 passed, 0 failed, about four minutes — the shipped configuration's first rows and the JSON's keys; a malformed file; `-ConfigPath` to a missing file and to a candidate (with `OrganizationName` in the HTML header and nowhere else); the no-internet recipe (no traceroute row, no row naming 1.1.1.1 or www.microsoft.com, one `Group: Company` row, ERP System FAIL and Company portal INFO, the run profile reading `disabled: Traceroute`); a required group without members (ERROR, Test Incomplete); the five comparison rows against unmet standards; six threshold warnings in one Thresholds row and no Validation row; five invalid values in one Validation row, the invalid required TCP target's own ERROR row, the invalid optional one skipped, the malformed URL attempted and failing; the switches (the `bad` TCP target dropped with its Startup Notice, Extra ping INFO, Extra DNS WARN, Extra URL INFO, `RawTargets` keeping what `ExtraTargets` dropped, the run-profile line, Attention Required); a rooted report folder and an unwritable one (the `%TEMP%` fallback with its notice); a list in the file replacing the shipped list, a one-key `Checks` keeping the other defaults, an unknown key ignored, and the traceroute probing 1.1.1.1 with only `AUTO_GATEWAY` configured. The walk changed the manual once — a malformed URL that is not blank is attempted and fails, not skipped (section 3.6) — and a self-review of every absolute sentence before it changed ten more, in all four files: what the tool writes (its reports and the error files, not "nothing outside the report folder"), the non-Windows row (after the configuration rows, not alone in the report), "every launcher" (the six language launchers; the root launchers look for a folder), "every physical adapter" (connected), where the Standard Configuration row sits (with the comparison, not at the top), and "nothing else changes" after a configuration edit (as long as the file is still valid JSON in the shipped form).

**Chain run from the branch (Windows 11 10.0.26200, Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, Python 3.14.4) — the set GitHub Actions runs, `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Steps parse,validator,guards,unit,report,envguard,launcher,campaign,gui-headless -Package -SkipGui`: 17 cases, 0 failed, 271 s.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 109 / 109; guard self-test OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); unit 178 × 2; report-stage 103 × 2; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; campaign self-test 125 / 125 (210 s); headless GUI 4 / 4; package built from the tracked files: `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4.zip`, 385 288 bytes, SHA256 `a1c99b88dfda2dc921396c4088de278376707381a5de4c5fff3d2aa391cdb6cd`, the validator 109 / 109 inside the extracted package. The real-window steps are not needed for a change that leaves the scripts' behaviour untouched; the release asset will be CI's build of the tagged commit, as for 1.2.3. A first run of the same set had failed its package step, 107 / 109 inside the built ZIP, because the manuals were edited while the campaign step ran and the manifest in the ZIP predated the edit — a race of this session's making, not a defect, recorded here so that the count is explained.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #21, round 1 · 2026-09-07, on `31c79d9`: two findings, both accepted.** *P2 — "Information rows" claimed too much for the IT diagnostics:* section 3.4 called every IT-scoped row an Information row, but when a collection fails — `netsh` for the wireless data, `Get-NetRoute` for the routes, `Get-NetNeighbor` for the neighbour table, the traceroute — the `Add-*Result` functions write an `ERROR` row (*Unable to Check*), and `Invoke-CheckStep` writes one for the whole IT step if it throws; their `IT` scope keeps all of them out of the verdict, which is the part the manual had right. The sentence now says the rows are Information when the collection succeeded and Unable to Check when it did not, never counted either way — four files, both languages — and the same family in the technical guides' §4.10 ("are `INFO` only", followed one paragraph later by the sentence that says a failed collection is Unable to Check) now reads "`INFO` rows when their collection succeeds (`ERROR` when it does not)", six copies. *P3 — a wrong cross-reference:* the checklist's step 6 sent the reader to section 5 (Deploying) for the report's first rows; they are in section 3.6, and every other section reference in the manual was checked against the headings while fixing it (all correct, both languages). Validator 109 / 109 on the tree and inside the rebuilt package (`NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4.zip`, 386 092 bytes, SHA256 `a49434f8…`); `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated. Round 2 followed on the owner's instruction.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #21, round 2 · 2026-09-07, on `7378c74`: two findings, both accepted, one of them opening a backlog item.** *P2 — the switches table gave the script's split rule as command-line syntax:* "several values separated by commas, semicolons or spaces" is what `Set-RunOptions` does to one value; from a shell those separators do not all reach the script as one value. Measured on the reference machine from cmd.exe (`powershell -File`, arguments as literal strings) and from a PowerShell prompt (arguments parsed by PowerShell, sent through `-EncodedCommand` so that no outer quoting interferes): `-PingTarget a,b` and `-PingTarget "a b"` give two targets from both shells; `-PingTarget a;b` gives two from cmd.exe and one from a PowerShell prompt, where the semicolon ends the statement and `b` runs as a command; `-HttpUrl "u1 u2"` gives two from both shells, `-HttpUrl u1,u2` two from a PowerShell prompt (an array) and one invalid URL from cmd.exe (the script never splits URLs on commas); and a bare second value — `-PingTarget a b`, `-HttpUrl u1 u2` — is bound to `-ConfigPath` from both shells, because the script's parameters are positional in declaration order and `-ConfigPath` is the first non-switch: the run records *Configuration file not found: b*, uses the built-in defaults, ends Test Incomplete and tests the first value only. The table now gives one form per row that works from both shells (a comma-separated list; a quoted, space-separated value for URLs) and a note below it names the two forms to avoid and what each does; the technical guides' §4.10 says the separators apply inside one value and names the two traps (six copies). The positional binding itself is **backlog #36**, a change to both scripts. *P3 — the Thresholds row promised a replacement value for every warning:* an ordering warning (`Packet-loss thresholds are not ordered correctly: Warning=30, Critical=20.`) states the pair and nothing else, because those values are used as written, as section 3.5 says; the row now says the replacement is named only for a value that was replaced. Four files, both languages. Validator 109 / 109 on the tree and inside the rebuilt package (`NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4.zip`, 389 834 bytes, SHA256 `d6f8ff73…`); `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated. Two rounds, four findings, all accepted, one deferred to the backlog as a tool change (#36). Round 3 followed on the owner's instruction.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #21, round 3 · 2026-09-07, on `83818e9`: two findings, both accepted.** *P2 — the recipe for a site without outbound internet forgot the shipped DNS target:* it removed the public ping, TCP and HTTP targets and the `Internet` group, but the shipped `DnsNames` entry for `www.microsoft.com` is required (`Required` defaults to `true` for DNS entries, as the manual's own table says two paragraphs up), so a site whose resolver does not answer public names would follow the recipe and still get Problem Detected. The recipe now says to replace that lookup with an internal name the resolver answers — four files, both languages. The walk's own no-internet case had replaced the DNS entry and so did not catch the recipe's omission: a walk tests the configuration the walker wrote, not the sentence the reader follows. *P2 — `docs/application-control.md` contradicted itself on Windows Home:* one sentence said every Windows 11 enforces AppLocker without regard to edition, the next that Home has no AppLocker at all. Microsoft's *Requirements to use AppLocker* (fetched 2026-09-07) separates the two: enforcement needs no specific edition on Windows 10 version 2004 and newer and on Windows 11 with KB 5024351 (older builds: Group Policy deployment on Enterprise and Server only, MDM deployment on all editions), while creating the rules needs "a device running a supported operating system to create the rules" — and Home ships neither `gpedit.msc` nor `secpol.msc`, which is where the campaign's M9 authors its policy. The page now says so, states that the driver's skip on Home is about local authoring, and that enforcement of an MDM-delivered policy on Home has not been measured. The checklist's "Any edition except Home" and the driver's "AppLocker is not available on this edition" describe the same authoring limit and are left as they are (`tests/`, outside this change); the sentence "Home has no AppLocker at all" survives in the machine-1 entry below as history. Validator 109 / 109 on the tree and inside the rebuilt package (`NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4.zip`, 390 110 bytes, SHA256 `4bb6ced5…`); `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated. Three rounds, six findings, all accepted, one deferred to the backlog (#36), none rejected; every round has landed on a sentence that stated a rule from one side only — a row status without its failure case, a split rule without the shell in front of it, a recipe without the one target it did not name, an edition rule without the authoring tools it presumes. Round 4 followed on the owner's instruction.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #21, round 4 · 2026-09-07, on `e47aaeb`: two findings, both accepted.** *P2 — "each run writes three files" and "the folder grows by three files per run" held only when every format could be written:* `Save-Reports` writes each format on its own and a failed one is reported (the window's *N of 3 report formats could not be written*, the console's *(not written)*), the others stay usable, and only when all three fail is the emergency `NetworkHealthCheck_FATAL_<time>.txt` written instead — the user manual's section 6 already said so. Section 3.1 now says "up to three files", names the two messages and the emergency file. *P2 — round 1's own fix stated the IT rows from one side again:* "Information when the collection succeeded and Unable to Check when it did not" left out the Information rows that report no collection at all — `netsh.exe` not found or no connected wireless interface (`Add-WifiRfResult`), `Get-NetRoute` not available or no default route (`Add-RouteTableResult`), no gateway to look up (`Add-GatewayNeighborResult`), no connected physical adapter (`Add-DriverInfoResult`) — while `ERROR` is written only when reading a source throws. Section 3.4 now says what an Information row there carries (what was collected, or that there was nothing to collect or no source on this machine, with the cases) and that Unable to Check means the read threw; the technical guides' §4.10 says the same in one clause, six copies. Four files, both languages. Validator 109 / 109 on the tree and inside the rebuilt package (`NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4.zip`, 392 414 bytes, SHA256 `39a1dc33…`); `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated. Four rounds, eight findings, all accepted, one deferred (#36), none rejected; the second finding of this round corrected a fix from round 1, the shape PR #17 and PR #19 taught — a replacement sentence needs the same check against the code as the sentence it replaces. Rounds 5 to 7 were authorized together on the owner's instruction, to stop at the first clean pass and merge.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #21, round 5 · 2026-09-07, on `519fdce`: two findings, both accepted.** *P2 — "the same options as parameters of the script" claimed parity the command line does not have:* the script exposes `-NoWifi` and `-NoTraceroute` only; the panel's other four boxes (Routes, Gateway ARP, Proxy, Drivers) reach `Set-RunOptions` as a hashtable the window builds and have no switch, so a scripted run that copied a panel configuration would meet a binding error. Section 4 now says "most of the panel's options", names the four without a switch and sends a console run to a configuration file passed with `-ConfigPath`; the `-NoTraceroute` / `-NoWifi` row says they are the only two. *P2 — "a flag that is not true or false disables that check and is reported" — not for `null`:* `Merge-ConfigObject` merges an explicit `null` over the built-in `true` (the property exists, its value is null), `Test-IsTrueFlag` then disables the check, and `Test-ConfigurationSemantics` validates only non-null values, so the diagnostic disappears without a row. The same holds for a `null` threshold, which `ConvertTo-DoubleSafe` replaces by its default while the semantic check skips it. Sections 3.4 and 3.5 now say that a `null` disables the check, or takes the default, silently. Four files, both languages. Validator 109 / 109 on the tree and inside the rebuilt package (`NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4.zip`, 393 542 bytes, SHA256 `7c363cf5…`); `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated. Five rounds, ten findings, all accepted, one deferred (#36), none rejected. Round 6 followed.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #21, round 6 · 2026-09-07, on `5d858dd`: one finding, accepted.** *P2 — "a signature from your own certificate authority would satisfy AllSigned" skipped the trust step:* `AllSigned` requires scripts signed by a *trusted* publisher, and PowerShell prompts before running a script whose publisher is not yet classified as trusted (Microsoft, *about_Execution_Policies*), so a signature chaining to an internal CA whose certificate is not in the machine's Trusted Publishers store leaves the person with a question in the launcher's window, and a session that cannot ask without the script. Section 8's *Signing* paragraph, and the repository page's allowing-by-signing bullet, now say the signature satisfies the policy when the certificate is also trusted on the machine and what happens when it is not. Four files, both languages, plus `docs/application-control.md`. Validator 109 / 109 on the tree and inside the rebuilt package (`NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4.zip`, 394 375 bytes, SHA256 `b78a2430…`); `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated. Six rounds, eleven findings, all accepted, one deferred (#36), none rejected. Round 7 followed, the last of the authorized block.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #21, round 7 · 2026-09-07, on `365a56a`: clean** — "Didn't find any major issues", zero inline comments, the first clean pass of this pull request. **Seven rounds, eleven findings, all accepted, one deferred to the backlog as a tool change (#36), none rejected.** The shape of the loop, for the next document: every finding but one was a sentence that stated a rule from one side — a row status without its failure or its no-data case, a split rule without the shell in front of it, a recipe without the one target it did not name, an edition rule without the authoring tools it presumes, a signature without the trust it needs — and one (round 4) corrected round 1's own fix. Two findings were settled by measurement rather than reading (the shell forms of round 2, which also found backlog #36), one by the vendor's page (round 3). The CI check on `365a56a` is green; this paragraph is a documentation-only commit on top of the reviewed one, and the pull request is merged on the owner's standing instruction for this block ("if no new problems, merge").

**Version.** 1.2.4, unchanged, in both scripts, the packaged validator, the front page, the four manuals, the six guide copies and the repository README; `SHA256SUMS.txt` regenerated over 28 files.

**Not in this change.** The tag; the walk of the user manual by a person on a virtual machine; backlog #33's document-fact step. The Codex loop on the pull request follows this entry.

## v1.2.4 — the end-user manual inside the package · 2026-09-06 — documents release, part 1 (the tool unchanged)

**What and why.** The decision of 2026-09-06, taken at the end of the 1.2.3 work: three manuals, each as markdown and as one HTML page. The support-engineer manual and the report template went to `sop/`, version-free (PR #17, #19); the end-user manual and the IT deployment manual go *inside* the package, because the person who needs them has the ZIP and nothing else, and a document inside the package takes the package's version — hence 1.2.4. This entry is the first half: the end-user manual in both languages, and the bump it forces. Neither script changed beyond its version string; `FUNCTION_COUNT` stays 81.

**The manual.** `en-US/NetworkHealthCheck_User_Manual_en-US.md` with its `.html`, and `zh-TW/NetworkHealthCheck_User_Manual_zh-TW.md` with its `.html` — nine sections: what the tool is, before you start, running the check, reading the result, sending the report to IT, if something does not work, the IT entry, the files in the package, questions people ask. Every string the person sees is quoted from the shipped scripts and launchers rather than paraphrased: the window title and its labels, the four verdicts with the descriptions the tool prints, the nine "What to tell IT" titles with their lines, the five result badges, the report's section titles, the launcher's error and suggested-action texts, the four startup notices, the three dialogs, the report file names. The two languages are one document twice, each quoting its own script; the HTML pages use the `sop/` design system and colour the badges the way the report does.

**Where the content came from, and what moved.** The user-facing half of `README_*.txt` — quick start, what it checks, error handling, report reading, security and privacy, the FAQ — is absorbed by the manual, so that no claim lives in two places (the failure mode PR #17 and PR #19 kept finding). The file stays, as the IT configuration notes it always half was — run options, company standards, service tests, thresholds, deployment notes — until the IT deployment manual absorbs those in turn and retires it. The technical guide's package table gains the manual's row and re-describes the README row, and its version history gains the 1.2.4 sentence; `README_BILINGUAL.md` names the manual; the repository README and the field manual in `sop/` point at the manual's §5 where they pointed at the README's privacy section; the package-acceptance runner's layout check requires the four files.

**Checked two ways, the rule PR #17 left.** *Against the code:* each claim was read off the function that implements it before it was written — `Get-OverallStatus`, `Get-FingerprintSummary`, `Get-StatusText`, `Initialize-Gui`, `Run-AllChecks`, `Complete-ReportStage`, `Initialize-OutputDirectory`, `Write-EnvironmentReport`, `Set-RunOptions`, the six launchers — and each rule is stated only as absolutely as the code makes it: **Close** is refused while a run is in progress because both the click handler and `FormClosing` test `IsRunning`; an added target that fails outright is an `INFO` row; a startup notice on its own turns the verdict to Attention Required, because it is a `WARN` row in `Main` scope and `Get-OverallStatus` counts it. *Against real reports:* the chain's acceptance step on this checkout — the en-US and zh-TW user runs and both languages against unreachable targets — has the structure the manual describes (header, verdict box, six counters, "What to tell IT", Computer and Run Information, Test Results with Show Details and Expand all / Collapse all, IT diagnostics collapsed) and the timing it gives (about ten seconds: 9 s each on the reference machine). The walk found one thing the code reading had not: when the graphical interface cannot start and the run through the user launcher falls back to console mode, the launcher exits 0 without a pause, so the report paths the console printed close with the window. The manual sends the person to the `Reports` folder and, for a run whose text should stay on screen, to the console launcher; the gap itself is backlog #34. The Codex loop on the pull request follows this entry.

**The validator.** `tools/validate_release.py` requires the four manual files, checks the en-US manual (markdown and HTML) for CJK like the other English files, and checks the version labels of every document that carries one: the front page, both READMEs and both manuals in both formats may name no tool version but this one — a manual carries it in its title, its heading, the window name it quotes and its footer, and a bump that fixes the first line alone fails here — while the six guide copies, which carry the version history, must name this version in their first line and in the bold version of their purpose paragraph (the only bold version label a guide carries), and nothing newer anywhere. A version label is three dotted numbers, each below 1000, that are not part of a longer dotted run, so an IP address, a CIDR, a Windows build or a PowerShell version is not one, a full stop after the label does not hide it, and a label from an earlier major version is still one. The packaged validation record — this file, outside the manifest by design because it is the last file a release touches — must have a `## v<version>` entry for this version and none for a newer one, and that entry's own **Version.** paragraph must name the version too. That closes the gap found on 2026-09-06 while planning this release: until now only `$script:ToolVersion` in the two scripts was asserted, and the 1.2.3 bump touched nine documents by hand with nothing checking them. 62 → 100 checks; `SHA256SUMS.txt` lists 26 files. The rule was proved on a copy of the package: a manual whose footer alone says 1.2.5, a zh-TW HTML manual whose quoted window name still says 1.2.3, a guide whose history names 1.2.5, a guide whose purpose paragraph still says 1.2.3 under the right heading, and a front page saying 1.2.5 each fail exactly their check, a record whose 1.2.4 heading is renamed, a record given a v1.2.5 entry and a record whose 1.2.4 entry keeps a Version paragraph saying 1.2.3 each fail their record check, and the copy unchanged passes 100 / 100.

**Chain run from the branch (Windows 11 10.0.26200, Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, Python 3.14.4) — the set GitHub Actions runs, `Invoke-ValidationChain.ps1 -Steps parse,validator,guards,unit,report,envguard,launcher,campaign,gui-headless -Package -SkipGui`: 17 cases, 16 passed, 1 failed, 119 s.** Parser 0 × 2; validator 85 / 85; guard self-test OK (152 top-level, 33 in-function, 57 constructor cases); unit 178 × 2; report-stage 103 × 2; envguard 18 × 2; launcher 114 / 114; headless GUI 4 / 4; package built from the tracked files, extracted, validator 85 / 85 inside the extracted package. The one failure was case 16 of the campaign self-test (124 / 125): the driver exited 3 where the case expects 2, because the bundle it zips at the end could not be written — `campaign_summary.md` was "being used by another process" at that instant — and the bundle row counts as a failure. The step run again on its own: **125 / 125, 106 s**. The lock is the machine's, not the driver's (something on this desktop had the freshly written file open for a moment), and is recorded here as a measurement rather than hidden by the re-run. After three wording edits in the manual the package was built and validated again: `NetworkHealthCheck-1.2.4.zip`, 306 631 bytes, SHA256 `efad882a…`, validator 85 / 85 inside it. The release asset will be CI's build of the tagged commit, as for 1.2.3; the real-window steps are not needed for a change that leaves the scripts' behaviour untouched.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #20, round 1 · 2026-09-06, on `ed0aceb`: two findings, both accepted.** *P2 — "It sends nothing" was false as written:* the lead-in of the manual's first section said the tool sends nothing, one paragraph after describing the pings, the name lookup, the TCP connection and the HTTPS request it makes, and the shipped configuration reaches 1.1.1.1 and www.microsoft.com; a reader weighing outbound traffic would have read "no external connections". It says "It uploads nothing" now, points at the address list in section 9, and the header chip of the HTML page says the same — four files, both languages. *P3 — the package step's reference count in `tests/README.md` still said 62 / 62*, one row below the validator row that had been updated to 85 / 85; the same present-tense "62 checks" survived in that README's guard paragraph and in the 1.2.1 sentence of both technical guides' history, and all three now say 62 through 1.2.3 and 85 since 1.2.4. Both findings have the shape PR #17 and PR #19 taught — one claim living in several places, corrected in one — so each fix was followed by a search for its family across the repository ("sends nothing" / "傳送任何" in the four manual files; `62` outside this record). Validator 85 / 85 on the tree and inside the rebuilt package. Round 2 followed.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #20, round 2 · 2026-09-06, on `8d90b03`: two findings, both accepted — one of them a correction to round 1's fix.** *P2 — "nothing else" was a new absolute:* the round-1 sentence said the tests reach the addresses in section 9 and nothing else, but the name lookup goes through the operating system's resolver, the page request goes through the system proxy and follows redirects, and the traceroute's probes are answered by the routers on the way — a reader building an outbound allowlist would have had an incomplete list. Section 1 now says only that nothing is uploaded and that the tests send traffic to the test targets through this computer's own DNS servers, proxy and route; section 9's answer names the resolver, the proxy and the redirects. Four files, both languages. *P2 — the document version check read the first two lines and the `<title>` only*, so a bump that fixed the title and left the window name, the heading or the footer at the old version would have passed, and a manual repeats its version in all four places. The rule is now the one described under "The validator" above — every label in the single-version documents, nothing newer in the guides — and its first negative test caught a defect in the rule itself before it shipped: a label at the end of a sentence (`1.2.4.`) was invisible to a pattern that excluded any dot after the label, so the manual's footer was not being checked at all; the pattern excludes only a dot followed by a digit now. `tests/README.md` carries 91 / 91 in both reference rows, and neither the guides' history sentence nor the tests README quotes the count in prose any more, because round 1 showed a count quoted in prose goes stale the moment the validator grows.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #20, round 3 · 2026-09-06, on `7395ee7`: three findings, all accepted.** *P2 — the guides' own current-version statement was unchecked:* line 5 of each guide says "It applies to version **x**" / "文件對應版本 **x**", and a bump that changed the heading alone would have passed the first-line check while the newest-label check read the old value as history. The bold version label — the only one a guide carries — must now be the tool's version, in all six copies; proved on a copy whose zh-TW guide says **1.2.3** under a 1.2.4 heading. *P2 — "Nothing was read or changed" in the exit-code-3 row claimed too much:* `Write-EnvironmentReport` reads the computer name, the user, the PowerShell version, the culture and the operating-system caption into the file it writes — which the same row's action cell already said. The row now says no network setting was read and nothing was changed, and names what the file records. *P3 — the root launcher's own stop was missing:* `Start-English.cmd` double-clicked inside the ZIP window finds no `en-US` folder and stops with *ERROR: en-US package is missing* before any language launcher runs, so the quoted *program file is missing* message and `LauncherError.txt` never appear on the manual's primary path. The section-2 warning and the troubleshooting table carry both messages now, in both languages, and the field manual's §6 row names the root launcher's stop as well. Round 4 followed.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #20, round 4 · 2026-09-06, on `027a0f7`: two findings, both accepted.** *P2 — "the report stays on this computer" held only for the shipped folder:* `ReportFolderName` may be a rooted path and `Initialize-OutputDirectory` takes a rooted path as given, so a UNC path or a mapped drive puts every report on a share without anyone sending it. Section 1, the privacy note of section 5 and the FAQ answer now say the report goes nowhere *with the settings as shipped*, that IT may have pointed the folder elsewhere, and that the report's Computer and Run Information section shows where it went. *P2 — "the report's rows name what was actually contacted" was too strong:* the page request follows redirects (`AllowAutoRedirect`) and the report keeps the original and the final URL only, so the hosts in between are not recorded. The FAQ now says the rows name the configured targets and, for a page request, the address it finally landed on, and the privacy list says "the addresses the tests were aimed at". Four files, both languages. Round 5 followed.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #20, round 5 · 2026-09-06, on `8ebcf89`: two findings, both accepted.** *P2 — the version-label rule kept only the tool's major:* a manual that kept a 1.x label in its window name or footer after the tool moved to 2.x would have passed, because the filter dropped every 1.x label before the single-version check ran. A label is now three dotted numbers each below 1000, whatever the major — which still excludes a Windows build (10.0.26200) and a PowerShell version (5.1.26100), the two shapes the filter existed for — proved on a copy whose validator says 2.0.0 and whose manual updated its title alone (`1.2.4, 2.0.0`), and on a guide copy given a Windows build and a PowerShell version, which passes. *P2 — the privacy list was shorter than the report:* the report also carries the proxy settings (user proxy server, PAC URL, WinHTTP), the first-hop routers of the traceroute, the network profile names, the driver versions and the absolute paths of the configuration file and the report folder, which can include the user profile folder. Section 5's list names them now, in both languages and both formats; the field manual's personal-data paragraph and the READMEs' deployment bullet stop enumerating and point at that list, so it lives in one place. Round 6 followed.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #20, round 6 · 2026-09-06, on `64637ec`: two findings, both accepted.** *P2 — the packaged validation record was unchecked:* `VALIDATION.md` ships in the package and is outside the manifest by design, so a bump that left its release entry behind would have passed. The validator now requires a `## v<version>` entry for the tool's version and none for a newer one; the entry may sit anywhere in the file, because campaign and review entries sit above releases. Proved on a copy with the 1.2.4 heading renamed and on one given a v1.2.5 entry. *P2 — the privacy list still missed infrastructure identifiers:* the gateway-neighbour row carries the router's hardware address and the DNS row every address a name resolved to, and neither was named. The list in section 5 was rewritten as an inventory of the report — identity and versions, every adapter's fields, the Wi-Fi radio, the router's hardware address, the proxy, the addresses aimed at, resolved to, landed on and traced through, the paths, the error text — in both languages and both formats. Round 7 followed.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #20, round 7 · 2026-09-06, on `b934aee`: two findings, both accepted.** *P2 — "everything the check can see is fine" said more than Overall Healthy means:* an optional or added target that fails outright is an `INFO` row, and `Get-OverallStatus` counts no `INFO` row, so a run can be Overall Healthy with a failed optional target on record — the manual's own badge table says so. The verdict's meaning now reads "every required check that could run passed", says that optional targets do not count, and sends the reader to the Information rows. *P2 — the configured company standards were missing from the inventory:* when IT fills `Expected`, the comparison rows carry the allowed addresses and subnets, gateways, DNS servers and the expected DHCP mode — values that describe the site, not this machine. The inventory names them, and names the test targets by name as well as address. Four files, both languages. Round 8 followed at once (rounds 7 to 10 authorized together, stopping at the first clean pass).

**Independent review — Codex, PR #20, round 8 · 2026-09-06, on `8340a11`: two findings — one accepted for the manual and deferred for the tool, one accepted.** *P2 — the summary contradicts the verdict note round 7 added:* `Get-FingerprintSummary` takes its default branch from the verdict alone, so a run with a failed optional target prints *Everything passed / All checks passed during this run* above the Information row that records the failure. The manual quotes what the tool prints, and now says beside the table that the tool prints *Everything passed* whenever every required check passed and that the Information rows must be read before concluding that nothing failed. The summary's own wording is the tool's, in both scripts and their tests, and a documents release does not change it: **backlog #35**. *P2 — "up to a minute or so" was an unqualified upper bound:* the IT panel keeps a configured sample length above its 120-second default range, the chain's own GUI test samples for 125 s, and added targets and raised time limits stretch a run further, so a person could kill a customized run as hung. Section 1 now ties both figures to the shipped settings, says a changed configuration lengthens the run, and points at the progress line, which names the step and counts down the remaining sampling seconds. Four files, both languages. Round 9 followed.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #20, round 9 · 2026-09-06, on `150704b`: two findings, both accepted.** *P2 — "earlier reports are not overwritten" held for one copy of the tool at a time:* `Save-Reports` names the three files by `yyyyMMdd_HHmmss` and the computer name, with no lock, so two copies sharing a report folder and finishing within the same second would name their files alike; the manual says so where it promises the names. *P2 — `validation-matrix.html` is not this release's record:* its title, heading and footer name the 1.1.3 scenario matrix of 2026-07-30, and the files table had listed it with `VALIDATION.md` as "the validation record of this release". The table now gives each its own row — the record kept release by release, and an earlier release's fault-scenario matrix whose heading names the release (the manual cannot write 1.1.3 itself: every version label in it must be the tool's, by the validator's own rule). Four files, both languages. Round 10 followed, the last of the authorized block. (This paragraph was written after round 10: the round-9 commit `a2f302d` carried the fix but not the record, because the step that appends the record failed on its anchor and the commit went ahead without it — the chain's steps were not joined.)

**Independent review — Codex, PR #20, round 10 · 2026-09-06, on `a2f302d`: two findings, both accepted.** *P2 — a guide's package-table row read as a current-version statement:* "The end-user manual (1.2.4)" was written as a history marker, like "IT entry (1.2)" beside it, but read as the manual's version — and after a bump the newest-label check would treat it as history. The row now says "since 1.2.4", the form every other history marker in the guides takes; a guide's current-version statements are its first line and the bold label of its purpose paragraph, both checked, and every other label is history written as "since x" or "Version x (date)". Six copies. *P2 — "every network adapter" claimed more than the report holds:* both discovery paths keep connected adapters only (the NetTCPIP path those with an address, the CIM fallback those with `IPEnabled`), and the report's own row says "connected network adapter(s)"; the inventory says "every connected network adapter" now. Four files, both languages. **The block of rounds 7 to 10 ends here without a clean pass**: ten rounds, 23 findings, 22 accepted and 1 accepted-and-deferred (backlog #35), none rejected. Every finding was a sentence one notch more absolute than the code or the configuration allows, or a count or a label repeated in a place no round had looked at yet; the last rounds still found one or two each, in the privacy inventory and in claims qualified by the shipped configuration. A further round is the owner's call.

**The inventory walked against a real report, before rounds 11 to 13 · 2026-09-06.** The owner asked for the privacy inventory of section 5 to be walked row by row against a real report before the next block, because rounds 5 to 8 had each extended it. Walked: the acceptance step's en-US IT run against unreachable targets (29 rows, extra targets typed in), its JSON (`RunOptions.RawTargets`, `System`, `Results[].Diagnostics`), `Get-ExceptionDiagnostics` and `Write-EnvironmentReport` in the script, and a `LauncherError.txt` the launcher step wrote. The inventory is regrouped — identity and the run, every connected adapter, Wi-Fi, the network around you, the tests, errors — and gained what the walk found missing: the time of the run, the entry point and the targets typed into the IT panel, link speed and traffic counters per adapter, the Wi-Fi rates, the routes in use, the timing of the probes that answered, and the JSON-only error details, which name the program's own folder. Section 4 now says the JSON holds those details, and section 6 says what the environment report (the program's folder, the language mode and settings among them) and `LauncherError.txt` (time, computer, user, folder, program path, reason) record. Four files, both languages. Rounds 11 to 13 follow, stopping at the first clean pass.

**Independent review — Codex, PR #20, round 11 · 2026-09-06, on `cd65828`: three findings, all accepted.** *P2 — the launcher's fallback file is shorter than the one beside the launcher:* when the launcher's folder cannot be written, `%TEMP%\NetworkHealthCheck_LauncherError.txt` records the time, the computer, the user, the error and the suggested action only — no folder, no program path, no PowerShell path — and the manual had promised all of them; it now says which fields belong to the file beside the launcher. *P2 — "the timing of every probe" — from the walk itself — was too much:* a TCP or HTTP attempt that fails discards the elapsed time it measured, a DNS exception records none, and a lost ping attempt records its status alone, so the inventory says "the timing of the probes that answered". *P2 — the record's entry was checked by its heading alone:* an entry copied from the previous release and left with its Version paragraph would have passed once its heading said the new version. The validator now finds the entry under `## v<version>` and requires its **Version.** paragraph — the record's house style — to name the version; proved on a copy whose 1.2.4 entry says 1.2.3 there. 99 → 100 checks. **Independent review — Codex, PR #20, round 12 · 2026-09-06, on `f29019f`: one finding, accepted.** (The first trigger of this round came back "Something went wrong" from the reviewer with the commit demonstrably on the remote and named as the pull request's head; a second trigger ran — a reviewer failure is not a round.) *P2 — "an optional or added target that did not answer sits in an Information row" was true of three of the four probes:* `Set-RunOptions` adds an extra DNS name with `Required = $false`, and `Test-DnsNames` records an optional name that does not resolve as `WARN`, so such a run is Attention Required with *Warnings to review*, not Overall Healthy — the field manual's §3 table had it right. The verdict's meaning, the note beside the "What to tell IT" table and the badge table now say ping, TCP or HTTP where they said target, and name the DNS exception. Four files, both languages. Round 13 followed — the last of the authorized block.

The verdict's meaning now says optional targets do not count on their own and names both exceptions, the group and the DNS name. Four files, both languages. **The block of rounds 11 to 13 ends here without a clean pass: thirteen rounds, 28 findings, 27 accepted and 1 accepted-and-deferred (backlog #35), none rejected.** The later rounds narrowed to one finding each, all on the same sentence family — what an optional target does to the verdict — which the field manual states in a table (§3) and this manual had compressed into a clause; the clause now carries the table's three cases.
